Written Questions & Answers · QP-2308

Engineering Management — August 2023

EXAMINATION OF MARINE ENGINEER OFFICER · Function: Marine Engineering Management at Management Level · M.E.O. Class I

Sr. No. QP-2308 3 hours Total marks 100 9 questions · answer six (India 2023)

Questions

Examination instructions, as printed
  • Answer SIX questions only.
  • All questions carry equal marks.
  • Neatness in handwriting and clarity in expression carries weightage
  • Blank pages if any, to be struck by (X) at the end of each question.

Instruction 2 on the paper states that all questions carry equal marks, and six answered questions are required against a printed 'Total Marks - 100'. Every question is therefore recorded at 16 marks, so six answered questions total 96 against the printed 100. The discrepancy is printed on the source copy and is reproduced, not corrected. NOTE FOR THIS PAPER: like January and February 2023, and unlike September 2023, the August 2023 copy prints NO mark allocation against any question or any limb. Every question therefore carries printed_marks_absent and null sub-part marks.

Understand this first

Machinery has always been able to tell you how it is doing - you just had to be standing next to it with a thermometer, a pressure gauge and a hand on the bearing. What has changed is that the equipment can now say it for itself, continuously, to somebody who is not in the engine room and may not be on the ship. That is the whole idea, and everything else follows from it. Once a machine reports on itself all the time, you stop maintaining it on a calendar and start maintaining it on evidence, because you can see it going wrong instead of guessing when it might. Once the office can see the same numbers, decisions that used to be yours alone become shared, which is a real change in who is responsible for what. And once a fuel figure is measured continuously rather than added up at the end of the year, an emissions rule that is calculated over a whole year becomes something you can actually manage rather than something you discover. But notice the two things that are easy to miss. None of this saves anything by itself - the measurement only tells you; the saving happens at the moment a human being changes a decision, cleans a hull or opens a bearing. And every wire you add so the machine can talk outwards is also a way in. Control systems that were safe because nothing could reach them are no longer unreachable, and on a ship the thing at risk is not a file - it is the main engine.

Model written answer

1. What the Internet of Things is

The Internet of Things is the extension of network connectivity to physical objects, so that equipment which formerly only ran can now report on itself and, in some arrangements, be acted upon remotely. The definition that matters for this paper is functional: an IoT system is one in which a physical asset is instrumented, its data is transmitted off the asset, the data is analysed, and the analysis causes an action - a maintenance decision, a load change, a route change, a purchase.

The word to underline is action. A sensor that logs to a local printer is instrumentation, not IoT; a system that measures without changing any decision is a cost with no benefit. At sea, the things are main and auxiliary engines, shafting, boilers, pumps, purifiers, tanks, reefer containers, ballast and cargo systems, the hull itself through its measured performance, and increasingly the crew's own safety equipment.

2. The principles - the five layers

Every maritime IoT installation, whatever its badge, is the same five layers. Naming them is the cheapest structure in this answer because limbs (a), (b) and (c) can each be hung on them.

  • Sensing. Transducers for pressure, temperature, vibration, torque, speed, flow, level, position, oil condition and exhaust composition, plus the digital outputs the machinery's own control system already produces. On most ships the majority of the data already exists inside the alarm and monitoring system; the IoT question is whether anything is done with it.
  • Connectivity. A shipboard network gathering the sensors - fieldbus, industrial Ethernet, and increasingly wireless for retrofit points where cabling is uneconomic - and a ship-to-shore link, in practice satellite, carrying a filtered subset ashore.
  • Edge processing. Computation done on board, because the satellite link is the scarce resource. The edge device filters, compresses, time-stamps and often does the first level of anomaly detection, so that what is transmitted is a summary and an exception rather than a raw stream.
  • Analytics and storage. A shore platform holding the fleet's history, where a single ship's behaviour can be compared with her own past and with her sisters. This is where value is actually created, because a reading means little and a trend against a fleet baseline means a great deal.
  • Application and action. The dashboards, alerts, work orders, charter reports and regulatory returns through which the analysis reaches a human being who can do something.

Two principles cut across all five and are worth a mark each. Interoperability: the value collapses if each maker's equipment speaks only its own protocol, which is why standardised data models matter more than any individual sensor. And security by design: every connection added for monitoring is also a path inward, which is the subject of section 8.

3. Advantages of loT in the maritime industry

The stem asks for advantages and disadvantages together, so give them as two balanced sets and do not let the first crowd out the second.

  • Machinery availability rises. Incipient defects are detected as trends long before they reach an alarm setpoint, so a failure that would have been a breakdown becomes a planned intervention in port with the spare already on board.
  • Maintenance cost falls where it should. Overhauls driven by running hours are replaced by overhauls driven by condition, which removes both the unnecessary opening-up of healthy machinery and the late opening-up of unhealthy machinery.
  • Fuel and emissions. Continuous measurement of hull and propeller performance, engine load and trim converts fuel saving from an annual estimate into a daily one, and it is the only practical way to manage an operational index that is calculated over a whole year.
  • Regulatory reporting becomes a by-product. Fuel oil consumption data required under the Data Collection System, and the figures behind the operational carbon intensity rating, come out of the same measurement chain rather than being reconstructed from noon reports at year end.
  • Shore expertise reaches the ship. The maker's specialist, the superintendent and the class surveyor can see the same data as the Chief Engineer, which shortens diagnosis and reduces the number of attendances that have to be physical.
  • Safety. Fewer unplanned entries into machinery spaces to investigate a symptom, fewer emergency repairs at sea, and better evidence after an incident.
  • Commercial and chartering value. Verified performance data settles speed and consumption disputes under a charter party on evidence rather than on assertion, and supports the emissions reporting that lenders and charterers increasingly require.
  • Fleet learning. A defect found on one ship can be checked for on every sister overnight - something no amount of experience on a single ship can achieve.

4. Disadvantages, and the ones that are specific to a ship

Divide these deliberately. The first group a ship shares with any industrial plant; the second group is maritime-specific, and it is where the marks are.

  • Capital and integration cost, and a retrofit is worse than a newbuilding because sensors, cabling and power have to be found in a completed machinery space.
  • Data quality. A drifting or wrongly sited sensor produces confident nonsense, and the analysis cannot tell the difference. Sensor calibration becomes a maintenance task in its own right - the instrument now needs maintaining as much as the machine.
  • Alarm and information overload. More data is not more understanding; a system that raises alerts nobody has time to read trains the watchkeeper to ignore it.
  • Skill and competence gap. The engineer is now expected to interpret trends and statistics, and the training has not generally kept pace with the instrumentation.
  • Dependence and de-skilling. An engineer who has only ever been told what is wrong may not diagnose it when the system is down.

Now the ship-specific set:

  • Connectivity is limited, intermittent and expensive. Satellite bandwidth at sea is not shore bandwidth; the architecture has to assume the link will be down, which is why edge processing is not optional.
  • Cyber security across an OT/IT boundary that used to be an air gap. Machinery control systems were designed when they were unreachable. Connecting them exposes systems with long lives, infrequent patching and safety consequences - the risk is not lost data, it is a lost main engine.
  • Data ownership and confidentiality. The data describes the owner's ship, the charterer's voyage, the maker's equipment and the crew's actions. Who owns it, who may see it and what it may be used for are contractual questions that are frequently unresolved, and performance data is discoverable in a dispute.
  • Fragmentation between makers and no single accepted standard, so a mixed-maker engine room can need several parallel systems.
  • Support at sea. A shore plant with a failed gateway calls the vendor; a ship in mid-ocean cannot, and the ship's staff become the first-line support for equipment they did not specify.
  • Crew privacy and monitoring. Systems that log actions as well as conditions change the relationship between ship and office, and are resented where the purpose is not explained.

5. How loT is changing the maritime industry

The stem asks how the industry is changing, so answer with changes and not with a list of gadgets. There are four, and they run outward from the engine room.

From calendar to condition. The oldest change and still the largest. Maintenance, hull cleaning, propeller polishing and even survey are moving from fixed intervals to measured need. It reaches beyond maintenance: a society that accepts continuous monitoring data can allow a machinery planned maintenance scheme in place of the opened-up examination it would otherwise require.

From the ship as an island to the ship as a node. Decisions that were the Master's and the Chief Engineer's alone are now taken jointly with a shore operations centre that can see the same data. This is a real and not always comfortable shift of authority, and the question of where responsibility now sits is one the industry has not fully answered.

From assertion to evidence in commerce. Charter party performance claims, warranty disputes with makers, hull-fouling claims and emissions reporting are all settled increasingly on continuous measured data. New commercial products follow - performance-based charters, and financing whose terms depend on measured emissions.

From periodic compliance to continuous compliance. An annual survey samples a ship's condition on one day. An operational index measured over a whole year, and reported from the same measurement chain, is a different regulatory model - and it is the model the current instruments are moving towards. The examiner will credit the observation that the regulation is what is pulling the technology aboard, not the other way round.

6. loT in energy efficiency optimization

This limb has to be answered against the position at this sitting, and the position is unusually fresh. The 2023 IMO Strategy on Reduction of GHG Emissions from Ships was adopted at MEPC 80 on 7 July 2023 - one month before this paper - and it sets the direction. What is already applicable is more important for an operational answer: EEXI and the operational carbon intensity indicator have applied since 1 January 2023, under MARPOL Annex VI as revised by resolution MEPC.328(76), which entered into force on 1 November 2022. The fuel oil Data Collection System has been reporting since 2019, and SEEMP Part III - the ship's own CII implementation plan - must be on board and kept under review.

That is the pull. EEXI is a design index and is settled once; the CII is an operational rating recalculated every year from how the ship is actually run. It is the operational index that IoT data serves, and the connection is direct: a rating computed over twelve months cannot be managed by looking at it in month twelve.

The measurable levers, and what is sensed for each:

  • Hull and propeller condition. Shaft torque and speed, speed through water, draught and trim, and weather, reduced continuously to a performance index. A rising power demand at constant speed is fouling, and the system converts a calendar-based cleaning interval into a condition-based one - the single largest controllable operational loss.
  • Engine condition and load. Cylinder pressure, exhaust temperatures, turbocharger condition and specific fuel consumption against load, so that deterioration is separated from operating point.
  • Trim and draught optimisation. The measured resistance at different trims for the actual loaded condition, rather than the model-basin curve.
  • Voyage and weather routing. Speed and route chosen against forecast, with just-in-time arrival where the charter permits it - avoiding the classic waste of steaming hard to wait at anchor.
  • Auxiliary and hotel load. Continuous electrical load monitoring, which usually finds unnecessary running machinery within days of installation.
  • Boiler and steam plant, and cargo systems on tankers and gas carriers, where heating policy is frequently more conservative than the cargo requires.

The honest qualification, and it earns a mark: the measurement itself saves nothing. It identifies and quantifies, and the saving is realised only when somebody changes an operating decision, cleans a hull or accepts a slower passage. An IoT installation that is not connected to authority to act is an expensive record of waste.

7. loT in predictive Maintenance

Place predictive maintenance among its neighbours first, because the distinction is examinable and is often blurred:

  • Reactive - run to failure. Cheap to plan, expensive when it happens, and unacceptable for anything with a safety or statutory consequence.
  • Preventive - overhaul at a fixed interval of running hours or calendar time. Safe, simple, and systematically wasteful, because it opens up healthy machinery and can still miss a machine that is failing early.
  • Predictive - intervene when measured condition and its trend say the machine is deteriorating, at a time chosen for operational convenience. This is what IoT enables.
  • Prescriptive - the further step, in which the system also recommends the action and its timing. Emerging rather than established at this sitting, and it should be described as such.

The mechanism is a chain of four steps, and it is worth setting out because it shows the examiner that the answer is not a slogan:

  1. Sense the parameters that carry a failure signature - vibration spectra for bearings, gears and imbalance; bearing and exhaust temperatures; lubricating oil condition by elemental analysis, infrared spectroscopy, particle count and base number; cylinder pressure diagrams; motor current signature; acoustic emission; and pressure or flow differentials across filters, coolers and pumps.
  2. Establish a baseline for the individual machine in its own healthy condition and at a defined operating point. Without a baseline there is no such thing as an abnormal reading, which is why the first weeks of any installation produce nothing useful.
  3. Detect the deviation and its rate. The value of the data is almost entirely in the trend: a single lubricating oil result is a data point, and the same result three times with a rising slope is a diagnosis. Compare against the machine's own history, against sister ships, and against the maker's model.
  4. Decide and act. Estimate remaining useful life, generate the work order, order the part, and choose the port. Then feed the outcome back - what was actually found on opening up - because a predictive system that is never corrected by findings degrades into superstition.

Two consequences deserve stating. First, the statutory and class dimension: condition data accepted by the society can support a planned maintenance scheme in place of an opened-up survey, so predictive maintenance is not only a cost measure but a route to a lighter survey burden. Second, the spares and logistics dimension: knowing three weeks in advance which part will be needed converts an emergency airfreight into a routine delivery, and this is often the largest realised saving of all.

8. What the Chief Engineer has to do about it

Close on the management content, because this is an Engineering Management paper and the examiner wants to see accountability rather than enthusiasm.

  • Own the data quality. Sensor calibration, correct siting and prompt repair of failed transducers become planned maintenance items. A neglected sensor is worse than no sensor, because it is believed.
  • Keep the manual competence alive. Indicator cards, sounding rods, feeling a bearing and reading a pressure gauge are the fallback when the system is down, and they are also the check on it.
  • Treat cyber risk as a management-system obligation, because it is one. Cyber risk has had to be addressed in the safety management system since the first annual verification of the company's Document of Compliance after 1 January 2021, under resolution MSC.428(98), and the guidance is MSC-FAL.1/Circ.3 with its identify, protect, detect, respond and recover elements. In practice: segregate the machinery control network from the business network, control removable media and maker remote access, and know how to run the plant with the link disconnected.
  • Fix responsibility. Agree in writing what the shore centre may see, what it may advise and what it may not instruct, so that the division of responsibility survives an incident.
  • Insist that alerts are actionable. Every alert should have an owner and a defined response, and alerts that are routinely ignored should be removed rather than tolerated.
  • Verify claimed savings against fuel figures the ship itself measures, and record what was found on opening up, so that the system is audited by reality.

End on the proposition that holds the whole question together: IoT does not maintain machinery, save fuel or comply with anything. It converts what the ship already knows into something a person can act on in time - and the value appears only at the moment someone acts.

Study guide

Why this structure scores

The stem prints one introduction and three limbs, and the commonest failure is to write a general essay about digitalisation that never separates them. The structure above gives sections 1 and 2 to the introduction the stem expressly asks for, sections 3 and 4 to limb (a), section 5 to limb (b), sections 6 and 7 to limb (c), and section 8 to the management content that lifts the answer out of a technology description. A marker looking for the three limbs can find each of them.

Section 2 is doing more work than it appears to. The five layers are set up once and then reused: the advantages in section 3 are mostly analytics-layer benefits, the ship-specific disadvantages in section 4 are mostly connectivity-layer and security problems, and the predictive chain in section 7 walks the same five layers in order. A candidate who has the layers has a structure for anything the examiner asks about this subject.

Splitting the disadvantages in section 4 into general and ship-specific is deliberate. Every candidate can write cost, training and data overload. The marks separate on bandwidth at sea, the OT/IT boundary that used to be an air gap, data ownership and first-line support in mid-ocean, and putting them under their own sub-heading makes the examiner see them.

Common mistakes

  • Writing a technology catalogue for limb (b). The stem asks how the industry is CHANGING; a list of sensors and platforms does not answer it. Give changes - calendar to condition, island to node, assertion to evidence, periodic to continuous compliance.
  • Treating IoT and predictive maintenance as the same thing. Predictive maintenance is one application of IoT, and limb (c) also asks about energy efficiency.
  • Confusing preventive with predictive. Preventive is a fixed interval; predictive is measured condition and trend. This is the single most examinable distinction in limb (c).
  • Claiming a saving figure. Vendor percentages are not evidence and an examiner may well ask where the number came from.
  • Forgetting that the measurement saves nothing by itself. The saving comes from the decision the measurement causes, and saying so shows judgement.
  • Ignoring cyber security, or treating it as an IT matter. It is an ISM safety-management obligation and the consequence is a lost engine, not a lost file.
  • Importing later law. See the traps below.
  • Answering as an enthusiast. The paper is Engineering Management and the examiner wants the limitations and the accountability as much as the benefits.

Examiner traps

  • EU ETS did not apply to shipping at this sitting. It applies from 1 January 2024, four months later. Material written for a 2024 sitting of this identical question uses it as its regulatory anchor and it is wrong here.
  • FuelEU Maritime did not exist at this sitting. Regulation (EU) 2023/1805 was adopted 13 September 2023 - one month after this paper.
  • The strategy in force is the 2023 Strategy, not the Initial Strategy of 2018. It was adopted on 7 July 2023, one month before this sitting, and it is the freshest thing on the paper. An answer that cites the 2018 Initial Strategy as current is a month out of date.
  • EEXI and CII are already applicable, not forthcoming. Both have applied since 1 January 2023, so an answer written in the future tense is wrong. The revised Annex VI that carries them entered into force on 1 November 2022 - not 2023.
  • EEXI is a design index; CII is operational. Expect to be asked which one IoT data actually serves. It is the CII.
  • Cyber risk in the SMS is not new and not optional. The deadline was the first annual DOC verification after 1 January 2021 and it has passed.
  • The IACS cyber resilience requirements were future at this sitting, applying to ships contracted for construction on or after 1 January 2024. Do not describe them as applying to your ship.

Likely oral follow-up

  • Give me the five layers of an IoT system and tell me which one is the bottleneck on a ship.
  • Why is edge processing not optional at sea?
  • Your vibration system says a bearing is deteriorating and it sounds normal to you. What do you do?
  • What is the difference between preventive and predictive maintenance? Which is your main engine on?
  • How would you satisfy yourself that a claimed fuel saving is real?
  • Your maker wants permanent remote access to the engine control system. What conditions do you impose?
  • Who owns the performance data your ship generates - you, the owner, the charterer or the maker?
  • What happens to your monitoring regime when the satellite link fails for a week?
  • Can condition monitoring data replace an opened-up survey? On what basis?

Memory framework

The route is WHAT - LAYERS - ADVANTAGES - DISADVANTAGES - CHANGES - EFFICIENCY - PREDICTIVE - GOVERNANCE, which maps one-for-one onto introduction, limb (a), limb (b), limb (c) and the close. Inside the layers hold SENSE - CONNECT - EDGE - ANALYSE - ACT. Inside predictive maintenance hold SENSE - BASELINE - DEVIATE - DECIDE, and remember that the fifth step is feeding back what you actually found. Inside the changes hold four FROM/TO pairs. The closing sentence is worth memorising: the value appears only at the moment someone acts.

Regulation and source map

  • MARPOL Annex VI as revised by MEPC.328(76) - in force 1 November 2022. Carries EEXI and the operational CII.
  • EEXI - applicable from the first annual, intermediate or renewal IAPP survey on or after 1 January 2023.
  • Operational CII and rating - applicable from 1 January 2023; the first rating is calculated on the 2023 calendar year.
  • Fuel oil Data Collection System - reporting since 2019.
  • SEEMP Part III - the CII implementation plan, under the 2022 SEEMP Guidelines, MEPC.346(78).
  • 2023 IMO GHG Strategy, MEPC.377(80) - adopted 7 July 2023 at MEPC 80. Operative as strategy at this sitting.
  • MSC.428(98) - cyber risk in the SMS by the first annual DOC verification after 1 January 2021.
  • MSC-FAL.1/Circ.3/Rev.2 (June 2022) - Guidelines on Maritime Cyber Risk Management; identify, protect, detect, respond, recover.
  • IACS cyber resilience unified requirements - adopted April 2022, ships contracted for construction on or after 1 January 2024. FUTURE at this sitting.
  • ISM Code element 10 - maintenance of the ship and equipment, the provision under which a planned maintenance scheme sits.

Uncertainty and evidence limits

  • No market size, adoption percentage or saving figure is quoted. None is held at a source MIW can stand behind, and an invented number is exactly the error this answer avoids.
  • The IACS cyber resilience application date is cited but not verified against the requirements themselves, which MIW does not hold. Industry application dates of this kind have moved before, so treat the date as indicative and the principle as the examinable point.
  • we hold MARPOL Annex VI at citation level only - it resolves to identity and provenance, never to text - so no regulation is quoted, only cited by number.
  • The revised Annex VI entered into force on 1 November 2022, and that is what is used here. The resolution's own operative paragraph 3 determines it: the amendments were deemed accepted on 1 May 2022 and enter into force on 1 November 2022. The date matters on this paper specifically, because putting it a year later would place an August 2023 sitting before entry into force and reverse the whole efficiency limb.
  • Class notation practice for continuous survey and planned maintenance schemes is described by function; no class rule number is cited, none being held.
Recall the structure

Before you read the answer: can you name all 8 sections, in order? Say them or write them down, then reveal.

  1. __________What the Internet of Things is
  2. __________The principles - the five layers
  3. __________Advantages of loT in the maritime industry
  4. __________Disadvantages, and the ones that are specific to a ship
  5. __________How loT is changing the maritime industry
  6. __________loT in energy efficiency optimization

Flashcards 9

1 what IoT is; 2 the five layers; 3 advantages; 4 disadvantages, general then ship-specific; 5 how the industry is changing; 6 energy efficiency; 7 predictive maintenance; 8 what the Chief Engineer must do. Sections 1-2 are the introduction the stem asks for, 3-4 are limb (a), 5 is limb (b), 6-7 are limb (c).

A system in which a physical asset is instrumented, its data is transmitted off the asset, the data is analysed, and the analysis causes an action. The layers are sensing, connectivity, edge processing, analytics and storage, and application and action. Measurement that changes no decision is not IoT, it is cost.

Preventive overhauls at a fixed interval of running hours or calendar time; predictive intervenes when measured condition and its trend show deterioration, at a time chosen for convenience. The neighbours are reactive - run to failure - and prescriptive, where the system also recommends the action and its timing. Prescriptive is emerging rather than established.

Sense the parameters carrying a failure signature; establish a baseline for that individual machine at a defined operating point; detect the deviation and its rate, because the value is in the trend and not the reading; then estimate remaining life, raise the work order, order the part and choose the port. Finally feed back what was actually found on opening up, or the system degrades into superstition.

Bandwidth at sea is limited, intermittent and expensive, which is why edge processing is not optional. Cyber risk crosses an OT/IT boundary that used to be an air gap, and the loss is a main engine rather than a file. Data ownership is contested between owner, charterer, maker and crew and is discoverable in a dispute. Makers are fragmented, and the ship's staff are first-line support in mid-ocean.

The 2023 IMO GHG Strategy, MEPC.377(80), adopted 7 July 2023 - one month earlier. EEXI and the operational CII applicable from 1 January 2023 under MARPOL Annex VI as revised by MEPC.328(76), in force 1 November 2022. The fuel oil Data Collection System reporting since 2019, and SEEMP Part III kept on board.

Because it applies to shipping only from 1 January 2024, four months after this paper. FuelEU Maritime is worse still - Regulation (EU) 2023/1805 was adopted 13 September 2023, one month after. The April 2024 sitting of this identical stem rests on both, so circulating material for it is wrong here. Use the 2023 IMO GHG Strategy and the applicable CII instead.

No. It has been a safety-management-system obligation since the first annual verification of the company's Document of Compliance after 1 January 2021, under resolution MSC.428(98), with MSC-FAL.1/Circ.3 as guidance. The exposed systems are operational technology with long lives and infrequent patching, so the consequence is a lost main engine, not a lost file.

No. It identifies and quantifies the loss. The saving is realised only when somebody changes an operating decision, cleans the hull, adjusts the trim or accepts a slower passage. An installation not connected to authority to act is an expensive record of waste - and saying so is what separates a management answer from a technology answer.

Quick revision
15-second recall
IoT = sense, connect, process at the edge, analyse ashore, ACT. Advantages are availability, cost, fuel, reporting, shore expertise, evidence. Disadvantages split general (cost, sensor drift, alert overload, skills) from ship-specific (bandwidth, OT/IT cyber, data ownership, no support at sea). Four changes: calendar to condition, island to node, assertion to evidence, periodic to continuous compliance. Efficiency serves the CII, applicable since 1 January 2023; the 2023 GHG Strategy is one month old. Predictive = baseline, deviation, RATE, act, feed back.

Critical regulation
MARPOL Annex VI as revised by MEPC.328(76), in force 1 November 2022, carrying EEXI and the operational CII from 1 January 2023. If you remember one thing, remember that the CII is an operational index computed over a whole year - which is precisely why continuous measurement, rather than an annual estimate, is what it takes to manage it.
Major trap
Citing EU ETS or FuelEU Maritime as the regulatory driver. EU ETS applies to shipping from 1 January 2024 and FuelEU was adopted 13 September 2023 - four months and one month AFTER this sitting. The April 2024 sitting of this identical question rests on both, so any circulating answer to it is wrong for this paper.

Also on the platform: QP2308 Q4 - the same data, seen from the ship: what a Chief Engineer actually watches on a UMS vessel and how he charts it · QP2308 Q6 - the power chain whose measured efficiency is what an energy-efficiency IoT system is monitoring · QP2308 Q8 - the survey regime that condition data can be accepted in support of

First in set First set here. The same task returns at April 2024 Q1.

Understand this first

Suing somebody is only worth doing if they have something you can take. The trouble with ships is that the company which owns one is very often a company that owns nothing else - formed for that ship, registered somewhere convenient, and quite capable of ceasing to exist the week after the accident. So the law does something unusual: it lets certain claimants proceed against the ship itself, as though the ship were the person who owed the money. The ship can be seized wherever she goes, and if nobody puts up money to get her back, she is sold and the claimants are paid out of the price. The claimants who get this right are not chosen at random. They are the ones who had no realistic way of protecting themselves - a crew left unpaid in a foreign port, someone injured, a salvor who saved her, a port whose services were used up. And because the right is meant to be effective, it has two hard edges. It is invisible: nothing in any register shows it, so a buyer cannot find it by looking. And it stays with the ship, so selling her, renaming her or re-registering her under another flag does not shake it off - the new owner inherits it whether or not he had any way of knowing. Only one thing wipes the slate clean, and that is a sale ordered by a court: the buyer then gets her free of everything, and the arguments move from the ship to the pile of money she fetched. Everything else in this subject is the law keeping so drastic a right within limits - a short list of who qualifies, a queue fixing who is paid first, and a clock that runs out.

Knowledge map
Maritime Lien, In Rem and In Personam
  1. 1What a maritime lien is
    • A privileged claim against the vessel herself, arising by operation of law
    • Feature one - it attaches to the res, not to a person; the ship is the debtor
    • Feature two - it arises the moment the claim arises, unregistered and undiscoverable: a SECRET lien
  2. 2How the lien travels with the property
    • It continues notwithstanding change of ownership, registration or flag
    • It binds a purchaser in good faith without notice, because it is secret
    • Extinguished after ONE year, but TWO years for crew claims
  3. 3In rem - the action against the ship
    • The vessel is named as defendant, served physically, and may be arrested
    • Every maritime lien gives a right in rem, but not every right in rem is a maritime lien
    • The statutory right in rem covers many more claims but does not survive a change of ownership and does not outrank a mortgage
  4. 4In personam - the action against the person
    • The ordinary action against owner, demise charterer, manager or operator
    • Worthless against a single-ship company with no other asset

  5. 5Which convention deals with maritime lien
    • The International Convention on Maritime Liens and Mortgages, 1993
    • Adopted at Geneva 6 May 1993; in force 5 September 2004
    • Successor to the Brussels Conventions of 1926 and 1967, neither widely accepted
  6. 6The five secured claims, and how they rank among themselves
    • (a) crew wages, repatriation costs and social insurance contributions

    • (c) salvage reward including special compensation

    • First, the costs and expenses of arrest, seizure and sale, and the upkeep of vessel and crew

    • The Admiralty (Jurisdiction and Settlement of Maritime Claims) Act, 2017, in force 1 April 2018

Memorise the 8 headings. The core points are there to help you check whether an answer is too thin — they are not a mark scheme, and they are not 48 separate facts to learn individually.

Show the core points to cover
1. What a maritime lien is
  • A privileged claim against the vessel herself, arising by operation of law
  • Feature one - it attaches to the res, not to a person; the ship is the debtor
  • Feature two - it arises the moment the claim arises, unregistered and undiscoverable: a SECRET lien
  • Feature three - it is inchoate until enforced, then crystallised by arrest and forced sale
  • Feature four - it ranks ahead of registered mortgages
  • The justification: the claimants protected are those who could not realistically pursue the owner
2. How the lien travels with the property
  • It continues notwithstanding change of ownership, registration or flag
  • It binds a purchaser in good faith without notice, because it is secret
  • Extinguished after ONE year, but TWO years for crew claims
  • Time runs CONTINUOUSLY without suspension, excluding any period under arrest or seizure
  • From discharge for wages; from accrual of the claim for the rest
  • Defeated only by a forced sale, which passes clean title and moves the liens to the proceeds
3. In rem - the action against the ship
  • The vessel is named as defendant, served physically, and may be arrested
  • Every maritime lien gives a right in rem, but not every right in rem is a maritime lien
  • The statutory right in rem covers many more claims but does not survive a change of ownership and does not outrank a mortgage
  • Arrest exists to compel SECURITY - a club letter, bank guarantee or payment into court
  • If no security is given, forced sale produces the fund
4. In personam - the action against the person
  • The ordinary action against owner, demise charterer, manager or operator
  • Worthless against a single-ship company with no other asset
  • The action in rem reaches a physical asset that must eventually enter a port
  • The two are commonly pursued together - in rem to secure, in personam to establish liability
  • In India both are exercised by the High Courts, and the statute keeps them in separate provisions
5. Which convention deals with maritime lien
  • The International Convention on Maritime Liens and Mortgages, 1993
  • Adopted at Geneva 6 May 1993; in force 5 September 2004
  • Successor to the Brussels Conventions of 1926 and 1967, neither widely accepted
  • Its solution is a short closed list of five, with national liens ranking after the five AND after mortgages
  • INDIA IS NOT A PARTY - the Admiralty Act, 2017, in force 1 April 2018, gives effect to the scheme
6. The five secured claims, and how they rank among themselves
  • (a) crew wages, repatriation costs and social insurance contributions
  • (b) loss of life or personal injury in direct connection with the operation of the vessel
  • (c) salvage reward including special compensation
  • (d) port, canal and other waterway dues and pilotage dues
  • (e) tort for loss or damage by the operation of the vessel, EXCLUDING cargo and containers
  • Liens outrank registered mortgages; salvage jumps liens attached before it and salvages rank in inverse order of time; within a category, equal and rateable
  • Excluded altogether: oil and HNS damage compensable elsewhere, and nuclear
7. The order of settlement after legal action
  • First, the costs and expenses of arrest, seizure and sale, and the upkeep of vessel and crew
  • Then wreck removal costs where national law gives them that priority - optional, not automatic
  • Then the five maritime liens in their order, with the salvage rules applied
  • Then a shipbuilder's or shiprepairer's right of retention where national law recognises one
  • Then registered mortgages, hypotheques and charges
  • Then all other claims, including national liens and ordinary maritime claims
  • Residue to the owner; the purchaser takes free of everything
8. The Indian position, stated separately
  • The Admiralty (Jurisdiction and Settlement of Maritime Claims) Act, 2017, in force 1 April 2018
  • Section 9(1) lists the same five liens in the same order - wages first
  • Section 9(2) - travels with the vessel; one year, two years for crew claims
  • Section 9(3) - commences on discharge or on accrual, and runs continuously excluding time under arrest
  • Section 10 - liens, then registered mortgages, then all other claims; equal within a category; salvages in inverse order of time
  • Section 8 - a court sale vests the vessel in the purchaser free of everything
  • Arrest in rem and jurisdiction in personam are separate provisions, both exercised by the High Courts
Model written answer

1. What a maritime lien is

A maritime lien is a privileged claim against the vessel herself, arising by operation of law, which travels with the ship into whosoever's hands she passes and which ranks ahead of a registered mortgage. It is not a lien in the ordinary commercial sense at all: there is no possession, no agreement and no registration.

Four features define it, and every later part of this answer is a consequence of one of them.

  1. It attaches to the res, not to a person. The ship is the debtor. The claimant does not have to establish that the owner is solvent, present, or even identifiable.
  2. It arises by operation of law at the moment the claim arises - not by contract and not by registration. It is therefore a secret lien: nothing on the register discloses it, and a purchaser cannot discover it by searching.
  3. It is inchoate until it is enforced. It sleeps with the ship and is crystallised only by an action in rem, arrest and, if security is not put up, forced sale.
  4. It ranks ahead of registered mortgages, which is the feature that makes it commercially significant and which a mortgagee bank has to price.

The justification for so drastic a right is practical, and it is worth one sentence because it explains which claims qualify: the claimants protected are those who could not realistically pursue the owner - a crew paid nothing and put ashore in a foreign port, a salvor who saved the ship, an injured person, a port whose services were consumed. They are given the ship instead of the owner because the owner may be a single-vessel company with no other asset.

2. How the lien travels with the property

This is the limb the stem singles out, and it should be answered as three propositions and a single exception.

It follows the ship. The lien continues on the vessel notwithstanding any change of ownership, of registration or of flag. A ship sold commercially, re-registered in another State and given a new name carries her liens with her under the new name, and the new owner takes her subject to them. This is the meaning of the expression that the lien travels with the property, and it is also why the technique is sometimes described as the claim following the ship rather than the man.

It binds even a purchaser who knew nothing. Because the lien is secret and unregistered, good faith and absence of notice are no defence. A buyer's only real protections are a warranty and indemnity from the seller, an inspection of the vessel's recent trading and claims history, and - decisively - buying at a court sale rather than privately.

It does not last forever. The lien is extinguished after one year unless, before that year expires, the vessel has been arrested or seized and that has led to a forced sale. For crew claims the period is two years. Time runs continuously, without suspension or interruption, save that any period during which the vessel was under arrest or seizure is excluded. It starts from the claimant's discharge from the vessel for a wages claim and from when the claim arises for the others. Underline continuously: the ordinary rules that stop the clock for infancy, absence or negotiation do not apply, and a claimant who negotiates for thirteen months has lost his lien and kept only an ordinary claim.

The one thing that defeats it is a forced sale by the court. A sale in the exercise of admiralty jurisdiction vests the vessel in the purchaser free from all encumbrances, liens, attachments, registered mortgages and charges of the same nature. The liens are not destroyed - they are transferred to the proceeds of sale, and the fight moves from the ship to the fund. That is the whole commercial point of a judicial sale, and it is why a ship with heavy liens is worth more sold by a court than sold by her owner.

3. In rem - the action against the ship

An action in rem is an action against the thing. The vessel herself is named as defendant; the writ is served on the ship, physically, and she may be arrested and held by the court. The maritime lien is precisely what gives the right to proceed in rem, and the two ideas are so close that candidates often merge them - but they are not the same, and the distinction is examinable:

  • Every maritime lien gives a right in rem. Not every right in rem is a maritime lien. Modern statutes also give a statutory right of action in rem for a much longer list of maritime claims - necessaries, towage, repairs, cargo damage, agency. That right is enforceable by arrest, but it does not travel with the ship on a change of ownership and it does not outrank a mortgage. It is a procedural right; the lien is a proprietary one.
  • Arrest is a means, not an end. The purpose of arrest is almost never to sell the ship. It is to compel security - a P&I club letter of undertaking, a bank guarantee or a payment into court - after which the ship is released and the merits are litigated with the money already secured.
  • If no security is given, the ship is sold by the court and the proceeds become the fund out of which the claims are settled in the order dealt with below.

4. In personam - the action against the person

An action in personam is the ordinary action against a person - the owner, the demise charterer, the manager or the operator - leading to a judgment enforceable against that person's assets generally.

Its weakness is exactly the weakness the maritime lien exists to cure. Ships are commonly owned by single-ship companies registered in jurisdictions chosen for that purpose, whose only asset is the ship and which may be wound up the day after the casualty. A judgment in personam against an assetless company is a piece of paper. A claim in rem against the ship reaches a physical asset of real value that must eventually enter some port, wherever the company is registered and whatever has become of it.

In practice the two are frequently pursued together: the action in rem to secure the claim by arrest, and the action in personam to establish liability against a defendant who can be made to pay if the ship's value is insufficient. In India both are exercised by the High Courts in their admiralty jurisdiction, and the statute keeps them separate - one provision governs arrest and the proceeding in rem, another the jurisdiction in personam.

5. Which convention deals with maritime lien

The International Convention on Maritime Liens and Mortgages, 1993, adopted at Geneva on 6 May 1993 and in force since 5 September 2004. It is the third attempt at the subject, replacing the Brussels Conventions of 1926 and 1967, neither of which achieved wide acceptance - the perennial difficulty being that States could not agree on how long the list of privileged claims should be, since every claim added to it is a claim taken away from the mortgagee.

The 1993 Convention's solution, and the reason it is examinable, is that it fixes a short and closed international list of five liens and then requires that any additional liens a State chooses to recognise under its own law rank after the five and after registered mortgages. The mortgagee therefore knows the worst case.

Say plainly that India is not a party to it. India gives effect to its scheme domestically through the Admiralty (Jurisdiction and Settlement of Maritime Claims) Act, 2017, in force 1 April 2018, which is the statute an Indian candidate is actually governed by. Answering only with the Convention, or presenting the Convention as applying in India, is the commonest error on this question.

6. The five secured claims, and how they rank among themselves

The Convention secures five claims against the owner, demise charterer, manager or operator of the vessel, and the Indian Act reproduces them in the same order. Learn the order; it is the answer to the last sentence of the stem.

  1. Crew claims - wages and other sums due to the master, officers and other members of the vessel's complement in respect of their employment on the vessel, including costs of repatriation and social insurance contributions payable on their behalf.
  2. Loss of life or personal injury occurring, whether on land or on water, in direct connection with the operation of the vessel.
  3. Salvage - reward for salvage services, including special compensation.
  4. Port, canal and other waterway dues and pilotage dues, and other statutory dues related to the vessel.
  5. Tort claims for loss or damage caused by the operation of the vessel, other than loss or damage to cargo, containers and passengers' effects carried on the vessel.

Three rules govern how these rank against each other and against everything else.

  • Maritime liens take priority over registered mortgages, hypotheques and charges. This is the central rule of the whole subject.
  • Salvage jumps the queue. A salvage lien ranks ahead of all liens which attached before the salvage operation, and salvage liens rank among themselves in inverse order of time - the later salvor is paid first. The logic is obvious once stated: without the last salvor there would be no ship to distribute, and nobody would answer a distress call if the reward ranked behind the debts he had just rescued.
  • Within a category, claims rank equally and abate rateably. Crew claims among themselves, tort claims among themselves - if the fund is short, each is paid the same proportion.

Two categories are excluded from lien protection altogether, and the reason is that they are compensated elsewhere: damage in connection with the carriage of oil or other hazardous and noxious substances by sea for which compensation is payable under any law for the time being in force, and damage arising from the radioactive properties of nuclear fuel, products or waste. Note the drafting - the oil exclusion operates by asking whether compensation is payable under another regime, so it works correctly whatever the state of ratification of the regime in question.

7. The order of settlement after legal action

This is what the stem finally asks for: once the court has sold the ship, in what order is the fund paid out? Under the Convention's forced-sale scheme it runs as follows, and the sequence is logical rather than arbitrary.

  1. The costs and expenses of the arrest, seizure and sale, and of the upkeep of the vessel and of the crew during that period. These come first for a plain reason: nobody would fund an arrest if the cost of it ranked behind the claims it was funding, and the crew must be fed and repatriated while the ship lies under arrest.
  2. Wreck removal costs, where the national law of the State of forced sale gives them that priority. This is an option States may take; it is not automatic.
  3. The five maritime liens, in the order set out in section 6 above, with the salvage rules applied.
  4. A shipbuilder's or shiprepairer's right of retention, where national law recognises one and the vessel is still in their possession.
  5. Registered mortgages, hypotheques and charges.
  6. All other claims - including any additional maritime liens granted under national law, and the ordinary maritime claims enforced by a statutory right in rem.
  7. Any residue to the owner, which in practice is rare.

And the purchaser takes the vessel free of every one of them. That is the consideration for the whole scheme: the ship is cleaned of her history so that she can be sold for what she is worth, and the claimants fight over the money instead of over the ship.

8. The Indian position, stated separately

Because India is not a party to the Convention, close on the statute that actually governs. The Admiralty (Jurisdiction and Settlement of Maritime Claims) Act, 2017, in force 1 April 2018, consolidated the admiralty jurisdiction of the High Courts and repealed the colonial-era enactments that preceded it.

  • It lists the same five maritime liens in the same order - wages first, then loss of life or personal injury, then salvage, then port, canal, waterway and pilotage dues, then tort excluding cargo and containers.
  • The lien continues notwithstanding any change of ownership, registration or flag, and is extinguished after one year unless arrest or seizure before that has led to a forced sale by the High Court - two years for crew claims, running from the date the sum falls due.
  • It commences on discharge for a crew claim and when the claim arises for the rest, and runs continuously without suspension or interruption, excluding time under arrest or seizure.
  • The same oil, HNS and nuclear exclusions apply.
  • The order of priority in an admiralty proceeding is three-tier and simpler than the Convention's: first, claims where there is a maritime lien; second, registered mortgages and charges of the same nature; third, all other claims. Within any single category claims rank equally, and claims for various salvages rank in inverse order of time.
  • On a court sale the vessel vests in the purchaser free from all encumbrances, liens, attachments, registered mortgages and charges of the same nature.
  • Arrest in rem and jurisdiction in personam are provided for separately, and both are exercised by the High Courts - not by the maritime administration.

Finish on the proposition that makes the whole subject coherent: a maritime lien is the law's answer to a debtor who can disappear but whose ship cannot. Everything else - the secrecy, the travelling with the property, the priority over mortgages, the one-year limit - is a consequence of granting that right and then having to keep it within bounds.

Study guide

Why this structure scores

The stem is five questions in one paragraph: what is it, how does it travel, what are in rem and in personam, which convention, and what is the order of settlement. The structure answers them in that order and adds nothing before the first of them, because the marker is looking for five answers and an essay that reaches the convention in the last two lines loses most of them.

Section 1's four numbered features are the load-bearing device. Once they are on the page, section 2 is feature 1 and feature 2 worked out, section 3 is feature 3 worked out, and the priority over mortgages in section 6 is feature 4 restated. A candidate who omits them has to argue each point separately and usually runs out of time.

Keeping the Convention and the Indian Act in separate sections is a deliberate decision and not padding. India is NOT a party to the 1993 Convention. An answer that merges them has told the examiner that the Convention binds India, which it does not, and that is the sort of error that costs more than it looks like it should.

Common mistakes

  • Getting the priority order inverted, with salvage first and wages second. WAGES ARE FIRST. The inverted form has circulated in study material and it is wrong; the statute reads (a) wages, (b) loss of life or personal injury, (c) salvage, (d) dues, (e) tort.
  • Saying the Convention applies in India. It does not - India is not a party. The Admiralty Act, 2017 gives effect to its scheme domestically.
  • Treating a maritime lien and a right of action in rem as the same thing. Every lien gives a right in rem; not every right in rem is a lien. The statutory right in rem does not survive a change of ownership and does not outrank a mortgage.
  • Confusing a maritime lien with a possessory lien. A shiprepairer's lien depends on possession and dies when he gives the ship back; a maritime lien has never involved possession.
  • Forgetting the two-year period for crew claims, or applying it to all five liens. It is a proviso for the crew claim only.
  • Saying time is suspended by negotiations. It runs CONTINUOUSLY, and the only exclusion is time under arrest or seizure.
  • Omitting the costs of arrest and sale from the top of the distribution. They rank ahead of the liens themselves and the answer looks incomplete without them.
  • Answering the printed 'debtor's claims' literally. What is being settled are the creditors' claims against the debtor's vessel.

Examiner traps

  • Wages rank first, not salvage. This is the single most reliably examined point in the question and it has been published the wrong way round.
  • But salvage jumps liens that attached before the salvage, and salvages rank among themselves in INVERSE order of time. Both propositions are true at once and the examiner will test whether you can hold them together.
  • India is not a party to the 1993 Convention. Expect 'so does the Convention bind an Indian court?' It does not; the Act does.
  • A forced sale by the court is the only thing that cleans the ship. A private sale, a change of flag or a change of name does not. Expect to be asked what a buyer should do about it.
  • The lien attaches to the ship, not to the owner's fleet. Sister-ship arrest is a feature of the statutory right in rem under national law, not of the maritime lien.
  • Cargo and container damage is expressly EXCLUDED from the tort lien. Candidates routinely include it because cargo claims are the commonest maritime claims there are.
  • Oil and HNS damage is excluded where compensation is payable under another regime - because it is compensated there, not because it is unimportant.
  • The 1993 Convention took eleven years to enter into force - adopted 6 May 1993, in force 5 September 2004. It is a usable example for the entry-into-force question elsewhere on this paper.

Likely oral follow-up

  • Your ship is arrested in Singapore for a claim against the previous owner. Can that be right?
  • What is the difference between a maritime lien and a shiprepairer's possessory lien?
  • Two salvors saved the ship, six months apart. Who is paid first, and why?
  • The crew have not been paid for fourteen months. Do they still have a lien?
  • The owner is a one-ship company with no other assets. Why would you bother suing him in personam at all?
  • What actually happens to the mortgage when the court sells the ship?
  • Cargo was damaged by bad stowage. Is there a maritime lien for it?
  • Which court in India would you go to, and under what statute?
  • How does a buyer protect himself against liens he cannot discover?

Memory framework

The route is WHAT - TRAVELS - IN REM - IN PERSONAM - CONVENTION - FIVE CLAIMS - ORDER - INDIA. For the four features of a lien hold RES - SECRET - INCHOATE - PRIORITY. For the five claims hold WAGES - LIFE - SALVAGE - DUES - TORT, in that order, and remember that cargo is out of the last one. For the distribution hold COSTS FIRST, LIENS, RETENTION, MORTGAGES, THE REST. And hold one sentence verbatim: a maritime lien is the law's answer to a debtor who can disappear but whose ship cannot.

Regulation and source map

  • International Convention on Maritime Liens and Mortgages, 1993 - adopted Geneva 6 May 1993, in force 5 September 2004. Successor to the Brussels Conventions of 1926 and 1967. India is not a party.
  • Convention article 4 - the five secured claims and the oil, HNS and nuclear exclusions.
  • Convention article 5 - priority over registered mortgages; the inter se order; salvage jumping earlier liens and ranking in inverse order of time.
  • Convention article 6 - national liens rank after the five and after mortgages.
  • Convention article 8 - the lien follows the vessel notwithstanding change of ownership, registration or flag.
  • Convention article 9 - extinction after one year, two years for crew claims.
  • Convention article 12 - forced sale, the distribution order, and the purchaser's clean title.
  • Admiralty (Jurisdiction and Settlement of Maritime Claims) Act, 2017 - in force 1 April 2018. Section 8 vesting free of encumbrances; section 9(1) the five liens in order; 9(2) travelling and extinction; 9(3) commencement and continuous running; 9(4) the exclusions; section 10 the three-tier order of claims.
  • Merchant Shipping Act, 1958 - the general Indian shipping statute at this sitting. It does not govern admiralty priority, which is the 2017 Act's subject.

Uncertainty and jurisdiction notes

  • The Indian half of this answer was read at primary source for this paper. Sections 8, 9 and 10 of the Admiralty Act, 2017 were read verbatim at the statute itself, and the priority order given here is the statutory text and not a summary of it.
  • We do not hold the 1993 Convention itself. Its articles are carried from our own verified records, which read the certified text. Nothing here quotes a Convention article, and where the Convention and the Act differ the Act is what is relied on.
  • The wreck-removal priority is optional under the Convention and is not asserted as Indian law. Whether India has exercised the option is not established from held sources.
  • No monetary figure or limit appears. Maritime liens are not capped and this question does not reach limitation of liability.
  • Admiralty jurisdiction is exercised by the High Courts, not by the Directorate General of Shipping. The maritime administration does not appear in this answer at all.
Recall the structure

Before you read the answer: can you name all 8 sections, in order? Say them or write them down, then reveal.

  1. __________What a maritime lien is
  2. __________How the lien travels with the property
  3. __________In rem - the action against the ship
  4. __________In personam - the action against the person
  5. __________Which convention deals with maritime lien
  6. __________The five secured claims, and how they rank among themselves
  7. __________The order of settlement after legal action
  8. __________The Indian position, stated separately
Flashcards 9

1 what a lien is, in four features; 2 how it travels with the property; 3 in rem; 4 in personam; 5 which convention; 6 the five claims and their internal ranking; 7 the order of settlement after forced sale; 8 the Indian position under the Admiralty Act 2017, kept separate because India is not a party.

A privileged claim against the vessel herself, arising by operation of law. It attaches to the res and not to a person; it arises at the moment the claim arises without registration, so it is secret; it is inchoate until enforced by an action in rem, arrest and forced sale; and it ranks ahead of registered mortgages. The claimants protected are those who could not realistically pursue the owner.

(a) crew wages and other sums due to master, officers and complement, including repatriation costs and social insurance contributions; (b) loss of life or personal injury in direct connection with the operation of the vessel; (c) salvage reward including special compensation; (d) port, canal and other waterway dues and pilotage dues; (e) tort for loss or damage by the operation of the vessel, excluding cargo and containers. Wages first.

In rem is against the ship herself, which is named as defendant, served physically and may be arrested to compel security. In personam is against a person - owner, demise charterer, manager or operator - and is worthless against a single-ship company with no other assets. Both are commonly brought: in rem to secure the claim, in personam to establish liability if the ship's value is insufficient.

First the costs and expenses of arrest, seizure and sale and the upkeep of vessel and crew; then wreck removal where national law gives it priority; then the five maritime liens in order; then a shipbuilder's or shiprepairer's right of retention; then registered mortgages and charges; then all other claims; then any residue to the owner. The purchaser takes free of all of it.

The International Convention on Maritime Liens and Mortgages, 1993, adopted at Geneva on 6 May 1993 and in force since 5 September 2004, replacing the Brussels Conventions of 1926 and 1967. India is NOT a party. India gives effect to its scheme through the Admiralty (Jurisdiction and Settlement of Maritime Claims) Act, 2017, in force 1 April 2018, which the High Courts apply.

No. Wages rank first, then loss of life or personal injury, then salvage. The inverted order with salvage first has circulated in study material and is wrong. What is true about salvage is different: a salvage lien ranks ahead of liens that attached BEFORE the salvage operation, and salvage liens rank among themselves in inverse order of time, so the later salvor is paid first.

No. The lien is secret, unregistered and undiscoverable, and it continues notwithstanding change of ownership, registration or flag - good faith is no defence. Only a forced sale in the exercise of admiralty jurisdiction passes clean title, the liens transferring to the proceeds. That is why a court sale can be worth more than a private one.

No. The tort lien expressly excludes loss or damage to cargo and containers carried on the vessel. Cargo claims are the commonest maritime claims there are, which is exactly why candidates include them by reflex. A cargo claimant still has a statutory right of action in rem and can arrest, but he ranks after the liens and after registered mortgages.

Quick revision
15-second recall
A lien on the ship, not on the man. Four features: attaches to the res, is secret, is inchoate until arrest, and outranks mortgages. It follows the ship through sale, re-registration and re-flagging, dies after one year (two for crew), and is cleaned only by a court sale. In rem = against the ship, to compel security; in personam = against a person, worthless against a one-ship company. Convention: Maritime Liens and Mortgages 1993, in force 5 September 2004 - India is not a party; the Admiralty Act 2017 governs. Order: WAGES - LIFE - SALVAGE - DUES - TORT, after the costs of arrest and sale.
