Critical regulation
Admiralty (Jurisdiction and Settlement of Maritime Claims) Act, 2017, section 9(1) - the five liens in their statutory order, wages first. If you remember one thing, remember that this is the provision an Indian court actually applies, because India is not a party to the 1993 Convention even though the Act reproduces its scheme.
Major trap
Putting salvage first in the priority order. It is fourth in the list as printed and third in the ranking - wages, then loss of life or personal injury, then salvage. The inverted order has been published in study material. What IS true about salvage is separate: a salvage lien jumps ahead of liens that attached before the salvage, and salvages rank among themselves in inverse order of time.

Also on the platform: QP2308 Q5 - the other question on this paper where a private body acts with public consequences, and where the limits of its authority decide the answer · QP2308 Q7 - the treaty machinery by which a convention such as the 1993 Convention enters into force, and why this one took eleven years · QP2308 Q8 - the certificates whose absence can lead to detention, which is a different kind of hold on a ship from an arrest

Repeated — same wording Returns in the same words from April 2023 Q5. Also set at April 2024 Q3, September 2025 Q4, December 2025 Q9.

Understand this first

A propeller pushes the ship forward, but it also leaves the water behind it spinning, and that spinning column is energy the engine paid for and the ship never got. There is a second waste right behind the middle of the propeller, where the boss stops abruptly and the water cannot close in behind it, so a tight whirlpool forms and sits there for the whole voyage. Now notice where the rudder is: it is standing directly in both of those, and it is the only big thing back there. So the idea behind every one of these devices is the same, and it is worth holding as a single sentence - put something in the wasted water and get a little of it back. A horizontal fin sitting in the spin meets it at an angle and, like any wing, produces lift; because the fin lies flat, part of that lift points forwards, which is thrust you did not pay for. Twisting the rudder itself does something subtler: the water is coming from one side above the shaft and the other side below it, so an ordinary flat rudder is being pushed sideways all the time even with the wheel amidships, and shaping each half to face its own flow simply stops that waste. A bulb on the rudder's nose fills the hole behind the boss so the whirlpool never forms. And the strangest of them is a free-spinning wheel that nothing drives - its inner part is turned by the spinning race like a windmill, and that rotation makes its outer part, which is out in still water, act as a small propeller. The two things to remember are the limits. Nothing here adds power - each device is only recovering a share of a waste that has a fixed size, so fitting three of them does not give you three times the saving, because they are all eating the same pie. And a dirty hull will swallow the whole gain, which is why cleaning comes before buying.

Knowledge map
Rudder Efficiency Improvement Devices
  1. 1Where the recoverable energy is
    • Rotational or swirl energy left in the race - the largest recoverable loss
    • The hub vortex behind the boss - drag, cavitation erosion and pressure pulses
    • Separation and drag on the rudder itself, which the rudder introduces
  2. 2(a) Rudder Thrust Fins
    • Horizontal wing-like fins each side of the rudder, in the slipstream, above and below the shaft line
    • The swirling inflow meets the fin at an angle of attack and the fin develops lift
    • Because the fin is horizontal, the resolved component of that lift points FORWARD - recovered thrust
  3. 3(b) Asymmetric Rudders
    • The section differs ABOVE and BELOW the shaft line - twisted leading edge, or offset halves
    • Swirl arrives from one side above the shaft and the other side below it
    • A symmetrical rudder therefore carries an incident angle over its whole span with the helm amidships
  4. 4(c) Rudder Bulbs
    • A streamlined body on the rudder leading edge, aligned with and close abaft the propeller boss
    • Fills the low-pressure space where the hub vortex forms and lets the flow close along a fair body
    • Removes vortex drag, a source of rudder cavitation erosion, and part of the hull pressure pulse
  5. 5(d) Grim Vane Wheels
    • A freely rotating wheel of LARGER diameter than the propeller, mounted abaft it and connected to nothing
    • Inner span lies inside the race and is driven round as a TURBINE
    • Outer span lies outside the race in undisturbed water and acts as a PROPELLER, producing thrust
  6. 6What the four have in common, and how they combine
    • All four are recovery devices, not power sources
    • Three attack rotational energy; only the bulb attacks the hub vortex - which is why it combines so well
    • THE GAINS DO NOT ADD - they draw on the same finite pool of wasted energy
  7. 7Selection and retrofit in practice
    • Bulb and fins are realistic dry-dock retrofits; an asymmetric rudder generally is not
    • Judge against the ship's ACTUAL operating profile, not the design point the gain was computed at
    • Do the cheap things first - a fouled hull or rough propeller erases the whole gain
  8. 8Judging whether a claimed gain is real
    • Quoted figures are model-scale or CFD at design draught, trim, speed, calm water and clean hull
    • Scale effects on wake and boundary layer flatter the model, and wake devices are where that error is largest
    • Combined-device gains are not additive
Start here 16 marks

Write these headings first, then expand them in order. If time is short, get every heading down before you expand any of them.

  1. Where the recoverable energy is
  2. (a) Rudder Thrust Fins
  3. (b) Asymmetric Rudders
  4. (c) Rudder Bulbs
  5. (d) Grim Vane Wheels
  6. What the four have in common, and how they combine
  7. Selection and retrofit in practice
  8. Judging whether a claimed gain is real

Memory cue. LOSSES - FINS - ASYMMETRIC - BULB - VANE WHEEL - COMMON GROUND - SELECTION - VERIFICATION (1 to 8). Inside step 1 hold SWIRL - HUB VORTEX - RUDDER DRAG. Inside each of steps 2 to 5 hold WHAT - HOW - PRACTICAL - LIMITS.

Remember8 route headings
Cover49 core points beneath them

Memorise the 8 headings. The core points are there to help you check whether an answer is too thin — they are not a mark scheme, and they are not 49 separate facts to learn individually.

Show the core points to cover
1. Where the recoverable energy is
  • Rotational or swirl energy left in the race - the largest recoverable loss
  • The hub vortex behind the boss - drag, cavitation erosion and pressure pulses
  • Separation and drag on the rudder itself, which the rudder introduces
  • The rudder is the only large appendage standing in that accelerated, swirling flow
  • A rudder device RECOVERS waste; it does not create thrust, so its gain is bounded by the loss
2. (a) Rudder Thrust Fins
  • Horizontal wing-like fins each side of the rudder, in the slipstream, above and below the shaft line
  • The swirling inflow meets the fin at an angle of attack and the fin develops lift
  • Because the fin is horizontal, the resolved component of that lift points FORWARD - recovered thrust
  • Fins are HANDED and set at opposite angles above and below, because the swirl reverses
  • Usually mounted on a bulb, which carries the roots; they add wetted surface and some drag
  • Claimed at about 2 to 5 per cent at design condition, falling away off design draught and speed
3. (b) Asymmetric Rudders
  • The section differs ABOVE and BELOW the shaft line - twisted leading edge, or offset halves
  • Swirl arrives from one side above the shaft and the other side below it
  • A symmetrical rudder therefore carries an incident angle over its whole span with the helm amidships
  • Consequences: induced drag, separation and rudder CAVITATION at the leading edge and horn gap
  • Twisting aligns each half with its own local flow; course keeping improves too
  • Designed for one propeller and one direction of rotation; a newbuilding or conversion item, not a bolt-on
  • Claimed at about 2 to 6 per cent - and the cavitation and erosion benefit is often the real one
4. (c) Rudder Bulbs
  • A streamlined body on the rudder leading edge, aligned with and close abaft the propeller boss
  • Fills the low-pressure space where the hub vortex forms and lets the flow close along a fair body
  • Removes vortex drag, a source of rudder cavitation erosion, and part of the hull pressure pulse
  • Gap and alignment are critical - too far aft or off axis and it achieves little
  • Commonly the structural base for thrust fins; one of the more practicable retrofits
  • Claimed at about 2 to 5 per cent, larger on heavily loaded propellers where the vortex is strongest
5. (d) Grim Vane Wheels
  • A freely rotating wheel of LARGER diameter than the propeller, mounted abaft it and connected to nothing
  • Inner span lies inside the race and is driven round as a TURBINE
  • Outer span lies outside the race in undisturbed water and acts as a PROPELLER, producing thrust
  • Absorbs no shaft power at all - it is driven entirely by wasted rotational energy
  • Claims reached about 10 per cent, the highest of the four
  • Service experience was poor: blade fatigue and blade loss on the slender outer span, and bearing problems
  • Rare in service today - the stem says 'currently in use' and the honest answer says so
6. What the four have in common, and how they combine
  • All four are recovery devices, not power sources
  • Three attack rotational energy; only the bulb attacks the hub vortex - which is why it combines so well
  • THE GAINS DO NOT ADD - they draw on the same finite pool of wasted energy
  • Every device is specific to a wake field, propeller, rotation direction, draught and speed
  • They are permanent appendages with structural, fatigue and survey consequences
  • The propeller-side family - ducts, boss cap fins, pre-swirl stators - attacks the same losses from ahead
7. Selection and retrofit in practice
  • Bulb and fins are realistic dry-dock retrofits; an asymmetric rudder generally is not
  • Judge against the ship's ACTUAL operating profile, not the design point the gain was computed at
  • Do the cheap things first - a fouled hull or rough propeller erases the whole gain
  • Driver at this sitting: EEXI and operational CII since 1 January 2023, and the 2023 GHG Strategy of 7 July 2023
  • Class approval for attachment, scantlings and fatigue; reassess rudder cavitation after modification
  • Exposed appendages are damaged by ice, tugs and contact, and are a docking survey item
8. Judging whether a claimed gain is real
  • Quoted figures are model-scale or CFD at design draught, trim, speed, calm water and clean hull
  • Scale effects on wake and boundary layer flatter the model, and wake devices are where that error is largest
  • Combined-device gains are not additive
  • The ship rarely operates at the design point - trim, draught, fouling, weather, part load
  • Verify by continuous hull and propeller performance monitoring on ISO 19030, over enough time to separate the device from fouling
  • Ask for full-scale before-and-after data from a sister ship in a comparable trade
Model written answer

1. Where the recoverable energy is

Start here, because all four devices in the stem are doing the same thing and the answer is incoherent without it. A propeller does not convert all the shaft power it absorbs into useful thrust. Two of the losses are physically recoverable, and both of them are left in the water immediately abaft the propeller - which is exactly where the rudder sits.

  • Rotational or swirl loss. The propeller imparts a tangential velocity to the race as well as an axial one. That rotating column of water carries real kinetic energy which does nothing for the ship, and it is typically the largest single recoverable loss - of the order of several per cent of delivered power on a conventional single-screw ship.
  • The hub vortex. Behind the propeller boss there is a low-pressure core into which a concentrated vortex forms. It costs drag, it is a source of cavitation erosion and pressure pulses, and it is entirely wasteful.
  • Separation and drag on the rudder itself. Not a propeller loss at all, but a loss the rudder introduces - and one that can be reduced by shaping the rudder correctly for the flow it actually meets.

The rudder is the only large appendage standing in the accelerated, swirling slipstream, and it is already there, already structurally supported and already surveyed. That is why so much energy-saving effort has been directed at it. State plainly what follows: a rudder device recovers energy that is already being wasted. It does not create thrust from nothing, and its gain is therefore bounded by the size of the loss it is recovering from.

2. (a) Rudder Thrust Fins

What it is. A pair of horizontal, wing-like fins projecting from each side of the rudder, set within the propeller slipstream, usually one pair above and one pair below the shaft line, and generally close to the leading edge.

How it works. The flow arriving at the fin is not axial - it carries the propeller's swirl, and above the shaft line the tangential component is in one direction while below it the direction is reversed. Each fin is therefore set at a small angle of attack to its own local flow, and as an aerofoil it develops lift. Because the fin is horizontal, the lift vector is roughly athwartships and its resolved component points forward - that forward component is recovered thrust. Simultaneously the fin straightens the swirl it has taken energy out of, which reduces the residual rotational loss further aft.

Practical points that earn marks. The fins are handed: because the swirl reverses across the shaft line, the upper and lower fins are set at opposite angles, and a fin fitted the wrong way up is a drag device. They are usually combined with a rudder bulb, which carries the fin roots and gives them a sound structural attachment. They add wetted surface and therefore some frictional drag, so the net gain is the recovered thrust minus that penalty. Claimed gains are of the order of 2 to 5 per cent at design condition.

Limitations. The gain falls away off the design draught and speed, because the swirl field and the fin's angle of attack both change. In heavy weather and astern running the fins are loaded in ways they were not optimised for, and their attachment to the rudder must be designed for it.

3. (b) Asymmetric Rudders

What it is. A rudder whose section is not the same above and below the propeller shaft line - either twisted, so that the leading edge is turned one way above the shaft and the other way below it, or offset, with the two halves displaced relative to the centreline.

How it works. The reasoning follows directly from section 1. Above the shaft line the swirling race approaches the rudder from one side; below it, from the other. A conventional symmetrical rudder set on the centreline therefore meets an incident angle of attack over almost its entire span, even with the helm amidships. The consequences are continuous lift-induced drag, flow separation, and - most importantly on a modern high-power ship - cavitation on the rudder surface, particularly at the leading edge and around the gap at the horn.

Twisting the leading edge so that each half is aligned with its own local inflow removes the built-in incidence. The result is less separation, less drag, and a rudder that is working with the flow rather than against it. Course-keeping usually improves as well, because the rudder is no longer generating a small standing side force that the helmsman or autopilot must trim out.

Practical points. The twist is designed for one propeller, one direction of rotation and one loading condition, so an asymmetric rudder is not a general-purpose component and cannot simply be transferred between ships. It is frequently combined with a leading-edge bulb and with fins, the three being designed together. It is a newbuilding or major-conversion item rather than a bolt-on retrofit, because the rudder itself is replaced. Claimed gains are of the order of 2 to 6 per cent, and on many installations the cavitation and erosion benefit is the real one - a rudder that stops eroding saves steel repairs and off-hire whether or not the fuel saving is realised.

4. (c) Rudder Bulbs

What it is. A streamlined, roughly axisymmetric body fitted on the leading edge of the rudder, aligned with and close abaft the propeller boss, so that boss and bulb together form a continuous faired body.

How it works. It attacks the hub vortex specifically. The propeller boss ends abruptly, the flow cannot close behind it, and a low-pressure core forms in which the hub vortex is generated. Placing a bulb in that space fills the volume the vortex would occupy and allows the flow to close gradually along a fair body instead of separating. The vortex is suppressed or greatly weakened, and with it go the vortex drag, a source of cavitation erosion on the rudder, and part of the pressure-pulse excitation transmitted into the hull.

Practical points. The gap between boss and bulb, and the alignment, are critical - a bulb that is too far aft or off the shaft axis achieves very little, and the installation tolerance is a real construction issue. The bulb is very commonly the structural base for thrust fins, and the combined arrangement is what most makers actually supply. It is one of the more practicable retrofits, because it can often be added to an existing rudder in dry dock without replacing it. Claimed gains are of the order of 2 to 5 per cent, and are larger on heavily loaded propellers, where the hub vortex is strongest - which also means the benefit is greatest on exactly the ships that suffer most from rudder erosion.

5. (d) Grim Vane Wheels

What it is. A freely rotating wheel of larger diameter than the propeller, mounted on the propeller shaft or on its own bearing immediately abaft the propeller, and connected to nothing. It absorbs no shaft power at all.

How it works. This is the most elegant idea of the four and it should be explained in two parts, because the whole device turns on the fact that its blades do two different jobs at two different radii.

  • The inner portion of the span lies inside the propeller race. There the swirling, accelerated flow drives the blade section round - the wheel is acting as a turbine, extracting rotational energy from the slipstream and using it to spin itself.
  • The outer portion of the span lies outside the race, in undisturbed water. There the same rotation makes the blade section act as a propeller, generating additional thrust.
  • The wheel therefore takes wasted rotational energy from the race and converts it into axial thrust in the surrounding water, taking nothing from the engine. It free-wheels at a speed set by the balance between the turbine torque inboard and the propeller torque outboard.

Why it is treated differently from the other three. The stem asks about devices currently in use, and an honest answer distinguishes here. Claimed gains reached about 10 per cent, the highest of any device in this family, and a number of ships were fitted in the 1980s. Service experience was poor. The outer blade span is long, slender and lightly supported, and it runs in a highly unsteady flow at the boundary between the race and the free stream; blade fatigue and blade loss were experienced, along with bearing problems on the free-running hub. A lost blade means severe imbalance on an unrestrained wheel immediately abaft the propeller. The device is consequently rare in service today, and is examined mainly because it demonstrates the principle in its purest form.

6. What the four have in common, and how they combine

Pull the four together before moving on, because this is where a candidate demonstrates understanding rather than recall.

  • All four are recovery devices, not power sources. Each takes energy that is already in the water and turns some of it into thrust or removes a drag penalty. None improves the engine, the shafting or the propeller itself.
  • Three of the four attack rotational energy - fins, twist and the vane wheel all work on the swirl. Only the bulb attacks a different loss, the hub vortex, which is why the bulb combines so well with the others.
  • The gains do not add. This is the single most important qualification in the answer. A bulb, a pair of fins and a twisted section are all drawing on the same finite pool of wasted energy, so fitting all three does not give the sum of their individually claimed percentages. The combined arrangement must be designed and tested as one system.
  • They are propeller-and-hull specific. Every one of them is optimised for a particular wake field, propeller, direction of rotation, draught and speed. A device transferred from a sister ship in a different trade will not perform as advertised.
  • They are permanent appendages with structural and survey consequences. Attachment, welding, fatigue and inspection access have to be considered, and the fitting is examined at the ship's underwater surveys.

For completeness in one sentence, so the examiner sees the boundary: the propeller-side members of the same family - ducts, boss cap fins and pre-swirl stators - attack the same losses from ahead of or on the propeller, and the stem confines this answer to the rudder side.

7. Selection and retrofit in practice

The stem is set in an Engineering Management paper, so say how the choice is actually made.

  • Retrofit feasibility. A bulb, and fins on a bulb, are realistic retrofits in a scheduled dry docking. An asymmetric rudder generally is not, because the rudder is replaced; it belongs to a newbuilding or a major conversion.
  • Operating profile, not design point. The device is optimised at a draught and speed. Judge it against the ship's actual laden and ballast draughts and her actual service speed, which after years of slow steaming is frequently well below the design point at which the gain was calculated.
  • Do the cheap things first. A fouled hull or a rough, pitted propeller will erase the entire gain of any of these devices, and hull and propeller cleaning costs a fraction of a retrofit. It is difficult to justify an energy saving device on a ship whose hull performance is not already being managed.
  • Commercial and regulatory driver. At this sitting the pressure is real and current: EEXI and the operational carbon intensity rating have applied since 1 January 2023, and the 2023 IMO GHG Strategy was adopted on 7 July 2023, one month before this examination. A device that improves the attained EEXI or the annual rating has a value beyond the fuel bill.
  • Structural and class approval. The attachment, the local scantlings and the fatigue assessment need the society's approval, and the rudder's cavitation behaviour after modification should be assessed rather than assumed.
  • Docking and inspection. Fins and bulbs are exposed appendages. They are damaged by ice, by tugs and by contact, and their condition is a docking survey item.

8. Judging whether a claimed gain is real

Close on the critical judgement, because this is what separates an engineer's answer from a brochure.

  • Quoted percentages are almost always model-scale or computational results at the design condition - design draught, design trim, design speed, calm water, clean hull.
  • Scale effects flatter the model. The wake field and the boundary layer of a model do not scale exactly to full size, and energy saving devices working in the wake are precisely where that error is largest.
  • The gains of combined devices are not additive, for the reason given in section 6.
  • The ship rarely operates at the design point. Trim, draught, fouling, weather and part-load running all move her away from it.
  • Verify in service by measurement, not by assertion. The route is continuous hull and propeller performance monitoring - shaft power against speed through water, corrected for displacement, trim and weather - assessed on the industry standard for measuring changes in hull and propeller performance, ISO 19030, over enough time to separate the device's effect from fouling.
  • Ask for full-scale before-and-after data from a sister ship in a comparable trade, and treat a maker unable to supply it accordingly.

End on the proposition that governs the whole subject: these devices do not make the ship more powerful - they make her waste less. The gain is bounded by the loss, it is largest where the loss is largest, and it is real only if it can still be measured on the ship a year later.

Study guide

Why this structure scores

The stem names four devices and invites four disconnected descriptions. The structure above refuses that: section 1 establishes the two recoverable losses first, and then each device is explained as an attack on one of them. The payoff is visible in section 6 - the candidate can say why the gains do not add, which is a proposition he cannot reach at all if he has described four devices in isolation.

Sections 2 to 5 are deliberately parallel - what it is, how it works, practical points, limitations - so the marker can find each device instantly and so the candidate has a template he can apply under time pressure to a device he half remembers. The parallel structure is also insurance: if time runs short, the last device still gets its two most valuable sentences.

Sections 7 and 8 are what lift this from a naval-architecture answer to an Engineering Management answer. The examiner is a marine engineer and the paper is a management paper; a candidate who can say which of these is a realistic retrofit, and how he would verify a claimed saving in service, has answered the question a Chief Engineer would actually be asked.

Common mistakes

  • Describing four devices without ever saying what energy they are recovering. The loss budget is the answer's spine and without it nothing else can be justified.
  • Adding the claimed percentages together. A bulb at 4 per cent plus fins at 4 per cent plus a twisted section at 4 per cent is not 12 per cent - all three are drawing on the same wasted energy.
  • Quoting percentages as though they were measured in service. They are model-scale or CFD figures at the design point and should be labelled as claims.
  • Getting the thrust fin mechanism wrong. The fin is HORIZONTAL, generates lift because the swirling flow meets it at an angle, and the FORWARD component of that lift is the recovered thrust. Fins are handed and set at opposite angles above and below the shaft line.
  • Saying an asymmetric rudder is asymmetric port-and-starboard for steering. It is asymmetric ABOVE AND BELOW the shaft line, because that is where the swirl reverses.
  • Presenting the Grim vane wheel as current equipment. The stem says 'currently in use' and honesty about its service record is worth more than pretending.
  • Forgetting that a fouled hull or a rough propeller erases the whole gain. Maintenance is the first energy saving measure.
  • Citing the 2018 Initial Strategy as the current IMO strategy. See the traps.

Examiner traps

  • The operative IMO strategy at this sitting is the 2023 Strategy, MEPC.377(80), adopted 7 July 2023 - one month before this paper. The Initial Strategy of 2018 has been superseded. Note the direction of this trap: it is not a later paper importing later law, it is an EARLIER sitting of the identical question whose correct citation has gone stale in the seven months between January and August 2023.
  • EEXI and the operational CII are already applicable, not forthcoming - both since 1 January 2023. The revised Annex VI that carries them entered into force 1 November 2022.
  • The swirl reverses across the shaft line. Expect to be asked why the upper and lower fins are set at opposite angles. If they were set the same way, one pair would be a drag device.
  • The rudder bulb attacks a DIFFERENT loss from the other three. Fins, twist and the vane wheel all work on rotational energy; the bulb works on the hub vortex. That is why the bulb combines with the others and why they compete with each other.
  • The vane wheel takes no power from the engine. Expect 'where does its energy come from?' From the wasted rotational energy in the race, which drives the inner span as a turbine.
  • An asymmetric rudder is designed for one propeller and one direction of rotation. Expect to be asked whether you could fit the sister ship's rudder. Not if she turns the other way.
  • The cavitation benefit is often the real benefit. A rudder that stops eroding saves steel and off-hire whether or not the fuel saving materialises - and saying so is a mark for judgement.

Likely oral follow-up

  • Where exactly is the energy that these devices recover?
  • Why are rudder thrust fins horizontal and not vertical?
  • Your fins are fitted the wrong way up. What happens?
  • Why is a rudder bulb aligned with the propeller boss, and what happens if the gap is wrong?
  • The vane wheel takes no shaft power. So is it free energy?
  • Which of these four would you actually retrofit in a scheduled docking, and which would you not?
  • The maker claims six per cent. How would you find out whether you are getting it?
  • Your ship trades at 12 knots and the device was optimised at 15. What do you expect?
  • What would you do before spending anything on an energy saving device?

Memory framework

The route is LOSSES - FINS - ASYMMETRIC - BULB - VANE WHEEL - COMMON GROUND - SELECTION - VERIFICATION. For the losses hold SWIRL - HUB VORTEX - RUDDER DRAG. For each device hold the same four-part template: WHAT - HOW - PRACTICAL - LIMITS. Hold one sentence for section 6: three of the four attack swirl, only the bulb attacks the hub vortex, and that is why the gains do not add. And hold the closing proposition verbatim: they do not make the ship more powerful, they make her waste less.

Regulation and source map

  • Resolution MEPC.377(80) - the 2023 IMO Strategy on Reduction of GHG Emissions from Ships, adopted 7 July 2023 at MEPC 80. The operative IMO strategy at this sitting.
  • MARPOL Annex VI chapter 4 as revised by MEPC.328(76) - EEXI and the operational CII, applicable from 1 January 2023. Context and commercial driver; no index value or rating boundary is asserted here.
  • ISO 19030 - measurement of changes in hull and propeller performance. The industry standard by which a claimed gain is verified in service. Named only; not read.
  • Classification society approval - required for the attachment, local scantlings and fatigue assessment of a retrofitted appendage, and for the reassessment of rudder cavitation after modification. Described by principle; no rule number is cited.

Uncertainty and evidence limits

  • No percentage in this answer is verified full-scale service data. Every figure is a claim at design condition and is labelled as such in the answer's own text. Published ranges are wide and inconsistent between sources, which is itself part of the answer.
  • ISO 19030 is named but not read. No part or clause is cited and nothing turns on its detail.
  • No class rule number is cited for appendage attachment or rudder cavitation. We hold no class rule text and the requirements are described by principle.
  • No claim is made about how many ships carry any device, or about any maker's market position. The statement that Grim vane wheels are rare in service today is a statement about service history, not a count.
  • we hold MARPOL Annex VI at citation level only - it resolves to identity and provenance, never to text - so no regulation is quoted, only cited.
Recall the structure

Before you read the answer: can you name all 8 sections, in order? Say them or write them down, then reveal.

  1. __________Where the recoverable energy is
  2. __________(a) Rudder Thrust Fins
  3. __________(b) Asymmetric Rudders
  4. __________(c) Rudder Bulbs
  5. __________(d) Grim Vane Wheels
  6. __________What the four have in common, and how they combine
  7. __________Selection and retrofit in practice
  8. __________Judging whether a claimed gain is real
Flashcards 9

1 where the recoverable energy is; 2 thrust fins; 3 asymmetric rudders; 4 rudder bulbs; 5 Grim vane wheels; 6 what they have in common and why the gains do not add; 7 selection and retrofit; 8 judging whether a claimed gain is real. Each of steps 2 to 5 uses the same template: what it is, how it works, practical points, limitations.

Two recoverable propeller losses: the rotational or swirl energy left in the race, which is the largest, and the hub vortex forming in the low-pressure core behind the boss. A third loss, separation and drag, is introduced by the rudder itself. The rudder is targeted because it is the only large appendage standing in the accelerated swirling slipstream, and it is already there and already structurally supported.

The swirling inflow meets the horizontal fin at an angle of attack, so the fin develops lift like any aerofoil. Because the fin lies flat, the resolved component of that lift points forward, and that is recovered thrust. The swirl reverses across the shaft line - one direction above, the other below - so the upper and lower fins must be set at opposite angles. A fin fitted the wrong way up is a drag device.

It is asymmetric above and below the propeller shaft line, not port and starboard: the section is twisted or offset so each half faces its own local inflow. Because the swirl arrives from opposite sides above and below the shaft, a symmetrical rudder carries an incident angle over its whole span even with the helm amidships. Twisting removes that built-in incidence, cutting induced drag, separation and rudder cavitation.

It is a freely rotating wheel of larger diameter than the propeller, connected to nothing. Its inner span lies inside the propeller race and is driven round as a turbine by the swirling flow; its outer span lies outside the race in undisturbed water and acts as a propeller, producing thrust. It converts wasted rotational energy into axial thrust and takes nothing from the engine.

No. All three are drawing on the same finite pool of wasted energy behind the propeller, so their gains are not additive - and three of the four devices in this family attack the same loss, rotational swirl. Only the bulb attacks a different one, the hub vortex, which is why it combines with the others better than they combine with each other. A combined arrangement must be designed and tested as one system.

Largely not, and the stem asks about devices currently in use, so say so. Claimed gains reached about 10 per cent, the highest of the four, and ships were fitted in the 1980s. The outer blade span is long, slender and lightly supported in a highly unsteady flow, and blade fatigue, blade loss and bearing problems on the free-running hub ended its use. It is examined because it shows the principle in its purest form.

Treat the figure as a model-scale or CFD result at design draught, trim and speed in calm water with a clean hull. Verify by continuous hull and propeller performance monitoring - shaft power against speed through water corrected for displacement, trim and weather - on ISO 19030, over long enough to separate the device's effect from fouling. Ask for full-scale before-and-after data from a sister ship in a comparable trade.

The 2023 IMO Strategy on Reduction of GHG Emissions from Ships, resolution MEPC.377(80), adopted at MEPC 80 on 7 July 2023 - one month earlier. NOT the Initial Strategy of 2018, which it superseded. The January 2023 sitting of this identical question cites the 2018 Strategy correctly for its own date, so this is a stale citation inherited from an earlier paper rather than a later one imported.

Quick revision
15-second recall
Three losses behind the propeller: swirl, hub vortex, rudder drag. The rudder is the only big thing standing in them. Fins - horizontal, lift from the swirl, forward component is thrust, HANDED. Asymmetric - twisted above and below the shaft because the swirl reverses; kills induced drag and cavitation. Bulb - fills the space behind the boss, suppresses the hub vortex, best retrofit. Vane wheel - turbine inboard, propeller outboard, no shaft power, ~10 per cent claimed but blade fatigue killed it. The gains do not add, and a dirty hull erases all of them.

Critical regulation
MARPOL Annex VI chapter 4 as revised by MEPC.328(76) - EEXI and the operational CII, applicable from 1 January 2023. If you remember one thing about the regulation here, remember that it is the commercial reason these devices are being fitted at all: a permanent efficiency gain improves the attained EEXI and helps the annual rating, which is worth more than the fuel bill alone.
Major trap
Citing the Initial IMO Strategy of 2018 as the current IMO strategy. It was superseded on 7 July 2023 by the 2023 IMO GHG Strategy, resolution MEPC.377(80) - one month before this sitting. Note the direction: the January 2023 sitting of this identical question cites the 2018 Strategy CORRECTLY for its own date, so the trap here is a stale citation from an EARLIER paper, not an imported one from a later paper.

Also on the platform: QP2308 Q6 - the power chain these devices act on, and where the propulsive coefficient sits in it · QP2308 Q1 - the monitoring that would tell you whether a claimed gain is actually being realised in service · QP2308 Q8 - the survey regime under which a retrofitted appendage and its attachment are examined

Repeated — same wording Returns in the same words from January 2023 Q1. Also set at April 2024 Q4, September 2024 Q9, June 2025 Q1.

Understand this first

A manned engine room is watched by a person, and a person is a remarkably good instrument. He hears a knock that was not there yesterday, smells a bearing before it is hot enough to alarm, sees a weep on a flange and notices that the note of a pump has changed. None of that is written down anywhere, and all of it is monitoring. When the space is left unattended, every bit of that disappears at once, and what you get in exchange is a machine that writes down a few hundred numbers, faithfully, at the same place and the same interval, for ever. So the trade is continuous human noticing swapped for continuous instrumental recording, and the whole of this question follows from that one exchange. The recording does not notice anything by itself - it just accumulates - so somebody has to go and look, deliberately and on a schedule, for the thing the watchkeeper would have spotted. That is why the answer is about selecting a small number of parameters rather than watching all of them, about comparing each reading with what that same machine did before rather than with a number in a book, and about acting when the direction changes rather than when a limit is crossed. Hold on to the sharpest version of it: an alarm is not the system working, it is the system telling you that you missed it - by the time a reading crosses a setpoint, the deterioration has already happened and the only choice left is how to stop safely. Everything else - the chart, the graphs, the statistics - is machinery for noticing early.

Model written answer

1. The position you are in, and what the question is asking

As Chief Engineer of a UMS ship with central scanning and data logging, you have more measurements than any engineer has ever had and fewer people looking at the machinery than any engineer has ever had. The question follows directly from that trade, and it asks three things: which data deserves special attention and why; a chart you would formulate for condition monitoring; and the computer tools you would use to present it graphically and to analyse the trend. Answer them in that order and answer all three - the chart and the tools are as markable as the data.

2. What a UMS ship gives you, and what it takes away

State this first, because every later choice is a consequence of it.

  • What it takes away is the watchkeeper's senses. A manned engine room is monitored continuously by a human being who hears a knock, smells a hot bearing, sees a weep and feels a change in vibration long before any transducer registers a number outside its limit. On a UMS ship nothing is observed between rounds except what is instrumented.
  • What it gives you is a continuous, time-stamped, objective record of every instrumented point, taken at the same place, in the same way, at the same interval, without transcription error and without the drift of judgement that affects a hand-written log.
  • The consequence, and it is the whole answer in one sentence: the ship has exchanged continuous human observation for continuous instrumental observation, so the value of the instrumental record has to be actively extracted - which means the Chief Engineer's job is no longer to read the alarm printout but to interrogate the log.

Two further consequences shape the chart. An alarm is a failure of monitoring, not a success of it - by the time a point crosses an alarm setpoint the deterioration is complete and the only decision left is how to stop safely. And the arrangements the ship carries because she is unattended - the bridge and engineers' alarms, the fire and bilge detection, the machinery and boiler safeguards required of a periodically unattended machinery space under SOLAS chapter II-1 part E - are themselves equipment whose availability must be monitored, because their failure removes the ship's only substitute for a watchkeeper.

3. The specific data that deserves special attention

The scanning system will log several hundred points. You cannot attend to all of them, and an answer that lists everything has made no engineering decision. Divide them into three groups by what the data is for, because that determines how it is treated.

Group A - slow-moving condition parameters. These change over weeks and months, they carry the deterioration signal, and they are the raw material of the chart. They matter as trends and are almost meaningless as single readings.

  • Main engine cylinder performance - maximum and compression pressure per unit, exhaust gas temperature per unit and, more usefully, the deviation of each unit from the mean of all units. The deviation is the diagnostic quantity: it removes load, ambient and fuel-quality effects that move every cylinder together, and it is what identifies the one unit that is different.
  • Turbocharger condition - speed at given load, air inlet and outlet temperatures, scavenge air pressure and temperature, and the pressure drop across the air cooler and across the air filter. A charger fouling and a cooler fouling look similar in one reading and quite different in a trend.
  • Specific fuel consumption at reference load, and the shaft power against speed relationship normalised for displacement, trim and weather, which separates hull and propeller fouling from engine deterioration.
  • Lubricating oil condition - viscosity, base number against acid number, water content, insolubles, particle count and elemental spectrometric analysis. A single oil result is a data point; three with a slope are a diagnosis.
  • Cylinder liner wear and cylinder oil feed rate, and the scrape-down oil iron content where sampled.
  • Bearing temperatures - main, crankpin where measured, thrust and the stern tube - and shaft earthing where fitted.
  • Vibration on auxiliary engines, pumps, purifiers, compressors and fans, taken at fixed points in a fixed manner.
  • Differential pressures across every filter, strainer, cooler and purifier - the cheapest and most under-used deterioration signal on the ship.
  • Boiler water chemistry, exhaust gas boiler differential pressure and soot deposition indicators, and feed and condensate condition.
  • Auxiliary engine running hours, load sharing and specific consumption, which show up an engine that is doing more work than its share for the same fuel.

Group B - fast-moving safety and integrity parameters. These may be perfectly normal until the moment they are not. They are watched for the event rather than for the trend, and they are the parameters on which the ship's unattended status actually depends.

  • Crankcase oil mist detector and bearing temperature high alarms, and scavenge space temperature - the three that precede a crankcase explosion or a scavenge fire.
  • Bilge level and bilge alarm status in every space, and any overboard discharge interlock.
  • Fire detection and the fixed extinguishing system's readiness.
  • Steering gear - hydraulic level, power availability and failure alarms.
  • Main engine safety system - slowdown and shutdown functions, and any override in force.
  • Electrical plant - generator availability, blackout and auto-start of the stand-by generator, preferential trips, and emergency generator readiness.
  • Starting air pressure and compressor availability, and control air.
  • The engineers' alarm, the dead-man alarm and the bridge alarm transfer - and the record that the duty engineer acknowledged, because an unacknowledged alarm on a UMS ship is a different event from an unacknowledged alarm on a manned one.

Group C - system-health parameters. Data about the monitoring itself, which nobody logs and which is the first thing to fail quietly.

  • Sensors in fault, bypassed, inhibited or reading a frozen value, and the record of who inhibited what and when. An inhibited channel on a UMS ship is a blind spot in the only observation the ship has.
  • Alarm frequency and repeat-alarm analysis. A point that alarms thirty times a month is telling you something whether or not it is within limits; and a rising alarm count is itself a deterioration signal.
  • Calibration status and due dates of the instruments the chart depends on.
  • Data gaps in the log - periods where the scanner recorded nothing are periods where the ship was unobserved.

4. Telling a trend from noise

Before any chart is drawn, four disciplines have to be imposed, and stating them is what shows the examiner that you understand why most shipboard trending is worthless.

  1. Fix the operating condition. A parameter is only comparable with itself if it is taken at the same load, the same fuel, comparable ambient and comparable sea conditions. Either record at a defined reference condition - for example a fixed percentage of MCR at steady state - or normalise the reading to reference conditions before plotting it. Unnormalised data is the commonest reason a genuine trend is invisible.
  2. Fix the method. Same instrument, same measurement point, same mounting, same operator procedure. A vibration reading taken 50 mm from where the last one was taken is a different reading.
  3. Establish the baseline. The ship's own healthy values after commissioning, or after the last overhaul, at the reference condition - not the maker's shop-test figures. Without a baseline there is no such thing as an abnormal reading, which is why the first weeks of any monitoring regime produce nothing.
  4. Distinguish three limits. The baseline, the alert limit at which you begin to plan, and the alarm or safe limit at which the machine must be stopped. The alert limit is derived from the parameter's own scatter about the baseline; the alarm limit comes from the maker. Confusing the two is why alert limits are usually set uselessly close to alarm limits.

5. Formulating the condition monitoring chart

The stem says formulate a chart, so give one, and give it a structure that could actually be issued as a shipboard document. Each row is one monitored parameter, and each row carries ten fields — parameter, measurement point and method, frequency, reference condition, baseline, alert, alarm or safe limit, trend rule, action on alert, and record. On a ship's own landscape form those are ten separate columns; below they are grouped three to a column so that the chart stays readable on a page. This is a specimen extract; the full chart would run to several pages.

Parameter, point and frequencyReference condition, baseline and alertAlarm, trend rule, action and record
M/E exhaust temp deviation from the mean of all units. Scanner channel per unit. Continuous, logged 4-hourlyAt 75 % MCR steady, normalised for ambient. Baseline is the ship's own post-overhaul value. Alert on a deviation beyond the normal spread of the unitsAlarm is the maker's high-temperature setpoint. Act on a rise over 3 consecutive logs: check fuel pump timing, injector and exhaust valve, and inspect through the scavenge ports. Record as a PMS job
Cylinder Pmax and Pcomp per unit. Indicator or online pressure sensor. WeeklyAt fixed load, steady state. Baseline is the post-overhaul indicator card. Alert on falling Pcomp, or on the Pmax spread across units wideningAlarm is the maker's limit. Act on two successive sets moving the same way: check valve leakage, ring condition and fuel timing. File the indicator card set
Turbocharger air cooler differential pressure. DP across the cooler. DailyAt fixed load. Baseline is the clean-cooler value. Alert on a rise beyond the normal scatterAlarm is the maker's limit. Act on a steady rise over 2 weeks: plan air-side cleaning at the next opportunity. Record in the log and the PMS
Shaft power at reference speed. Torsionmeter. Daily at noonNormalised for displacement, trim and weather. Baseline is the post-docking clean-hull value. Alert when power demand exceeds baseline at the same speedNo safety alarm; this one is commercial. Act on a sustained rise over 30 days: assess hull and propeller cleaning. Record in the performance report
Lubricating oil base number against acid number. Sample to a shore laboratory. MonthlyIn normal service. Baseline is the fresh charge value. Alert on approaching the condemning rangeAlarm is the maker's condemning limit. Act on a slope across 3 consecutive samples: increase the renewal rate and investigate fuel sulphur and water ingress. File the laboratory report
Lubricating oil wear metals - iron, copper, chromium, lead. Spectrometric analysis. MonthlyIn normal service. Baseline is the established running value. Alert on any element rising against its own historyNo limit; this is diagnostic. Act on the rate of rise rather than the absolute value: identify the component the element points to. File the laboratory report
Auxiliary engine and pump vibration, overall level and spectrum. Fixed points, portable analyser. MonthlyAt rated load, steady. Baseline is the post-overhaul signature. Alert on a rising overall level, or on a new frequency component appearingAlarm is the maker's or the standard limit. Act on a rise over 2 readings or on any new component: diagnose from the spectrum and plan bearing renewal. Store the signature
Main and thrust bearing temperatures. Scanner channels. ContinuousAt fixed load. Baseline is the ship's own normal. Alert on a rise relative to the other bearings and to sea temperatureAlarm is the maker's high alarm and slowdown. Act on divergence from the group of bearings: reduce load, investigate and prepare to stop. Record in the alarm log
Fuel oil filter differential pressure. DP across the filter. DailyIn normal service. Baseline is the clean-element value. Alert on a rise beyond the normal scatterAlarm is the changeover DP. Act on a shortening interval between cleanings: investigate fuel quality and purifier performance. Record in the log
Purifier back-pressure and sludge discharge quantity. Gauge and discharge count. DailyAt normal throughput. Baseline is the commissioning value. Alert on any change against baselineAlarm is the maker's limit. Act on a change over one week: check the bowl, the gravity disc, the throughput and the temperature. Record in the log
Cylinder liner wear. Measured at overhaul, with scrape-down iron monthlyNo reference condition. Baseline is the as-new dimension. Alert when the wear rate exceeds the established rate per 1000 hoursAlarm is the maker's condemning wear. Act on the rate rather than the accumulated wear: review the cylinder oil feed rate and the base number grade. Record on the wear card
Alarm frequency and repeat alarms. Alarm history extract. WeeklyNo reference condition. Baseline is the established normal count. Alert on any channel repeating abnormallyNo limit. Act on a rising count on any channel: investigate the plant, and do NOT raise the setpoint. Record in the weekly review minute
Inhibited, bypassed or faulty channels. Scanner status report. Daily and at every handoverNo reference condition. Baseline is zero. Alert on any channel inhibited without a raised defectNo limit. Act on any inhibit older than its authorised period: restore it, or record it with an authority and a time limit. Record in the inhibit register

Four things make this a chart and not a list, and each should be said in a sentence because each is a mark.

  • Every row carries its reference condition and its baseline, so the reading is comparable with itself. A chart without those two fields produces numbers nobody can interpret.
  • Alert and alarm are separate fields. The alert is yours and is the one that creates work; the alarm is the maker's and is the one that stops the engine.
  • Every row has a trend rule, expressed as a number of consecutive readings or a rate, so that acting on a trend is a defined event and not a matter of opinion.
  • Every row has an action and a record, so the chart connects to the planned maintenance system and to the safety management system rather than sitting beside them. Under the ISM Code's maintenance element the ship must inspect at appropriate intervals, report and correct non-conformity with its possible cause, keep records, and identify the equipment whose sudden failure may result in a hazardous situation - and the chart is how that is done on a UMS ship.

6. The computer tools for graphical representation

Answer this limb by what graphical form answers what question, not by naming software - a product list dates immediately and does not show engineering judgement. The tools sit in three layers: the scanning system's own historian, a general-purpose spreadsheet for the analysis you actually do at sea, and the planned maintenance and performance software that holds the history.

  • Time-series line chart with the parameter on the vertical axis and date on the horizontal, and the baseline, alert and alarm limits drawn as horizontal reference lines. This is the workhorse: it shows position and direction in one picture and it is the only chart most parameters need.
  • Line chart with a fitted trend line and a moving average. The moving average suppresses scatter; the fitted line gives a slope, and the slope extrapolated to the alert or alarm line gives an estimated time to intervention - which is the number that lets you choose the port.
  • Multi-series line or column chart for per-unit comparison - all cylinder exhaust deviations on one chart, all bearing temperatures on one chart. The outlier is visible instantly and no calculation is needed.
  • Scatter plot with a regression for one parameter against another - shaft power against speed, exhaust temperature against load - which is how a reading is normalised and how a cause is separated from a symptom.
  • Control chart, plotting individual readings with the mean and control limits derived from the parameter's own scatter. Its value is that it flags a run of points on one side of the mean as a signal even though no single point has crossed a limit - which is precisely the case a limit-based alarm cannot detect.
  • Radar or spider chart for a multi-parameter snapshot of one machine against its own baseline profile, which is a good handover and superintendent's document.
  • Bar chart and Pareto for alarm counts by channel and for defects by system, which directs effort at the few causes producing most of the events.
  • Histogram for the distribution of a parameter, which shows whether scatter has widened even when the mean has not moved - often the first sign of an instability.
  • Vibration spectrum and waterfall plot - amplitude against frequency, and successive spectra stacked against time - which is the only presentation that identifies which component is deteriorating rather than merely that something is.
  • Conditional formatting and exception reporting, colouring cells that breach an alert and producing a one-page exception list. On a plant with several hundred points this is what makes a daily review possible at all.

Two practical points earn marks here. Automate the extraction: the scanner's log should be exported to the spreadsheet on a schedule rather than transcribed by hand, because manual transcription introduces error and stops the moment the ship gets busy. And pivot tables and lookup functions are what turn a raw export of several hundred thousand rows into the twenty charts that matter - which is a more useful skill for the engineer than any specialist package.

7. Trend analysis proper

Graphing is presentation; trend analysis is the interpretation, and the stem asks for both. Six methods, in the order you would apply them.

  1. Normalisation. Correct the reading to the reference condition before anything else. This is the step that is usually omitted and it invalidates everything after it.
  2. Smoothing. A moving average over an appropriate window separates the signal from the scatter. Choose the window from how fast the mechanism actually deteriorates - too short and you chase noise, too long and you hide the event.
  3. Slope and rate of change. Fit a line and read the gradient. The rate matters more than the value: a wear metal at half the condemning limit and rising fast is a more urgent problem than one just below it and static for a year.
  4. Extrapolation to the limit. Project the slope to the alert and alarm lines to estimate remaining useful life and therefore the intervention window. State the estimate with its uncertainty - it assumes the deterioration mechanism does not change, which is exactly what fails when a bearing begins to pick up.
  5. Correlation and cross-checking. No single parameter should decide an intervention. A rising bearing temperature with rising vibration, rising oil iron and a falling oil film pressure is a diagnosis; any one of them alone is a suspicion. Correlating parameters is also how a sensor fault is distinguished from a plant fault - a genuine deterioration shows in more than one place.
  6. Comparison. Against the ship's own history, against the sister ship, and against the maker's expected values. The sister-ship comparison is the strongest evidence available at sea and costs nothing.

Two failure modes should be named because the examiner will be listening for them. Do not adjust the baseline to accommodate deterioration - resetting the reference because the readings have drifted is the mechanism by which a plant declines invisibly for years. And do not raise an alarm setpoint to stop a nuisance alarm; investigate what the alarm is telling you and, if the setpoint really is wrong, change it through the management-of-change process with a recorded technical justification.

8. What you do with what the trend tells you

Close on the management content, because the paper is Engineering Management and the data is worth nothing until it changes a decision.

  • Convert the alert into a planned job in the PMS, with the spare identified, the port chosen and the manpower planned. A trend that generates no work order has achieved nothing.
  • Review formally and on a fixed cycle - a weekly engineers' review of the exception list and the alarm history, minuted, with actions owned. On a UMS ship this meeting replaces the watchkeeper's continuous observation and should be treated with the same seriousness.
  • Report to the office in the same format every month, so that a superintendent and a sister ship can be compared without re-working the data.
  • Feed back what was actually found on opening up. If the trend said a bearing was wiping and it was not, the interpretation was wrong and the limit or the method needs changing. A monitoring regime that is never corrected by findings becomes superstition.
  • Use the record in the survey. Recorded condition data accepted by the society can support a machinery planned maintenance scheme in place of an opened-up examination, so the chart has a value in the survey regime and not only in the maintenance budget.
  • Maintain the instrumentation as machinery. Calibration, correct siting and prompt repair of failed transducers are planned maintenance items. A neglected sensor is worse than no sensor, because it is believed.
  • Keep the manual competence alive. Indicator cards, sounding rods, a hand on a bearing and a look at the exhaust are the fallback when the system is down and the check on it when it is not.

End on the proposition that holds the whole answer together: on a UMS ship the log is the watchkeeper. Its value is not in the readings it contains but in the trends somebody extracts from it, and an alarm is the evidence that nobody did.

Study guide

Why this structure scores

The stem is one unbroken paragraph with no limb labels, which is exactly why candidates lose marks on it - they write a general essay on condition monitoring and never produce the chart or the tools. The structure above splits the paragraph into its three actual tasks and signposts them: sections 2 and 3 answer 'the specific data', section 5 answers 'formulate a chart', sections 6 and 7 answer 'the tools for graphical representation and trend analysis'. A marker can find all three.

Section 3's division into three groups is the piece of thinking the question is really testing. A scanning system logs hundreds of points and an answer that lists them has made no decision. Grouping them by what the data is FOR - slow condition data that matters as a trend, fast safety data that matters as an event, and system-health data about the monitoring itself - shows the examiner a Chief Engineer's judgement rather than a memorised list. The third group in particular is what separates a strong answer: almost nobody mentions inhibited channels, alarm frequency or data gaps, and on a UMS ship they are the blind spots that matter most.

Section 4 exists because most shipboard trending is worthless for reasons that have nothing to do with the software. Unnormalised readings, an inconsistent method and no baseline will defeat any analysis, and putting those four disciplines before the chart makes the chart credible.

Common mistakes

  • Never producing a chart. The stem says FORMULATE a chart and it is one of three tasks. An answer without a tabulated chart has abandoned a third of the marks.
  • Listing every point the scanner logs. That is not special attention, it is a screenshot. Select, and say why you selected.
  • Naming software products instead of graphical forms. The question asks for tools in computer application for graphical representation and trend analysis - answer with what chart answers what question.
  • Treating exhaust temperature as an absolute value. The diagnostic quantity is the DEVIATION of each unit from the mean of all units, which removes load, ambient and fuel effects.
  • Forgetting normalisation. A parameter compared across different loads, drafts and sea states is not being compared at all.
  • Confusing the alert limit with the alarm limit. The alert comes from the parameter's own scatter and creates work; the alarm comes from the maker and stops the engine.
  • Deciding on one parameter. Correlate - rising temperature plus rising vibration plus rising wear metal is a diagnosis; one of them alone is a suspicion.
  • Omitting the UMS-specific dimension entirely and writing a generic condition monitoring answer. The question is set on a UMS ship for a reason.

Examiner traps

  • An alarm is a failure of monitoring, not a success. By the time a point crosses its alarm setpoint the deterioration is complete. Say this and the examiner knows you understand what the log is for.
  • The rate of change matters more than the value. A wear metal at half the limit rising fast is more urgent than one just below the limit and static. Expect to be asked exactly this.
  • Never move the baseline to accommodate deterioration, and never raise an alarm setpoint to silence a nuisance alarm. Both are the classic ways a plant declines invisibly, and both are management-of-change failures.
  • An inhibited channel is a blind spot. On a UMS ship inhibiting a sensor removes the only observation the ship has of that point. It must be registered, authorised and time-limited.
  • A neglected sensor is worse than no sensor, because it is believed. Calibration is a planned maintenance item.
  • SOLAS chapter II-1 part E is the regulatory basis for a periodically unattended machinery space - the alarms, detection and safeguards that let the space be left unmanned. Do not attribute the UMS arrangements to the ISM Code; ISM element 10 is the maintenance obligation, which is a different thing.
  • Condition data can support a planned maintenance scheme in place of an opened-up survey - but only where the society has accepted the scheme. It is not automatic.

Likely oral follow-up

  • Your scanner logs six hundred points. Which twenty do you actually look at, and why those?
  • Why is exhaust temperature deviation more useful than exhaust temperature?
  • What is the difference between your alert limit and the alarm setpoint, and who sets each?
  • A bearing temperature has risen four degrees over three weeks and is well inside the alarm. What do you do?
  • The oil analysis shows iron rising steeply but still at half the condemning limit. Urgent or not?
  • One channel alarms twenty times a week. What do you do about it - and what do you NOT do?
  • How would you normalise shaft power so that you can compare last week with last year?
  • You open up the bearing the trend condemned and it is perfect. What now?
  • Show me how a chart entry becomes a job in the PMS.
  • The duty engineer inhibited a bilge alarm during a repair and went off watch. What is your position?

Memory framework

The route is POSITION - TRADE-OFF - DATA - DISCIPLINE - CHART - GRAPHS - TREND - ACTION. For the data hold three groups: SLOW CONDITION / FAST SAFETY / SYSTEM HEALTH. For the disciplines hold CONDITION - METHOD - BASELINE - LIMITS. For the chart fields hold PARAMETER - POINT - FREQUENCY - REFERENCE - BASELINE - ALERT - ALARM - RULE - ACTION - RECORD. For trend analysis hold NORMALISE - SMOOTH - SLOPE - EXTRAPOLATE - CORRELATE - COMPARE. And hold the closing line: on a UMS ship the log is the watchkeeper.

Regulation and source map

  • SOLAS chapter II-1 part E - periodically unattended machinery spaces: fire and bilge detection and alarm, machinery and boiler safeguards, bridge and engineers' alarm arrangements, communications, and the requirement that the ship be as safe in all sailing conditions as one with the machinery space manned.
  • ISM Code element 10 - maintenance of the ship and equipment: inspection at appropriate intervals, reporting and correcting non-conformity with its possible cause, records, identification of equipment whose sudden operational failure may result in a hazardous situation, and regular testing of stand-by arrangements not in continuous use.
  • Classification society unattended machinery space notation, and the survey arrangements under which recorded condition data may support a machinery planned maintenance scheme. Described by function; no rule number is held or cited.
  • Statistical process control - individual and moving-range charts, control limits from the process's own variability, and the run rule. Industrial practice, not a maritime instrument.
  • Lubricating oil condition analysis - elemental spectrometric analysis, infrared spectroscopy, particle count, base number against acid number.
  • Hull and propeller performance normalisation - shaft power against speed corrected for displacement, trim and weather.

Uncertainty and evidence limits

  • Every number in the chart is an example, not an authority. Alert and alarm limits belong to a particular engine, a particular maker's recommendation and a particular ship's baseline. The chart's STRUCTURE is the answer to the question; the figures illustrate it.
  • No maker's sampling interval or condemning limit is quoted. These differ between makers and engine types, and the answer directs the candidate to the manual.
  • we hold SOLAS at citation level and not as quotable text. Chapter II-1 part E is cited by identity and subject; no regulation below part level is asserted and nothing is quoted.
  • No class rule number is cited for the unattended notation or for the planned maintenance scheme survey arrangement. Both are described by function.
  • No software product is recommended. Tools are given by function because a product list dates immediately and answers nothing.
  • No earlier solved paper we hold sets this task. The engineering content is authored fresh from practice and corroborated against our own verified records for lubricating oil analysis, hull performance, preventive maintenance and data architecture. The grouping of parameters and the chart's field structure is the author's design and are recorded as engineering judgement.
Recall the structure

Before you read the answer: can you name all 8 sections, in order? Say them or write them down, then reveal.

  1. __________The position you are in, and what the question is asking
  2. __________What a UMS ship gives you, and what it takes away
  3. __________The specific data that deserves special attention
  4. __________Telling a trend from noise
  5. __________Formulating the condition monitoring chart
  6. __________The computer tools for graphical representation
  7. __________Trend analysis proper
  8. __________What you do with what the trend tells you
Flashcards 10

1 the position you are in and the three tasks; 2 what UMS gives and takes away; 3 the data in three groups; 4 the four disciplines that make a trend readable; 5 the condition monitoring chart; 6 the graphical tools; 7 trend analysis methods; 8 what you do with the result. Sections 2-3 answer the data limb, 5 answers the chart limb, 6-7 answer the computer tools limb.

By what the data is for. Group A slow condition parameters, which matter as trends and carry the deterioration signal. Group B fast safety parameters, which matter as events and on which the unattended status depends. Group C system-health parameters - inhibited channels, alarm frequency, calibration, data gaps - which are data about the monitoring itself. Grouping by purpose is what turns a list of hundreds of points into a decision.

Parameter; measurement point and method; frequency; reference condition; baseline; alert limit; alarm or safe limit; trend rule; action on alert; record. Reference condition and baseline make the reading interpretable; alert and alarm are separate because one creates work and the other stops the engine; the trend rule makes acting a defined event; action and record connect the chart to the PMS.

The alert limit is yours, derived from the parameter's own scatter about the ship's baseline, and crossing it creates planning and investigation. The alarm or safe limit is the maker's, derived from what the machine can withstand, and crossing it stops or slows the engine. Confusing them is why alert limits are usually set uselessly close to alarms, leaving no time to plan anything.

Fix the operating condition, or normalise the reading to a reference condition. Fix the method - same instrument, same point, same mounting, same procedure. Establish the ship's own baseline after commissioning or overhaul, not the maker's shop-test figure. And distinguish three limits: baseline, alert from the parameter's scatter, alarm from the maker. Unnormalised data is the commonest reason a genuine trend is invisible.

Normalise to the reference condition; smooth with a moving average whose window matches how fast the mechanism deteriorates; fit a slope and read the rate of change; extrapolate to the alert and alarm lines for remaining useful life; correlate across parameters, which also distinguishes a sensor fault from a plant fault; and compare against own history, sister ship and the maker's expectation.

Because load, ambient temperature and fuel quality move every cylinder together, so an absolute temperature tells you mostly about the operating point. The deviation of each unit from the mean of all units cancels those common effects and isolates the one unit that is behaving differently - which is the unit with the fuel pump, injector or exhaust valve defect.

Investigate what the alarm is telling you about the plant, because a rising alarm count is itself a deterioration signal. Do NOT raise the setpoint to silence it, and do not inhibit it and forget. If the setpoint is genuinely wrong, change it through management of change with a recorded technical justification. An inhibited channel on a UMS ship is a blind spot in the only observation the ship has.

Record the finding and correct the interpretation - the limit, the measurement method or the diagnosis was wrong, and one of them must change. Feeding back what was actually found on opening up is what keeps a monitoring regime honest; a system never corrected by findings degrades into superstition, and the crew stop believing it long before the office does.

SOLAS chapter II-1 part E governs periodically unattended machinery spaces - the fire and bilge detection and alarm, the machinery and boiler safeguards, and the bridge and engineers' alarm arrangements that substitute for a watchkeeper. ISM Code element 10 is separate: maintenance of the ship and equipment, inspection at intervals, records, and identification of equipment whose sudden failure may be hazardous.

Quick revision
15-second recall
On a UMS ship the log is the watchkeeper. Select data in three groups: slow condition (exhaust DEVIATION per unit, Pmax, cooler DP, normalised shaft power, oil wear metals, vibration, filter DPs), fast safety (oil mist, bilge, fire, steering, safety system, electrical), system health (inhibited channels, alarm frequency, calibration, data gaps). Before plotting: normalise, fix method, baseline, three limits. Chart columns: parameter, point, frequency, reference, baseline, alert, alarm, trend rule, action, record. Trend: normalise - smooth - slope - extrapolate - correlate - compare. An alarm means you missed it.
