Critical regulation
SOLAS chapter II-1 part E - periodically unattended machinery spaces. If you remember one regulatory point, remember that it is part E which permits the space to be unmanned, by requiring the detection, alarm and safeguard arrangements that substitute for the watchkeeper - and that ISM Code element 10 is a separate obligation, about maintaining the ship and identifying the equipment whose sudden failure would be hazardous.
Major trap
Treating an alarm as the monitoring system working. It is the opposite: by the time a reading crosses its setpoint the deterioration is complete and the only decision left is how to stop safely. The related traps are moving the baseline to accommodate a drifting plant, and raising a setpoint to silence a nuisance alarm - both hide deterioration rather than manage it, and both are management-of-change failures.

Also on the platform: QP2308 Q1 - the same data seen from the shore: what connectivity and analytics add to what the ship already logs · QP2308 Q8 - the survey regime, and the condition data that can be accepted in support of a planned maintenance scheme · QP2308 Q6 - the engine performance parameters whose trend this chart is tracking, and where they sit in the power chain

Once in this set Set once in the sittings MIW has transcribed.

Understand this first

A flag State promises the rest of the world that its ships meet the international rules, and then has to make that good on ships scattered across the planet that hardly ever come home. Almost no administration has surveyors everywhere its ships trade, so it hires organisations that already do - the classification societies, which have a global surveying network built for their own commercial work. And that is the point to hold on to: the society is doing somebody else's job, with somebody else's authority, and the certificate it hands over has somebody else's name on it. Every limit on what it may do follows from that one sentence. It cannot take on the responsibility, because the promise was made between governments and a government cannot discharge a promise by hiring a contractor. It cannot decide for itself what it is allowed to do, because the permission was written down job by job by the State that gave it. It cannot let a ship off a requirement or accept something different instead, because changing the rules is an act of the State that agreed them - the surveyor can recommend, but only the flag can decide. It cannot detain anybody, because seizing property is a power of government, not of a company; all it can do is refuse the certificate and tell the flag. And underneath all of it sits an uncomfortable fact the rules are mostly built around: the organisation doing the checking is paid by the owner being checked, and can be replaced by him. Notice too that the same society is wearing two hats. When it surveys under its own rules for the owner, that is its own private business and nothing to do with any government. When it surveys under a convention for the flag, it is borrowing public authority. The same surveyor may swap between the two in a single afternoon, and telling the two apart is most of this question.

Knowledge map
Limitations of Recognized Organizations, and the Statutory Services Delegated
  1. 1The boundary the question turns on
    • Classification is the society's own work, under its own rules, for the owner, by contract
    • Statutory work is the flag State's work, under convention and national law, done on delegation
    • The society wears the second hat only where a flag delegated, only to that extent, and only for that flag's ships
  2. 2Limitation one - the function is delegated, the responsibility is not
    • The RO surveys and certifies; the Administration remains responsible for the ship's statutory compliance
    • REASON: the convention obligation runs between Contracting Governments, and a treaty duty cannot be discharged by contracting with a company
    • No other Government has any treaty relationship with the society
  3. 3Limitation two - the authority is specific, never general
    • Only what the instrument of authorization confers, function by function and convention by convention, in a formal written agreement
    • REASON one - capability: the Code forbids authorizing beyond capability, assessed by elementary module
    • REASON two - transparency: the list of ROs and the conditions of delegated authority are deposited with the Secretary-General so a port State knows what a certificate represents
  4. 4Limitation three - the RO cannot make, waive or dispense with the law
    • Exemptions and Exemption Certificates are the Administration's
    • Equivalents are the Administration's, and must be communicated to the Organization
    • Interpretation of national law is the flag's; the RO refers and does not decide
  5. 5Limitation four - independence, and the conflict the scheme is built around
    • Independence, a code of ethics, and no activity conflicting with independence
    • May not certify as flag State what it designed, built, supplied or maintains
    • Quality system certified by a body accredited independently of the ROs AND their associations
  6. 6Limitation five - the RO has no enforcement power
    • It cannot detain, prosecute, penalise or compel
    • It may refuse, suspend or withdraw a certificate within its authorization, impose conditions, and report
    • REASON: detention and prosecution are exercises of state power over persons and property, not transferable by contract
  7. 7Limitation six - practical and jurisdictional limits
    • A survey is a sample at a moment in time - compliance AS FOUND, not a guarantee for the certificate's life
    • The RO depends on the drawings submitted, the access given and the records produced; it has no power to compel disclosure
    • Recognition is not authorization, and neither is universal
  8. 8The statutory services undertaken on behalf of the Administration
    • The enabling provisions: SOLAS I/6, Load Lines article 13, MARPOL Annex I regulation 6 and Annex II regulation 8, Tonnage article 6

Cover48 core points beneath them

Memorise the 8 headings. The core points are there to help you check whether an answer is too thin — they are not a mark scheme, and they are not 48 separate facts to learn individually.

Show the core points to cover
1. The boundary the question turns on
  • Classification is the society's own work, under its own rules, for the owner, by contract
  • Statutory work is the flag State's work, under convention and national law, done on delegation
  • The society wears the second hat only where a flag delegated, only to that extent, and only for that flag's ships
  • The RO Code - MSC.349(92) and MEPC.237(65), mandatory 1 January 2015 via SOLAS XI-1/1
  • Parts 1 and 2 mandatory, Part 3 recommendatory; recognition and authorization are separate acts
2. Limitation one - the function is delegated, the responsibility is not
  • The RO surveys and certifies; the Administration remains responsible for the ship's statutory compliance
  • REASON: the convention obligation runs between Contracting Governments, and a treaty duty cannot be discharged by contracting with a company
  • No other Government has any treaty relationship with the society
  • CONSEQUENCE: the flag must run an oversight programme, may inspect its own ships additionally, and is itself audited under the III Code and the Member State Audit Scheme
3. Limitation two - the authority is specific, never general
  • Only what the instrument of authorization confers, function by function and convention by convention, in a formal written agreement
  • REASON one - capability: the Code forbids authorizing beyond capability, assessed by elementary module
  • REASON two - transparency: the list of ROs and the conditions of delegated authority are deposited with the Secretary-General so a port State knows what a certificate represents
  • REASON three - controllability: a specified scope can be narrowed as easily as withdrawn
  • Flag by flag; may not survey a ship not entitled to that flag; must cover withdrawal and cancellation; time-limited and revocable
4. Limitation three - the RO cannot make, waive or dispense with the law
  • Exemptions and Exemption Certificates are the Administration's
  • Equivalents are the Administration's, and must be communicated to the Organization
  • Interpretation of national law is the flag's; the RO refers and does not decide
  • Class rules are not statutory requirements and cannot discharge a convention obligation
  • REASON: these are sovereign acts altering the bargain struck with other Contracting Governments
  • Delegating survey delegates the finding of FACT; delegating exemption would delegate the LAW
  • In practice: the RO recommends, the Administration decides
5. Limitation four - independence, and the conflict the scheme is built around
  • Independence, a code of ethics, and no activity conflicting with independence
  • May not certify as flag State what it designed, built, supplied or maintains
  • Quality system certified by a body accredited independently of the ROs AND their associations
  • REASON: the organization surveying the ship is paid by the owner of it and competes for his business
  • The dual capacity is itself a conflict to be managed - class and flag State in one visit
  • Commercial pressure is a real limit: an owner can change class, which is why flag oversight and port State control matter
6. Limitation five - the RO has no enforcement power
  • It cannot detain, prosecute, penalise or compel
  • It may refuse, suspend or withdraw a certificate within its authorization, impose conditions, and report
  • REASON: detention and prosecution are exercises of state power over persons and property, not transferable by contract
  • The counterpart is the reporting duty - withdrawal or suspension of class, unmet conditions, deficiencies
  • Co-operation with port States in rectifying reported deficiencies
  • A failure to report is a compliance failure, not merely poor service
7. Limitation six - practical and jurisdictional limits
  • A survey is a sample at a moment in time - compliance AS FOUND, not a guarantee for the certificate's life
  • The RO depends on the drawings submitted, the access given and the records produced; it has no power to compel disclosure
  • Recognition is not authorization, and neither is universal
  • Subcontracting of statutory work is constrained; work is by the RO's own exclusive surveyors under its control
  • Some services are reserved to the Administration and are not delegable at all
  • The RO cannot bind the flag State in its relations with other Governments
8. The statutory services undertaken on behalf of the Administration
  • The enabling provisions: SOLAS I/6, Load Lines article 13, MARPOL Annex I regulation 6 and Annex II regulation 8, Tonnage article 6
  • SOLAS - construction, equipment and radio surveys and certificates; DG document; Certificates of Fitness; ISM and ISPS where delegated; stability, cargo securing and ESP approvals
  • Load Lines - freeboard assignment and the International Load Line Certificate
  • Tonnage 1969 - measurement and the International Tonnage Certificate
  • MARPOL - IOPP, NLS, ISPP, IAPP, EIAPP and IEE certificates, and the associated plan and manual approvals
  • AFS, BWM, MLC and Polar certificates; plus type approval, welder qualification, inclining experiment and plan approval
  • NOT delegated: registry, Minimum Safe Manning Document, STCW seafarer certification, exemptions and equivalents, DMLC Part I, CLC and Bunkers financial-security certificates, casualty investigation, detention and prosecution
  • And class survey and condition of class are the society's OWN work, not statutory services at all
  • Which services a flag actually delegates is that flag's choice, recorded in its authorization and deposited with the Secretary-General
Model written answer

1. The boundary the question turns on

The stem asserts that classification societies act as Recognized Organizations and play an important part in implementing national and international regulation, and then asks two things: what the limits of that role are, with reasons, and what statutory services a classification body actually performs on the Administration's behalf.

Both halves turn on one boundary, and stating it first makes the whole answer coherent: a classification society does two entirely different kinds of work, under two entirely different authorities.

  • Classification is the society's own business, done under its own rules, for the owner who pays it, under a contract. Class rules are private standards. Condition of class, notations, periodical class surveys and the class certificate all belong here.
  • Statutory work is the flag State's business, done under convention and national law, on the Administration's behalf, because the Administration has delegated it. The certificates carry the flag State's name and authority, not the society's.

The society wears the second hat only where a flag State has handed it over, only to the extent handed over, and only for that flag's ships. Every limitation in this answer is a consequence of the statutory hat being borrowed rather than owned.

The instrument governing the arrangement is the Code for Recognized Organizations, adopted by resolutions MSC.349(92) of 21 June 2013 and MEPC.237(65), and made mandatory from 1 January 2015 through amendments to SOLAS, MARPOL Annexes I and II and the 1988 Load Line Protocol - the SOLAS hook being regulation XI-1/1. Parts 1 and 2 of the Code are mandatory; Part 3, the flag State oversight guidelines, is recommendatory. Note also the distinction the Code is built on and which candidates blur: recognition is the assessment of an organization against the Code, and authorization is the separate, later act of delegating specified functions. An organization can be recognized and still not authorized for a particular ship type or convention.

2. Limitation one - the function is delegated, the responsibility is not

The limitation. The RO performs the survey and issues the certificate, but the Administration remains responsible for the statutory certification of its ships and for the ship's compliance. The RO cannot assume that responsibility and the flag State cannot shed it.

The reason. The obligation created by a convention is an obligation between Contracting Governments. A flag State promises the other parties that ships flying its flag comply; it cannot discharge a treaty obligation by contracting with a private company, and no other Government has any treaty relationship with the society. So when a ship is detained abroad, or a casualty is investigated, or the flag State's performance is audited, it is the flag State that answers - and it may not answer by pointing at its RO.

The consequence in practice. The Administration must run an oversight programme over its ROs; it retains the power to conduct its own additional inspections of ships its ROs have certified; and it can be, and is, audited on exactly this under the III Code and the IMO Member State Audit Scheme.

3. Limitation two - the authority is specific, never general

The limitation. An RO may do only what the instrument of authorization says it may do. The Code requires the flag State to specify the functions conferred, function by function and convention by convention, and to record them in a formal written agreement. Anything not conferred has not been conferred.

The reasons, and there are three worth separating.

  • Capability. The Code expressly forbids a flag State to authorize functions beyond the RO's capabilities, and an appendix breaks the survey and certification work into elementary modules precisely so that capability can be assessed module by module rather than once and for all. A society competent in ship structure is not thereby competent in gas carrier containment systems.
  • Transparency to third parties. A port State inspecting a foreign ship has to know what the certificate in front of it represents. That is why the flag State must deposit with the Secretary-General a list of its ROs together with the specific responsibilities and conditions of the delegated authority. A general authorization to 'act on our behalf' tells a port State nothing.
  • Controllability. A scope that is specified can be narrowed - by ship type, by convention, by function - as easily as it can be suspended or withdrawn. A general authorization can only be revoked entirely, which is a blunt instrument no Administration wants to use.

Three practical corollaries. The authority is flag by flag: the same society may be an RO for one flag and not another, with different scopes from each, and it may not survey a ship not entitled to fly the flag that authorized it. The authorization must cover the unglamorous end - corrective action and the withdrawal or cancellation of certificates - and a flag State that has not said who may withdraw a certificate has not finished authorizing. And the authority may be time-limited and is always revocable.

4. Limitation three - the RO cannot make, waive or dispense with the law

The limitation. This is the sharpest limitation of the six and the one candidates most often miss. An RO applies the requirements; it cannot create them, relax them or excuse a ship from them.

  • Exemptions - the power to exempt a ship from a convention requirement - belong to the Administration. So does the issue of an Exemption Certificate.
  • Equivalents - the acceptance of a fitting, material, appliance or arrangement other than that required, on the basis that it is at least as effective - is likewise the Administration's decision, and the Administration must communicate the particulars to the Organization.
  • Interpretation of national law is the flag State's. Where a national requirement goes beyond or differs from the convention, the RO must apply the flag's interpretation and, in doubt, refer the matter rather than decide it. The written agreement is required to deal expressly with interpretations, deviations and equivalent solutions.
  • Class rules are not statutory requirements. An RO cannot satisfy a convention requirement by pointing to compliance with its own rules, and it cannot amend its rules so as to change what a convention demands.

The reason. These are sovereign acts. Exempting a ship, or accepting something different from what a treaty requires, alters the bargain the flag State struck with the other Contracting Governments, and only the party to that bargain can alter it. Delegating survey work is delegating the finding of fact; delegating exemption would be delegating the law.

The practical form of this limitation is worth stating because it is what an engineer actually meets: the RO may recommend, and the Administration decides. A surveyor faced with an arrangement that does not meet the letter of the rule refers it to the flag, with a recommendation, and waits.

5. Limitation four - independence, and the conflict the scheme is built around

The limitation. The Code requires the organization to be independent, to operate under a code of ethics, and not to engage in activities that could conflict with its independence. In particular it must not certify, as the flag State, a ship or an item it has itself designed, built, supplied, or is engaged to maintain; and its quality management system must be certified by a body accredited independently of the ROs and their associations.

The reason, and it is the structural weakness of the whole arrangement: the organization that surveys the ship is paid by the owner of the ship, and it competes commercially for that owner's business with other societies who also want it. Every independence rule in the Code is an answer to that single fact. The rule that the quality certifier must be independent of the ROs' own association exists because self-certification by the industry would defeat the object entirely.

Two related limits sit under this head. The society's dual capacity is itself a conflict to be managed - one surveyor attending one ship may be acting as class and as the flag State in the same visit, and the two roles have different masters and different consequences. And commercial pressure is a real limitation on what an RO will do: an owner dissatisfied with a society's rigour can change class, which is why oversight by the flag State, and the port State check that operates on the certificates, matter so much.

6. Limitation five - the RO has no enforcement power

The limitation. An RO is not a policeman. It cannot detain a ship, it cannot prosecute, it cannot impose a penalty, and it cannot compel anybody to do anything. Its powers are those of a certifier: it may refuse to issue a certificate, suspend or withdraw one where its authorization allows, impose conditions and recommendations, and report.

The reason. Detention and prosecution are exercises of state power over persons and property. They belong to the flag Administration in respect of its own ships and to the port State in respect of ships in its ports, and neither is transferable to a commercial company by a contract.

What follows is the RO's reporting duty, and it should be stated as the counterpart of its lack of power. Because the RO cannot act, it must tell somebody who can. It is required to report to the flag State - and its authorization must say so - on matters including withdrawal or suspension of class, conditions the ship has not met, and deficiencies found; and it is required to co-operate with port State authorities in rectifying reported deficiencies. A failure to report is therefore a compliance failure and not merely poor service, because the reporting duty is what makes a body without enforcement power useful to a flag State at all.

7. Limitation six - practical and jurisdictional limits

Collect the remaining limits, each with its reason, because they are separately markable and candidates rarely reach them.

  • A survey is a sample at a moment in time. A surveyor sees the ship on one day, in one condition, in one port, and much of her is inaccessible. He certifies compliance as found at survey; he does not guarantee her condition for the certificate's whole validity. The reason is physical, not legal - and it is why the master's and Chief Engineer's obligation to maintain the ship between surveys is not displaced by the certificate.
  • The RO depends on the information it is given. Plan approval rests on the drawings submitted, survey on access provided and records produced. It has no investigatory powers to compel disclosure.
  • Recognition is not authorization and neither is universal: an organization recognized by twenty flags is an RO only for those twenty and only within each one's scope.
  • Subcontracting is constrained. Statutory work is performed by the RO's own exclusive surveyors under its control, and the Code regulates the control of subcontractors and service suppliers; an RO cannot simply hire out a statutory survey.
  • Some services are reserved to the Administration and are not delegable at all - see the closing part of section 8.
  • The RO cannot bind the flag State in its relations with other Governments, and it has no standing to answer to a port State or a coastal State for the flag's compliance.

8. The statutory services undertaken on behalf of the Administration

Now the second limb. Note first the legal foundation, because the examiner will look for it: delegation is not a general power but rests on specific enabling provisions in the instruments themselves - SOLAS regulation I/6, Load Lines article 13, MARPOL Annex I regulation 6 and Annex II regulation 8, and Tonnage Convention article 6 - each of which permits the Administration to entrust surveys and inspections to nominated surveyors or recognized organizations.

What a classification body typically performs and issues on the Administration's behalf, by instrument:

InstrumentSurvey or service performedIssued on the flag State's behalf
SOLAS 1974 and 1988 ProtocolPlan approval and survey of hull, machinery, electrical systems, fire protection, life-saving and radio equipment; initial, annual, intermediate, periodical and renewal surveysCargo Ship Safety Construction, Safety Equipment and Safety Radio Certificates, or the combined Cargo Ship Safety Certificate; Passenger Ship Safety Certificate
SOLAS chapter II-2Verify the ship's arrangements for carrying dangerous goods in packaged formDocument of Compliance for the Carriage of Dangerous Goods
SOLAS chapter VII, IBC and IGC CodesSurvey of chemical tanker and gas carrier cargo containment, handling and safety systemsCertificate of Fitness for Dangerous Chemicals in Bulk; Certificate of Fitness for Liquefied Gases in Bulk
SOLAS chapter IX and the ISM CodeAudit of the company and of the ship's safety management systemDocument of Compliance and Safety Management Certificate, where ISM work is delegated
SOLAS chapter XI-2 and the ISPS CodeVerify the ship security system, and approve the Ship Security Plan where delegatedInternational Ship Security Certificate
SOLAS chapters II-1 and XI-1Approve stability and damage stability information, loading and cargo securing documents, and the enhanced survey programmeApproved stability booklet, Cargo Securing Manual and ESP survey report file
Load Lines 1966 and 1988 ProtocolAssign freeboard; survey the conditions of assignment, closing appliances and strengthInternational Load Line Certificate
Tonnage Convention 1969Measure and calculate gross and net tonnageInternational Tonnage Certificate (1969)
MARPOL Annex ISurvey the oil pollution prevention arrangements; approve the SOPEP; Condition Assessment Scheme where it appliesInternational Oil Pollution Prevention Certificate
MARPOL Annex IISurvey the noxious liquid substance arrangements; approve the Procedures and Arrangements Manual and the emergency planPollution Prevention Certificate for Noxious Liquid Substances in Bulk
MARPOL Annex IVSurvey the sewage treatment and holding arrangementsInternational Sewage Pollution Prevention Certificate
MARPOL Annex VISurvey the air pollution prevention arrangements; engine and energy efficiency verification; verify the SEEMPInternational Air Pollution Prevention Certificate; Engine IAPP Certificate; International Energy Efficiency Certificate
AFS Convention 2001Survey the anti-fouling systemInternational Anti-Fouling System Certificate
Ballast Water Management Convention 2004Approve the Ballast Water Management Plan and survey the treatment systemInternational Ballast Water Management Certificate
MLC 2006Inspect the fourteen areas of seafarer living and working conditions; review the owner's measuresMaritime Labour Certificate, and certification of the DMLC Part II
SOLAS chapter XIV and the Polar CodeSurvey the ship's capability for polar operation; approve the Polar Water Operational ManualPolar Ship Certificate
General technical servicesType approval of materials and equipment; welder and welding procedure approval; witness the inclining experiment; approve drawings; survey during building and after damage or repairApproval and test certificates, and the survey reports supporting the certificates above

Now the discriminating part of this limb - what a classification body does not do on the Administration's behalf, because knowing the boundary is worth more than lengthening the list.

  • Registration of the ship, and issue of the Certificate of Registry. An act of the registry, not a technical service.
  • The Minimum Safe Manning Document, which is a determination by the Administration of what manning the ship needs.
  • Seafarer certification under STCW - certificates of competency, endorsements and their recognition are the Administration's.
  • Exemption Certificates and the acceptance of equivalents, for the reasons in section 4.
  • The Declaration of Maritime Labour Compliance Part I, which states the flag State's own national requirements - the RO may certify Part II, the shipowner's measures, but only the flag can declare its own law.
  • Insurance and financial-security certificates under CLC, the Bunkers Convention and the Nairobi Wreck Removal Convention, which are issued by the State on evidence of insurance and are not technical surveys at all.
  • Casualty investigation under the Casualty Investigation Code, which is a flag State function that must be independent of certification.
  • Detention, prosecution and penalty - for the reasons in section 6.
  • And, at the other end of the boundary, class survey and condition of class are the society's OWN work, not statutory work done for anybody. They are the first thing candidates put in this list and they do not belong in it.

Add one honest qualification, because it is true and it is a mark: exactly which of these an Administration delegates is that Administration's choice. Some flags delegate almost everything; others retain ISM, ISPS or MLC certification. The list above is what is commonly delegated, and the authoritative answer for any ship is the flag State's own instrument of authorization and the list it has deposited with the Secretary-General.

Close on the proposition that ties both limbs together: a Recognized Organization does the flag State's work, with the flag State's authority, within limits the flag State has written down - and every limitation on it exists because the certificate it issues carries somebody else's name.

Study guide

Why this structure scores

The stem asks for limitations WITH REASONS and then for a list. That word 'reasons' is the whole design of this answer: each limitation is given as a proposition and then justified, because a bare list of limitations answers half of what was asked. Sections 2 to 7 therefore all follow the same shape - the limitation, the reason, the practical consequence - which also gives the candidate a template he can apply to a limitation he thought of himself.

Section 1 does the load-bearing work. Once the two hats are separated - class under the society's own rules for the owner, statutory under convention for the flag State - every limitation follows from the fact that the second hat is borrowed. A candidate who does not draw that distinction has no way to explain why any of the limits exist, and usually ends up listing them as arbitrary rules.

The table in section 8 is the answer to the second limb, but the bulleted list AFTER it is what separates a strong answer. Anyone can lengthen a certificate list; knowing that the Minimum Safe Manning Document, DMLC Part I, exemptions and CLC certificates are NOT delegated - and that condition of class is not statutory work at all - shows the examiner that the boundary is understood rather than the list memorised.

Common mistakes

  • Answering the RO Code question instead of this one. The corpus is full of questions asking what an RO is and how it is monitored; this examiner asks for LIMITATIONS and for a SERVICES LIST. Do not deliver a prepared RO Code answer.
  • Giving limitations with no reasons. The stem says 'highlighting them with reasons' and half the marks are in the justification.
  • Putting class survey, condition of class and class notations in the statutory services list. They are the society's own work under its own rules, not services performed for the Administration.
  • Saying the Administration delegates its responsibility. It delegates the function. This is the single proposition the examiner most wants to see.
  • Claiming an RO can grant exemptions or accept equivalents. Both are the Administration's, and this is the sharpest limitation of all.
  • Saying an RO can detain a ship. It cannot. It can refuse, suspend or withdraw a certificate and it must report.
  • Treating recognition and authorization as one act, or treating an authorization as universal. It is flag by flag and function by function.
  • Importing the 33rd Assembly resolution numbers. See the traps.

Examiner traps

  • The Assembly obligations list at this sitting is A.1157(32), not A.1187(33). A.1187(33) was adopted 6 December 2023, four months AFTER this paper, and it revoked A.1157(32). Almost every piece of circulating material is written for a later sitting and carries the wrong number here.
  • The ISM implementation guidelines at this sitting are A.1118(30) of 2017, not A.1188(33). Same boundary, same date, same trap.
  • Exemptions and equivalents are the Administration's alone. Expect 'the arrangement does not meet the rule but is just as good - can your surveyor accept it?' No. He recommends; the flag decides.
  • The RO cannot detain. Expect 'you find the ship unfit - what can you actually do?' Refuse, suspend or withdraw the certificate within your authorization, impose conditions, and report to the flag State; detention is for the Administration or the port State.
  • The DMLC has two parts and only one of them is delegable. Part I states the flag State's national requirements and is the flag's; Part II is the shipowner's measures and can be certified by the RO.
  • Class rules are not statutory requirements, and compliance with class does not discharge a convention obligation.
  • Capability is a legal limit, not a commercial one. The Code forbids a flag State to authorize beyond the RO's capabilities, and an appendix breaks the work into modules so that capability is assessed module by module.
  • The list of ROs and the conditions of the delegated authority are deposited with the Secretary-General. That is why a port State can know what a certificate represents - and it is the answer to 'how would anyone else find out what your society is allowed to do?'

Likely oral follow-up

  • Your society is my flag's RO. Can your surveyor exempt my ship from a SOLAS requirement?
  • You find the ship unfit to proceed to sea. What can you actually do about it?
  • What is the difference between a class survey and a statutory survey, on the same ship, on the same day?
  • Who issues the Minimum Safe Manning Document, and why not the RO?
  • Which part of the DMLC can your society certify, and which can it not?
  • Is a society that is recognized by my flag automatically allowed to survey my gas carrier?
  • How would a port State officer find out what your society is authorized to do for my flag?
  • The owner threatens to change class if you write that deficiency. What protects the system from that?
  • The ship is detained abroad. Who answers to the port State - you or the flag?
  • Name three services a flag State typically does NOT delegate.

Memory framework

The route is TWO HATS - RESPONSIBILITY - SPECIFIC - CANNOT MAKE LAW - INDEPENDENCE - NO ENFORCEMENT - PRACTICAL - SERVICES. For the six limitations hold RESPONSIBILITY / SCOPE / SOVEREIGNTY / INDEPENDENCE / ENFORCEMENT / PRACTICAL, and for each of them hold the same three-part shape: LIMIT - REASON - CONSEQUENCE. For the services table, run the instruments in order: SOLAS - LOAD LINES - TONNAGE - MARPOL - AFS - BWM - MLC - POLAR. And hold the closing line: every limitation exists because the certificate carries somebody else's name.

Regulation and source map

  • Code for Recognized Organizations - resolutions MSC.349(92) (21 June 2013) and MEPC.237(65); mandatory from 1 January 2015; Parts 1 and 2 mandatory, Part 3 recommendatory.
  • SOLAS regulation XI-1/1 - the provision giving the RO Code mandatory force.
  • RO Code Part 1 section 4 - the power to delegate, and the prohibition on authorizing beyond capability.
  • RO Code Part 1 section 5 - deposit with the Secretary-General of the list of ROs and the conditions of delegated authority.
  • RO Code Part 2 section 8 - the conventional bases for delegation, and the requirement to SPECIFY the functions, including withdrawal and cancellation.
  • RO Code Part 2 sections 2 and 7 - independence, ethics, and the independently accredited quality system.
  • RO Code appendices 2 and 3 - the elementary modules of survey and certification work, and the minimum elements of the written agreement.
  • SOLAS regulation I/6; Load Lines 1966 article 13; MARPOL Annex I regulation 6 and Annex II regulation 8; Tonnage 1969 article 6 - the enabling provisions for delegation.
  • SOLAS regulations I/4 and I/5 - exemptions and equivalents, powers of the ADMINISTRATION.
  • III Code, A.1070(28), mandatory 1 January 2016 - Part 2 requires an oversight programme for delegated authority.
  • A.1157(32) - the non-exhaustive list of obligations, operative at this sitting. A.1118(30) - ISM implementation guidelines, operative at this sitting.

Uncertainty and jurisdiction notes

  • Which services a flag actually delegates is that flag's choice. The table is the set that is COMMONLY delegated, not a statement of any Administration's instrument of authorization. The answer says so in its own text and no flag's authorization schedule is held.
  • The corpus copy of the RO Code has unverified edition and completeness (its edition and completeness are unverified in our source library). No section number rests on it alone; each is carried from our own verified records for two other sittings on this subject.
  • A.1157(32) and A.1118(30) are cited by identity only. We hold neither. Their identities, adopting sessions and revocation dates come from our own verified records; no clause of either is quoted.
  • we hold SOLAS at citation level and not as quotable text. Regulations I/4, I/5, I/6 and XI-1/1 are cited by identity and subject; nothing is quoted.
  • No certificate validity period is asserted. The stem asks for the services and the certificates, not for validity, and importing validity from an oil-tanker certificate register would import a tanker-specific set.
  • Three legal levels must be kept apart: the RO Code, an IMO instrument binding through the conventions; the authorization, an act of the flag State's national law; and classification, which is contractual and not statutory at all.
Recall the structure

Before you read the answer: can you name all 8 sections, in order? Say them or write them down, then reveal.

  1. __________The boundary the question turns on
  2. __________Limitation one - the function is delegated, the responsibility is not
  3. __________Limitation two - the authority is specific, never general
  4. __________Limitation three - the RO cannot make, waive or dispense with the law
  5. __________Limitation four - independence, and the conflict the scheme is built around
  6. __________Limitation five - the RO has no enforcement power
  7. __________Limitation six - practical and jurisdictional limits
  8. __________The statutory services undertaken on behalf of the Administration
Flashcards 9

1 the two hats - class versus statutory; 2 responsibility is not delegated; 3 the authority is specific, never general; 4 the RO cannot make, waive or dispense with the law; 5 independence and the built-in conflict; 6 no enforcement power; 7 practical and jurisdictional limits; 8 the statutory services delegated, and the ones that are not. Every limitation is given as LIMIT - REASON - CONSEQUENCE, because the stem asks for reasons.

Classification is its own work, under its own rules, for the owner who pays it, by contract - class survey, notations and condition of class belong here. Statutory work is the flag State's work, under convention and national law, done only because the Administration delegated it, and the certificates carry the flag State's name. Every limitation on an RO follows from the statutory hat being borrowed rather than owned.

No. Both are powers of the Administration, and the Exemption Certificate is the Administration's to issue. The reason is that they are sovereign acts: they alter the bargain the flag State struck with the other Contracting Governments, and only a party to that bargain can alter it. Delegating survey delegates the finding of fact; delegating exemption would delegate the law. The RO recommends and refers; the flag decides.

Refuse to issue, or suspend or withdraw, the certificate to the extent your authorization allows; impose conditions and recommendations; and report to the flag State. You cannot detain her, prosecute anyone or impose a penalty - those are exercises of state power belonging to the flag Administration and the port State, and are not transferable by contract. Because you cannot act, the reporting duty is what makes you useful.

For three reasons. Capability - the Code forbids a flag State to authorize beyond an RO's capabilities, and capability is assessed by elementary module. Transparency - the flag deposits the list of its ROs and the conditions of delegated authority with the Secretary-General, so a port State knows what a certificate represents. And controllability - a specified scope can be narrowed by ship type, convention or function as easily as suspended or withdrawn.

SOLAS regulation I/6, Load Lines 1966 article 13, MARPOL Annex I regulation 6, MARPOL Annex II regulation 8, and Tonnage Convention 1969 article 6 - each permitting the Administration to entrust surveys and inspections to nominated surveyors or recognized organizations. The RO Code itself is made mandatory through SOLAS regulation XI-1/1 and the corresponding MARPOL and Load Line Protocol amendments.

Registration of the ship and the Certificate of Registry; the Minimum Safe Manning Document; STCW seafarer certification and recognition; exemptions, Exemption Certificates and the acceptance of equivalents; DMLC Part I, which states the flag's own national requirements; CLC, Bunkers and wreck-removal financial security certificates; casualty investigation; and detention, prosecution and penalty. Class survey and condition of class are not statutory services at all.

A.1157(32) for the non-exhaustive list of obligations, and A.1118(30) of 2017 for the Guidelines on implementation of the ISM Code by Administrations. NOT A.1187(33) and A.1188(33) - both adopted 6 December 2023, four months after this sitting, each revoking its predecessor. Circulating material written for 2024 or later sittings carries the later numbers and is wrong here.

The organization surveying the ship is paid by the owner of the ship, and competes commercially with other societies for that owner's business - an owner dissatisfied with a society's rigour can change class. The Code answers with independence requirements, a code of ethics, a prohibition on certifying what the organization designed, supplied or maintains, and a quality system certified by a body accredited independently of the ROs and their own associations.

Quick revision
15-second recall
Two hats. Class is the society's own work for the owner; statutory work is the flag State's, borrowed. Six limits, each with its reason: responsibility is not delegated (treaty duty between governments); authority is specified, function by function, deposited with the Secretary-General; cannot exempt or accept equivalents (sovereign acts - it recommends, the flag decides); independence, because the surveyor is paid by the owner; no enforcement power - it can refuse, suspend, withdraw and must report; a survey is a sample in time. Services: SOLAS, Load Lines, Tonnage, MARPOL, AFS, BWM, MLC, Polar. NOT registry, manning, STCW, exemptions, DMLC Part I, CLC certificates.

Critical regulation
RO Code Part 2 section 8 - the conventional bases for delegation and the requirement that the authorization SPECIFY the functions conferred. If you remember one thing, remember the word specify: it is what makes every other limitation enforceable, because a scope that was written down function by function can be narrowed, suspended or withdrawn function by function.
Major trap
Citing A.1187(33) for the obligations list or A.1188(33) for the ISM implementation guidelines. Both were adopted 6 December 2023, four months after this sitting, and each revoked its 32nd or 30th Assembly predecessor. The operative instruments here are A.1157(32) and A.1118(30). Almost all circulating material is written for a later sitting and carries the wrong numbers.

Also on the platform: QP2308 Q8 - the surveys and certificates a Recognized Organization actually performs and issues under that delegation · QP2308 Q9 - the III Code, under which the flag State that delegated is itself audited on how it oversees its ROs · QP2308 Q7 - how the conventions under which these certificates are issued came into force in the first place

Once in this set Set once in the sittings MIW has transcribed.

Understand this first

Think of the engine's power leaking away through a series of gates on its journey from the cylinder to the sea. In the cylinder the gas pushes on the piston, and that is the most power that will ever exist - everything after it is less. Some is eaten getting the engine to turn itself: rings, bearings, its own pumps. What survives at the flywheel goes down the shaft, where the bearings and the stern tube take a little more. What arrives at the propeller is then handed to the water, and this is where most of it disappears, because a propeller is a fairly poor machine - it throws water backwards, and the water it throws is spinning as well as moving, which is energy the ship never sees. Right at the end there is a quantity that sounds odd until you see what it is for: the power you would need if you simply towed the ship along at that speed with a rope. That is the real output, because it is the only number that describes moving the hull rather than driving machinery. Two things about the last stage are worth holding, because they are what candidates lose marks on. The propeller is not swimming in clean water - it sits in the hull's wake, in water already being dragged along, so it is moving through that water more slowly than the ship is moving through the sea. And the propeller's own suction pulls back on the stern, so it makes the ship slightly harder to push than she would be if it were not there. The second limb is a different subject entirely. Once the ship is built, nobody actually knows how she will handle - the tank and the computer said one thing, and the sea has the final word. So before she is handed over she is taken out and made to do the things that will matter later: run at speed, turn hard both ways, be swung one way and then checked the other, and stopped from full speed. The point of every one of those is a number the master can rely on when he has thirty seconds to decide, and that is why the results end up on a poster in the wheelhouse rather than in a file.
