Cover46 core points beneath them

Memorise the 8 headings. The core points are there to help you check whether an answer is too thin — they are not a mark scheme, and they are not 46 separate facts to learn individually.

Show the core points to cover
1. The power chain, as one idea
  • Each named power is the previous one less the loss at one interface; the ratio between two is an efficiency
  • Indicated - Brake - Shaft - Delivered - Thrust - Effective
  • Effective power is not delivered to anything: it is the power needed to TOW the ship at that speed
  • Only indicated, brake and shaft power are directly measurable on a ship
  • The first losses are mechanical friction; the last two are hydrodynamic and far the largest
2. Indicated power - what the engine develops in the cylinder
  • Power developed by the gas on the piston, before any friction
  • From the indicator diagram - mean indicated pressure, piston area, stroke, working strokes per unit time, all cylinders
  • The only power attributable to ONE cylinder, so it is the basis of load balancing and combustion diagnosis
  • The loss begins at rings, gudgeon pin, crosshead, bearings, valve gear and engine-driven pumps
3. Brake power, and mechanical efficiency
  • Power available at the output coupling or flywheel, after engine friction and engine-driven auxiliaries
  • Mechanical efficiency = brake / indicated, typically 0.85 to 0.92
  • Friction power is nearly constant with load, so mechanical efficiency FALLS sharply at low load
  • Measured on a test bed against a dynamometer; inferred rather than measured on board
4. Shaft power and delivered power - the transmission
  • Shaft power is measured by torsionmeter from torque and revolutions, normally on the intermediate shaft
  • Delivered power is what actually reaches the propeller, after stern tube, plummer blocks, thrust block and any gearing
  • Transmission efficiency typically 0.97 to 0.99 direct coupled, lower with a gearbox
  • The difference depends on WHERE the torsionmeter is, not only on the ship
  • Without a torsionmeter, shaft power is inferred from brake power and an assumed efficiency - state the assumption
5. Thrust power and effective power - the propeller and the hull
  • Thrust power = thrust x speed of advance, and speed of advance is NOT ship speed
  • The propeller works in the hull's wake, described by the wake fraction
  • Effective power = total resistance x ship speed - the power to tow the hull
  • Thrust deduction: the propeller lowers pressure over the after body and increases the hull's resistance
  • Hull efficiency = effective / thrust = (1 - t) / (1 - w), commonly GREATER than 1 on a single-screw ship
6. The efficiencies that join the stages
  • Mechanical = brake / indicated, 0.85 to 0.92
  • Transmission = delivered / brake, 0.97 to 0.99 direct coupled
  • Propeller open-water efficiency 0.60 to 0.70 - by far the largest single loss
  • Relative rotative efficiency, close to unity, 0.95 to 1.05
  • Hull efficiency, often above 1
  • Quasi-propulsive coefficient = effective / delivered, 0.65 to 0.75; propulsive coefficient = effective / brake, 0.60 to 0.72
  • Roughly two thirds of cylinder power reaches the water, and most of the loss is at the propeller and hull
7. Sea trials - what a navigation trial is for, and the conditions it must be run in
  • To demonstrate the ship handles as designed, satisfy the manoeuvring standard, and produce the wheelhouse poster and manoeuvring booklet
  • IMO Standards for Ship Manoeuvrability, MSC.137(76), adopted 4 December 2002
  • Applies to ships of 100 m and over and all chemical tankers and gas carriers, constructed on or after 1 January 2004
  • Condition one - deep, unrestricted water
  • Condition two - calm environment, negligible current, runs on reciprocal headings
  • Condition three - full load even keel, or trial in ballast with a recorded correction to the loaded condition
  • Condition four - steady approach and steady conditions through the measured run
8. The essential manoeuvres, and the objective of each
  • Speed and power trial - the power/revolutions/speed relationship, contract speed, and the design index measurement; also the datum for the ship's whole service life
  • Turning circle both ways - advance, transfer, tactical diameter, drift angle and speed loss; advance not more than 4.5 L, tactical diameter not more than 5 L
  • Zig-zag 10/10 and 20/20 - yaw checking and course keeping; first and second overshoot angles, limits depending on the length-to-speed ratio
  • Initial turning - promptness of response; not more than 2.5 L travelled for a 10 degree heading change
  • Stopping test - track reach, head reach and time; not more than 15 L, and heading is not controllable
  • Spiral, direct and reverse - directional stability; a hysteresis loop measures the instability, and the reverse spiral works where the direct one cannot
  • Pull-out - a quick check of the same property by the residual rate of turn
  • Astern and low-speed trials, steering gear changeover and emergency steering, bow thruster; and man-overboard where programmed
  • Results become the wheelhouse poster, pilot card and manoeuvring booklet; criteria are in ship lengths so all sizes are judged alike
Model written answer

1. The power chain, as one idea

Before naming any stage, state the idea, because the stem asks for a transformation and a list of six powers is not one. Power is developed in the cylinder and is then reduced at every interface between there and the water. Each named power is the previous one less the loss at one interface, and the ratio between two adjacent powers is an efficiency. The last of them, effective power, is not power delivered to anything at all - it is the power that would be needed simply to tow the ship at that speed.

The chain, in order:

Indicated power → Brake power → Shaft power → Delivered power → Thrust power → Effective power.

Two remarks that repay a sentence each. Only three of these are directly measurable on a ship - indicated, brake and shaft power - and the rest are computed or inferred, which is why the propulsive coefficient is quoted rather than read off a gauge. And the losses are not all of the same kind: the first two are mechanical friction, and the last two are hydrodynamic and are far the largest.

2. Indicated power - what the engine develops in the cylinder

Indicated power is the power developed by the gas on the piston, before anything is lost to friction. It is obtained from the indicator diagram - the pressure-volume diagram taken from the cylinder - and in the classical form is calculated from the mean indicated pressure, the piston area, the stroke and the number of working strokes per unit time, summed over all cylinders.

  • Measured by mechanical indicator or, on a modern engine, by an electronic pressure transducer producing the same diagram continuously.
  • What it is used for: it is the only power that can be attributed to one cylinder, so it is the basis of load balancing between units and of combustion diagnosis. It is also the datum against which every later loss is expressed.
  • Where the loss begins: everything between the gas force on the piston and the flywheel - piston rings, gudgeon pin, crosshead, main and crankpin bearings, camshaft and valve gear drive, and the engine-driven pumps.

3. Brake power, and mechanical efficiency

Brake power is the power actually available at the engine output coupling or flywheel - the power the engine delivers after its own friction and its own auxiliaries have been paid for. The name is historical: it was measured by a brake absorbing the output on a test bed.

The ratio joining the first two stages is the mechanical efficiency:

  • Mechanical efficiency = brake power ÷ indicated power, and it is typically of the order of 0.85 to 0.92, being higher on a large slow-speed crosshead engine than on a medium-speed trunk-piston one.
  • The difference between the two is the friction power, which is very nearly constant with load. That has a practical consequence worth stating: mechanical efficiency falls sharply at low load, because a nearly constant friction loss is being taken from a much smaller indicated power - which is part of why prolonged low-load running is inefficient.
  • Measured on the test bed against a dynamometer; on board it is inferred rather than measured directly.

4. Shaft power and delivered power - the transmission

Shaft power is the power transmitted along the shafting, measured where the torsionmeter is fitted - normally on the intermediate shaft in the shaft tunnel. It is obtained from the torque and the rotational speed, the torque being derived from the measured twist of a known length of shaft of known stiffness.

Delivered power is the power actually delivered to the propeller, at the propeller itself. It is shaft power less the losses in the bearings between the measuring point and the propeller - principally the stern tube bearing and the aftermost plummer blocks, and, where they lie between, the thrust block and any gearbox.

  • The transmission or shafting efficiency is of the order of 0.97 to 0.99 for a direct-coupled diesel installation with a short shaft, and lower where a gearbox is interposed, a gearbox costing perhaps a further 2 to 3 per cent.
  • Note the practical point about the measuring position: the further forward the torsionmeter, the more bearing loss lies between it and the propeller. The difference between shaft power and delivered power depends on where you measured, not only on what the ship is.
  • Where no torsionmeter is fitted, shaft power is inferred from brake power and an assumed transmission efficiency - which is an assumption, and should be stated as one.

5. Thrust power and effective power - the propeller and the hull

This is where the two largest losses occur and where candidates most often stop too early.

Thrust power is the useful power the propeller produces in the water: thrust multiplied by the speed of advance - and the speed of advance is not the ship's speed. The propeller works in the hull's wake, in water that is already being dragged along with the ship, so it advances through that water more slowly than the ship advances through the sea. The difference is described by the wake fraction.

Effective power is the power required to tow the hull at that speed against her total resistance: total resistance multiplied by ship speed. It is what the naval architect predicts from the model tank, and it is the true output of the whole installation.

Two separate effects sit between thrust power and effective power, and naming both is what completes the answer:

  • Thrust deduction. The propeller working close astern of the hull lowers the pressure over the after body and so increases the hull's resistance. The thrust the propeller must produce is therefore greater than the resistance being overcome. This is described by the thrust deduction fraction.
  • Wake. The hull drags water with it, so the propeller advances into a slower stream, described by the wake fraction.
  • Together these two give the hull efficiency, which is the ratio of effective power to thrust power and is (1 minus the thrust deduction fraction) divided by (1 minus the wake fraction). On a conventional single-screw ship it is commonly greater than 1 - which surprises candidates and is worth explaining: it is not a violation of anything, but a statement that the propeller benefits more from working in the ship's own wake than it loses by increasing her resistance.

6. The efficiencies that join the stages

Set the ratios out as a chain, because it makes the whole answer countable and shows where every watt went.

  • Mechanical efficiency = brake ÷ indicated. Engine friction. Typically 0.85 to 0.92.
  • Transmission (shafting) efficiency = delivered ÷ brake. Bearings, thrust block, gearing. Typically 0.97 to 0.99 direct-coupled.
  • Propeller open-water efficiency = the propeller's efficiency measured alone in undisturbed water. Typically 0.60 to 0.70 for a conventional fixed-pitch propeller, and it is by far the largest single loss in the chain.
  • Relative rotative efficiency = the correction between the propeller's efficiency behind the hull and in open water, the wake being non-uniform. Close to 1, commonly 0.95 to 1.05.
  • Hull efficiency = effective ÷ thrust = (1 − t) ÷ (1 − w). Often greater than 1 on a single-screw ship.
  • Quasi-propulsive coefficient = effective ÷ delivered = hull × open-water × relative rotative. Typically 0.65 to 0.75.
  • Propulsive coefficient = effective ÷ brake (or effective ÷ shaft, if that is what is stated - and it should be stated). Typically 0.60 to 0.72.

Finish limb A with the proposition the chain exists to make: of the power developed in the cylinder, roughly two thirds reaches the water as useful towing power on a good conventional ship, and the great majority of what is lost is lost at the propeller and the hull, not in the machinery. That is why Q3's rudder devices and hull maintenance matter more to fuel consumption than any plausible improvement in mechanical efficiency.

7. Sea trials - what a navigation trial is for, and the conditions it must be run in

Turning to limb B. A navigation or manoeuvring trial is conducted to demonstrate that the completed ship handles as she was designed to, to satisfy the international manoeuvring standard, and to produce the data that goes into the wheelhouse poster and the manoeuvring booklet so that the master knows what his ship will actually do. It is quite separate from the machinery trials, which prove the plant.

The governing instrument is the IMO Standards for Ship Manoeuvrability, resolution MSC.137(76), adopted 4 December 2002, which applies to ships of 100 m in length and over and to all chemical tankers and gas carriers regardless of length, constructed on or after 1 January 2004. Its explanatory notes set out the procedures.

The trial conditions are part of the answer, not a preamble. A manoeuvring result obtained in the wrong water or the wrong loading cannot be compared with the criterion it is being tested against.

  1. Deep, unrestricted water. Shallow water and bank effects change turning and stopping behaviour completely.
  2. Calm environment - low wind and sea, and negligible current. Trials are run on reciprocal headings so that any residual current cancels.
  3. Full load, even keel, or as near as practicable; where the ship cannot be trialled loaded - as with most tankers and bulk carriers, delivered in ballast - the trial is run in the ballast condition and the results are corrected or predicted to the loaded condition, and the correction is recorded.
  4. Steady approach at the test speed and steady conditions throughout the measured run, with the engine at the specified setting.

8. The essential manoeuvres, and the objective of each

Give the objective first and the procedure second, since the stem asks for objectives.

  • Speed and power trial (progressive speed trial). Objective: to establish the relationship between shaft power, propeller revolutions and ship speed, to demonstrate the contract speed, and - on a modern newbuilding - to provide the measurement from which the ship's attained energy efficiency design index is verified. Procedure: runs at several engine settings on reciprocal headings over a measured distance, with the results corrected for wind, sea, current, water depth and displacement. This trial also fixes the datum against which the ship's whole service life is later judged - trial day is the best she will ever perform.
  • Turning circle test. Objective: to establish turning ability, and to measure the distances the master needs for collision avoidance and for planning a turn in confined water. Procedure: from a steady approach at test speed, the rudder is put over to the maximum design angle - normally 35 degrees - and held until the ship has turned at least 360 degrees, to port and to starboard. Measured: advance, transfer, tactical diameter, steady turning diameter, drift angle and the speed loss in the turn, which is substantial and surprises people. Criteria: the standard requires advance not to exceed 4.5 ship lengths and tactical diameter not to exceed 5 ship lengths.
  • Zig-zag test. Objective: to measure yaw-checking and course-keeping ability - how quickly the ship responds to helm and, more importantly, how quickly the swing can be stopped once she has started. This is the trial that matters most for course keeping in traffic. Procedure: the 10°/10° test - rudder to 10 degrees, held until the heading has changed 10 degrees, then reversed to 10 degrees the other way, and so on - and the 20°/20° test similarly. Measured: the first and second overshoot angles, and the initial turning time. Criteria: the standard sets limits on the overshoot angles that depend on the ratio of ship length to speed, recognising that a long slow ship must be allowed more overshoot than a short fast one; and it sets a separate limit for the 20°/20° first overshoot.
  • Initial turning test. Objective: to measure the promptness of the ship's initial response to helm, separately from her eventual turning circle. Procedure: rudder to 10 degrees from a steady approach. Criterion: the ship should not have travelled more than 2.5 ship lengths by the time her heading has changed by 10 degrees.
  • Stopping test (crash stop). Objective: to establish the distance and time to bring the ship to rest from full ahead using full astern power - the emergency the master will be asked about at every inquiry. Procedure: from full ahead at test speed, the engine is ordered full astern and held until the ship is stopped through the water. Measured: track reach, head reach, and the time; and note that the ship's heading is not controllable during the manoeuvre once steerage way is lost, which is itself a finding the master must know. Criterion: the standard requires the track reach not to exceed 15 ship lengths, with provision for the Administration to accept more for ships of large displacement where 15 is impracticable.
  • Spiral test - direct, and reverse. Objective: to establish whether the ship is directionally (course) stable, and if she is not, how unstable. Procedure: in the direct spiral, the rudder is set at a series of decreasing angles from one side through zero to the other, the steady rate of turn being recorded at each; in the reverse spiral, the ship is held at a series of rates of turn and the rudder angle required is recorded, which is quicker and works on an unstable ship. Result: a course-unstable ship produces a hysteresis loop around zero rudder, and the width of the loop measures the instability - meaning that within that band of rudder angles the ship will turn without being told to.
  • Pull-out test. Objective: a quick check of the same property. Procedure: after a steady turn, the rudder is returned to midships and the rate of turn recorded. Result: on a stable ship the rate of turn decays to zero; on an unstable ship a residual rate of turn persists, and the difference between the residual rates to port and to starboard indicates the size of the instability.
  • Astern and low-speed manoeuvring trials. Objective: to establish how the ship behaves going astern and at manoeuvring speeds, where the rudder is largely ineffective and transverse thrust dominates - which is what the master actually experiences berthing. Includes engine crash-astern and crash-ahead response times, steering gear tests including changeover to the second power unit and to emergency steering, and bow thruster performance where fitted.
  • Man-overboard and confined-water manoeuvres, where the trial programme includes them. Objective: to establish and record a recovery turn that will actually work in this ship.

Two closing points that turn a description into an answer. The results are not an academic record. They are reduced into the wheelhouse poster, the pilot card and the manoeuvring booklet, and their whole purpose is that the master and the pilot can predict the ship before they need to. And the manoeuvring performance is measured in ship lengths rather than in metres for a reason: it makes the criteria comparable between a coaster and a very large crude carrier, and it is why a large ship's alarming absolute stopping distance may still comfortably satisfy the standard.

End on the proposition that joins the two limbs: limb A accounts for where the engine's power goes, and limb B measures what the ship does with what is left. The trial is where the naval architect's prediction and the engineer's plant are checked against the sea for the first and best time.

Study guide

Why this structure scores

Limb A is a chain and the marks are in the chain, not in the six nouns. The structure states the transformation in one sentence before naming any stage, then takes the stages in order, and then collects the efficiencies into a single list in section 6 - which is where a marker can count them. A candidate who writes six definitions in isolation has described the stages without describing the transformation the stem asked about.

Giving WHERE each power is measured is the discriminating detail in limb A. Only indicated, brake and shaft power are directly measurable on a ship; delivered, thrust and effective power are computed. That single observation explains why the propulsive coefficient is quoted from the trial rather than read from an instrument, and why the position of the torsionmeter changes the answer.

In limb B the objective is given before the procedure for every manoeuvre, because the stem asks for the typical objectives of each test and candidates reliably describe procedures instead. The trial CONDITIONS are given their own numbered section for the same reason: a result obtained in shallow water or the wrong loading cannot be compared with the criterion, and saying so shows the examiner that the standard is understood rather than listed.

Common mistakes

  • Stopping the chain at delivered power. The stem asks for the transformation into EFFECTIVE power, and the propeller and hull stages are where most of the loss is.
  • Confusing ship speed with speed of advance. Thrust power uses the speed of advance, which is lower because the propeller works in the hull's wake.
  • Omitting thrust deduction, or confusing it with wake. Wake is the propeller seeing slower water; thrust deduction is the propeller increasing the hull's resistance. Both are needed to get hull efficiency.
  • Saying hull efficiency cannot exceed 1. On a conventional single-screw ship it commonly does, and being able to explain why is worth a mark.
  • Quoting the propulsive coefficient without saying whether it is against brake or shaft power. Say which.
  • Describing sea trial procedures without giving the objectives. The stem asks for the objectives explicitly.
  • Forgetting the trial conditions - deep unrestricted water, calm, full load, reciprocal headings.
  • Running the turning circle one way only. It is done to port AND starboard, because a single-screw ship is not symmetrical.
  • Treating the zig-zag as a turning test. It measures yaw checking and course keeping - how the swing is STOPPED.

Examiner traps

  • Mechanical efficiency falls at low load, because friction power is nearly constant while indicated power is not. Expect this as a follow-up on slow steaming.
  • The difference between shaft power and delivered power depends on where the torsionmeter is. The further forward it is, the more bearing loss lies between it and the propeller.
  • Hull efficiency = (1 − t) ÷ (1 − w), and it is usually greater than unity on a single-screw ship. The examiner will ask how a hull can have an efficiency above 100 per cent. It is a ratio of two powers, not a conversion.
  • Open-water propeller efficiency is the largest single loss in the chain - typically 0.60 to 0.70. Machinery improvements cannot compete with it, which is the whole justification for energy saving devices.
  • The manoeuvring criteria are expressed in SHIP LENGTHS, not metres, so that a coaster and a VLCC are judged on the same scale. A very large ship's frightening absolute stopping distance may satisfy the standard comfortably.
  • The 10°/10° zig-zag overshoot limits depend on the ratio of length to speed. A long slow ship is allowed more overshoot than a short fast one - the criterion is not a single number.
  • Heading is not controllable in a crash stop once steerage way is lost. The ship will usually swing, and which way she swings depends on her propeller.
  • Trials are usually run in ballast on tankers and bulk carriers and the results corrected or predicted to the loaded condition. Expect to be asked what you would do about that, and record the correction.
  • The reverse spiral works on a course-unstable ship and the direct spiral does not - which is the reason the reverse spiral exists.

Likely oral follow-up

  • Name the powers in order from the cylinder to the water, and tell me which of them you can actually measure.
  • What is the difference between ship speed and speed of advance, and which one does thrust power use?
  • How can hull efficiency be greater than one?
  • Where is the biggest loss in the whole chain, and what does that tell you about where to spend money?
  • Your torsionmeter reads shaft power. How do you get delivered power?
  • Why does mechanical efficiency get worse when you slow steam?
  • What does a zig-zag test tell you that a turning circle does not?
  • The ship was trialled in ballast. Is her manoeuvring booklet any use to you loaded?
  • You order full astern from full ahead. Which way will she go, and can you steer her?
  • What is a hysteresis loop in a spiral test, and what does its width mean for the man on the wheel?

Memory framework

For limb A hold the chain as six words in order: INDICATED - BRAKE - SHAFT - DELIVERED - THRUST - EFFECTIVE, and between them five efficiencies: MECHANICAL - TRANSMISSION - OPEN WATER - RELATIVE ROTATIVE - HULL. Remember that only the first three powers are measurable. For limb B hold the conditions as DEEP - CALM - LOADED - STEADY, and the manoeuvres as SPEED - TURNING - ZIG-ZAG - INITIAL TURN - STOPPING - SPIRAL - PULL-OUT - ASTERN. For the criteria hold three numbers in ship lengths: 4.5 advance, 5 tactical diameter, 15 track reach, and 2.5 for initial turning.

Regulation and source map

  • IMO Standards for Ship Manoeuvrability, resolution MSC.137(76), adopted 4 December 2002 - ships of 100 m and over and all chemical tankers and gas carriers regardless of length, constructed on or after 1 January 2004.
  • Criteria under that standard - advance not more than 4.5 L; tactical diameter not more than 5 L; initial turning within 2.5 L for a 10-degree heading change; stopping track reach not more than 15 L, with provision for more on large displacement ships; and zig-zag overshoot limits expressed against the length-to-speed ratio.
  • The explanatory notes to that standard - the trial procedures and the treatment of trials in non-standard conditions.
  • SOLAS chapter V - the carriage of manoeuvring information on board, which is what the trial results become.
  • MARPOL Annex VI chapter 4 as revised by MEPC.328(76) - the speed trial as the measurement underlying the attained design index on a newbuilding. Cited as purpose only; no formula is asserted.
  • International towing tank practice for the conduct and correction of a speed and power trial. Cited generically; no procedure number or edition is held.

Uncertainty and evidence limits

  • No efficiency figure here applies to a particular ship. Every value is a typical range for a conventional single-screw motor ship, and values differ with machinery type, shafting arrangement, hull form and loading.
  • We hold no copy of MSC.137(76) or of its explanatory notes. Application, conditions and criteria are cited by identity and by criterion from our own verified records. No paragraph is quoted, and the zig-zag overshoot limits are stated as depending on the length-to-speed ratio rather than reproduced as a table, because the table is not held.
  • No towing tank recommended procedure is held or cited by number or edition. Speed-trial conduct and correction are described by principle.
  • No shipyard or class trial programme is held. The set described is the standard manoeuvring set; an actual programme is agreed between builder, owner and society and contains items that are not manoeuvring trials at all.
  • The paper that prints the nearest version of this stem has not yet completed our own review, so its answer is deliberately not relied on. The power chain is re-derived here from principle and every relationship stated is checkable from its own definition.
Recall the structure

Before you read the answer: can you name all 8 sections, in order? Say them or write them down, then reveal.

  1. __________The power chain, as one idea
  2. __________Indicated power - what the engine develops in the cylinder
  3. __________Brake power, and mechanical efficiency
  4. __________Shaft power and delivered power - the transmission
  5. __________Thrust power and effective power - the propeller and the hull
  6. __________The efficiencies that join the stages
  7. __________Sea trials - what a navigation trial is for, and the conditions it must be run in
  8. __________The essential manoeuvres, and the objective of each
Flashcards 10

Limb A: 1 the chain as one idea; 2 indicated power; 3 brake power and mechanical efficiency; 4 shaft and delivered power; 5 thrust and effective power; 6 the efficiencies collected. Limb B: 7 what a navigation trial is for and the conditions it must be run in; 8 the essential manoeuvres with the objective of each stated before the procedure.

Indicated, brake, shaft, delivered, thrust, effective. Between them: mechanical efficiency (brake/indicated), transmission or shafting efficiency (delivered/brake), propeller open-water efficiency, relative rotative efficiency, and hull efficiency (effective/thrust). Quasi-propulsive coefficient is effective over delivered; propulsive coefficient is effective over brake or shaft - say which.

Effective power is total resistance multiplied by ship speed - the power that would be needed simply to tow the hull at that speed. It is the true output of the whole installation, and it is not delivered to any component: it is the measure of moving the hull rather than of driving machinery. That is why it is the numerator of both the quasi-propulsive and the propulsive coefficient.

Wake is about the water the propeller sees: the hull drags water with it, so the propeller advances through that stream more slowly than the ship advances through the sea, which is why thrust power uses speed of advance. Thrust deduction is about the hull: the propeller lowers the pressure over the after body and so increases the hull's resistance, meaning the thrust required exceeds the resistance overcome. Hull efficiency combines both as (1−t)/(1−w).

Because it is a ratio of two powers, not a conversion, and nothing is being created. On a conventional single-screw ship the propeller gains more from working in the ship's own wake - advancing through slower water - than it loses by increasing her resistance through thrust deduction. So (1−t)/(1−w) commonly exceeds unity. It is not a violation of anything.

Deep unrestricted water, because shallow water and bank effects change turning and stopping completely; a calm environment with negligible current, with runs on reciprocal headings so any residual current cancels; full load on even keel, or trial in ballast with a recorded correction to the loaded condition; and a steady approach with steady conditions through the run. A result obtained outside these conditions cannot be compared with the criterion it is tested against.

Yaw-checking and course-keeping ability - how quickly the swing can be STOPPED once it has started, not how tightly the ship turns. The 10°/10° and 20°/20° tests measure the first and second overshoot angles, and the standard's limits depend on the ratio of ship length to speed, so a long slow ship is allowed more overshoot than a short fast one. It is the trial that matters most for course keeping in traffic.

Turning ability: advance not more than 4.5 ship lengths and tactical diameter not more than 5. Initial turning: not more than 2.5 lengths travelled by the time heading has changed 10 degrees. Stopping: track reach not more than 15 lengths, with provision for more on ships of large displacement. Yaw checking: overshoot limits in the zig-zag, depending on the length-to-speed ratio. All are in ship lengths so every size is judged alike.

Whether the ship is directionally stable. The rudder is set at a series of decreasing angles from one side through zero to the other, and the steady rate of turn is recorded at each. A course-unstable ship produces a hysteresis loop around zero rudder, whose width measures the instability - within that band she will turn without being told to. The reverse spiral instead holds set rates of turn and records the rudder needed, which is quicker and works on an unstable ship where the direct spiral does not.

Because friction power is very nearly constant with load while indicated power is not. The same friction loss is being taken from a much smaller indicated power, so brake divided by indicated falls. It is one of the reasons prolonged low-load running is inefficient, alongside poorer combustion, cooler components and turbocharger mismatch.

Quick revision
15-second recall
INDICATED - BRAKE - SHAFT - DELIVERED - THRUST - EFFECTIVE. Joined by mechanical, transmission, open-water, relative rotative and hull efficiencies. Only the first three are measurable. Effective power is the power to tow the ship; thrust power uses speed of advance, not ship speed; hull efficiency is (1−t)/(1−w) and is usually above 1. Trials: deep, calm, loaded, steady, reciprocal headings. Manoeuvres: speed, turning circle both ways, zig-zag 10/10 and 20/20, initial turn, crash stop, spiral, pull-out, astern. Criteria in ship lengths: 4.5 advance, 5 tactical diameter, 2.5 initial turn, 15 track reach.

Critical regulation
IMO Standards for Ship Manoeuvrability, resolution MSC.137(76). If you remember one thing about it, remember that its criteria are expressed in ship lengths and not in metres - which is what allows a coaster and a very large crude carrier to be judged against the same standard, and why a large ship's alarming absolute stopping distance can still comply comfortably.
Major trap
Stopping the power chain at delivered power, or confusing ship speed with speed of advance. Thrust power is thrust times the speed of ADVANCE, which is lower than ship speed because the propeller works in the hull's wake. The related trap is treating wake and thrust deduction as the same thing: wake is the propeller seeing slower water, thrust deduction is the propeller making the hull harder to push, and you need both to reach hull efficiency.

Also on the platform: QP2308 Q3 - the rudder devices that act on the propeller losses inside the propulsive coefficient described here · QP2308 Q4 - the shipboard monitoring by which the trial datum is compared with the ship's actual performance years later · QP2308 Q8 - the surveys at which a ship's machinery and steering arrangements are examined after she enters service

Repeated — reworded Returns reworded from March 2023 Q5.

Understand this first

Start from the one fact that explains everything else: nobody can make a country obey a treaty it has not agreed to. The Organization is a place where governments negotiate and a secretariat that keeps the paperwork - it has no parliament and no police. So a rule only becomes law for a ship when her flag State has personally said yes and then written the rule into its own statute book. That single constraint produces the whole shape of the answer. First somebody has to propose the idea and the technical people have to argue it into a text everybody can live with. Then the text is fixed at a conference - and at that moment nobody at all is bound by it, which surprises people. Then each government goes home and does the slow part: passes a law, gives itself the power to inspect and prosecute, trains or hires surveyors, and only then formally lodges its agreement. And finally the treaty waits until enough governments have lodged - not just enough by headcount, but enough ships, because a rule agreed by thirty countries that own hardly any tonnage would regulate almost nothing and would simply penalise the owners of those thirty. That tonnage requirement is the clever part and the painful part at once. It is what makes the convention worth having, and it is exactly why it takes ten years, because it means waiting for a handful of very large registries. Underneath it all sits an awkward piece of arithmetic that no procedure can abolish: everyone is better off if the rule comes in, and each one individually is slightly better off going last, because whoever moves first pays for compliance while his competitors do not. Once you see that, the cures make sense - they all work either by making it cheaper to say yes, or by finding a way not to have to ask again.

Knowledge map
The Four Stages of an IMO Convention, and the Entry-into-Force Delay
  1. 1What an IMO convention is, and the four stages
    • The Organization has no power to make law - binding force comes from States' consent and their national legislation
    • Stage 1 proposal and development; stage 2 adoption; stage 3 acceptance; stage 4 entry into force
    • Implementation and enforcement FOLLOW the fourth stage and are not a fifth
  2. 2Stage one - proposal and development
    • A Member State or group proposes to MSC, MEPC, the Legal Committee or the Facilitation Committee
    • Proposals commonly follow a casualty, a scientific finding or a commercial development
    • Technical drafting is done in a sub-committee over several sessions, with consultative organisations contributing
  3. 3Stage two - adoption
    • A new convention is normally adopted by a diplomatic conference open to all States, not only IMO Members
    • Amendments and codes are adopted by the relevant committee or by the Assembly, by resolution
    • Adoption creates NO obligation - the text exists and nobody is bound
  4. 4Stage three - acceptance, and the four ways of giving it
    • Signature subject to ratification, acceptance or approval - a two-step route; the gap is where national legislation is done
    • Definitive signature, where permitted - consent in one step; rare for technical conventions
    • Accession - a single act by a State that did not sign, with identical legal effect to ratification
  5. 5Stage four - entry into force
    • Every convention contains its own entry-into-force article - that article decides, not the Organization
    • For an important technical convention: a minimum number of Contracting States, commonly 15 to 30
    • Plus a minimum percentage of world merchant shipping by gross tonnage, commonly 50 per cent
  6. 6Why the delay happens
    • STRUCTURAL: demanding thresholds by design; conflicting national interests; the Organization cannot compel
    • NATIONAL: parliamentary time for enabling legislation; administrative capacity to survey, certify and prosecute; legal and linguistic work; political change
    • INDUSTRIAL: the technology may not exist or be affordable at adoption - the BWM case
  7. 7The delay, in worked examples
    • SOLAS 1974 - adopted 1 November 1974, in force 25 May 1980: about five and a half years
    • MARPOL - the 1973 Convention never entered into force alone; absorbed into the 1978 Protocol, Annexes I and II in force 2 October 1983
    • Bunkers 2001 - adopted 23 March 2001, in force 21 November 2008: nearly eight years, with no technology requirement
  8. 8What can be done to reduce the delay
    • TACIT ACCEPTANCE - the burden reversed, silence means yes; MARPOL article 16 and the SOLAS equivalent. The single most effective reform
    • Make a standard binding through a convention already in force - the ISM, ISPS, RO and III Codes all arrived this way
    • Amend by protocol where an instrument has stalled - the 1978 Protocol rescued MARPOL; the 1988 Protocols modernised SOLAS and Load Lines

Remember8 route headings
Cover47 core points beneath them

Memorise the 8 headings. The core points are there to help you check whether an answer is too thin — they are not a mark scheme, and they are not 47 separate facts to learn individually.

Show the core points to cover
1. What an IMO convention is, and the four stages
  • The Organization has no power to make law - binding force comes from States' consent and their national legislation
  • Stage 1 proposal and development; stage 2 adoption; stage 3 acceptance; stage 4 entry into force
  • Implementation and enforcement FOLLOW the fourth stage and are not a fifth
  • Much of what a State must do to implement has to be done BEFORE it can responsibly ratify
2. Stage one - proposal and development
  • A Member State or group proposes to MSC, MEPC, the Legal Committee or the Facilitation Committee
  • Proposals commonly follow a casualty, a scientific finding or a commercial development
  • Technical drafting is done in a sub-committee over several sessions, with consultative organisations contributing
  • Slow legitimately: a text pushed through against unresolved objections is one States will not ratify
3. Stage two - adoption
  • A new convention is normally adopted by a diplomatic conference open to all States, not only IMO Members
  • Amendments and codes are adopted by the relevant committee or by the Assembly, by resolution
  • Adoption creates NO obligation - the text exists and nobody is bound
  • Adoption is the date from which the delay is measured
  • Signature at the conference is usually subject to ratification and is not consent to be bound
4. Stage three - acceptance, and the four ways of giving it
  • Signature subject to ratification, acceptance or approval - a two-step route; the gap is where national legislation is done
  • Definitive signature, where permitted - consent in one step; rare for technical conventions
  • Accession - a single act by a State that did not sign, with identical legal effect to ratification
  • In every case the instrument is DEPOSITED with the Secretary-General as depositary
  • It is the date of deposit, not the date of the national law, that counts
  • Ratification is the END of a long domestic process, not a formality
5. Stage four - entry into force
  • Every convention contains its own entry-into-force article - that article decides, not the Organization
  • For an important technical convention: a minimum number of Contracting States, commonly 15 to 30
  • Plus a minimum percentage of world merchant shipping by gross tonnage, commonly 50 per cent
  • Occasionally a third subject-specific criterion - the Hong Kong Convention's recycling capacity
  • Then a prescribed period, commonly 12 or 24 months, before it takes effect
  • The numerical condition secures legitimacy; the tonnage condition secures practical effect - and causes the wait
6. Why the delay happens
  • STRUCTURAL: demanding thresholds by design; conflicting national interests; the Organization cannot compel
  • NATIONAL: parliamentary time for enabling legislation; administrative capacity to survey, certify and prosecute; legal and linguistic work; political change
  • INDUSTRIAL: the technology may not exist or be affordable at adoption - the BWM case
  • INDUSTRIAL: cost, and the first mover bearing it before his competitors
  • INDUSTRIAL: the free-rider problem - each State gains slightly by going last, which is the economic root
  • INDUSTRIAL: uncertainty about interpretation where guidelines are still being written
7. The delay, in worked examples
  • SOLAS 1974 - adopted 1 November 1974, in force 25 May 1980: about five and a half years
  • MARPOL - the 1973 Convention never entered into force alone; absorbed into the 1978 Protocol, Annexes I and II in force 2 October 1983
  • Bunkers 2001 - adopted 23 March 2001, in force 21 November 2008: nearly eight years, with no technology requirement
  • Maritime Liens and Mortgages 1993 - adopted 6 May 1993, in force 5 September 2004: eleven years
  • Nairobi Wreck Removal 2007 - adopted 18 May 2007, in force 14 April 2015: nearly eight years
  • Ballast Water Management 2004 - adopted 13 February 2004, in force 8 September 2017: thirteen years, technology and cost
  • Hong Kong Convention 2009 - adopted 15 May 2009, conditions satisfied June 2023, TWO MONTHS BEFORE THIS SITTING; twenty-four-month clock running; NOT IN FORCE today
  • And some never arrive - the HNS regime and the Cape Town Agreement
  • Typical range five to fifteen years, driven by cost to industry and by how concentrated tonnage is
8. What can be done to reduce the delay
  • TACIT ACCEPTANCE - the burden reversed, silence means yes; MARPOL article 16 and the SOLAS equivalent. The single most effective reform
  • Make a standard binding through a convention already in force - the ISM, ISPS, RO and III Codes all arrived this way
  • Amend by protocol where an instrument has stalled - the 1978 Protocol rescued MARPOL; the 1988 Protocols modernised SOLAS and Load Lines
  • Technical co-operation, model national legislation and regional workshops, attacking the national-capacity cause
  • The Member State Audit Scheme and the III Code, raising the capability ratification depends on
  • Publish guidelines WITH the instrument - States will not ratify into interpretive uncertainty
  • Propositions: realistic thresholds at drafting; phasing and grandfathering; encourage early voluntary application; do not adopt requirements depending on undemonstrated technology; targeted diplomacy with major flags; accept regional pressure as part of the mechanism, at the cost of fragmentation
Model written answer

1. What an IMO convention is, and the four stages

The Organization has no power to make law. It is a forum in which States negotiate treaties and a secretariat that administers them; the binding force of every IMO convention comes from the consent of the States that become party to it, and from the national legislation each of them then enacts. Everything in this question follows from that, including the delay.

Name the four stages before explaining any of them:

  1. Proposal and development - a Member State or group of States raises the matter, and the technical work is done in the committee and sub-committee structure.
  2. Adoption - the finished text is adopted, normally by a diplomatic conference convened for the purpose.
  3. Acceptance - States individually express their consent to be bound, by ratification, acceptance, approval or accession, depositing an instrument with the Secretary-General as depositary.
  4. Entry into force - when the conditions written into the convention itself have been satisfied, and the prescribed period after that has run.

What follows the fourth stage is implementation and enforcement - national legislation, administrative machinery, surveyors, certificates and penalties. That is not a fifth stage of the convention; it is what each Party must already have done before it could sensibly ratify, and it is one of the reasons the third stage takes so long.

2. Stage one - proposal and development

Who may start it. A Member State, or a group of them, submits a proposal to the appropriate committee - the Maritime Safety Committee for safety, the Marine Environment Protection Committee for pollution, the Legal Committee for liability and compensation, the Facilitation Committee for documentary matters. In practice a proposal follows a casualty, a scientific finding or a commercial development; much of the modern instrument set can be traced to a specific disaster.

What happens. The committee decides whether the matter justifies a new instrument, and if so places it on its work programme with a target completion date. The detailed technical drafting is done in a sub-committee, over several sessions, with industry and non-governmental organisations in consultative status contributing expertise. The output returns to the parent committee, which settles the text.

Why this stage is slow, and legitimately so. The text must be technically sound, must work for every trade and every size of ship, and must be acceptable to States with radically different interests - large flags, coastal States with a pollution exposure, developing States with limited administrative capacity, and shipbuilding States. A text pushed through this stage against unresolved objections is a text that States will not later ratify, so speed here is a false economy.

3. Stage two - adoption

Adoption fixes the text. A new convention is normally adopted by a diplomatic conference convened by the Organization, to which all States are invited - including States that are not Members of IMO, because a treaty is open to States generally. Amendments to an existing convention, and codes, are adopted by the relevant committee or by the Assembly, by resolution.

Three points that are commonly got wrong:

  • Adoption creates no obligation on anybody. The text exists; nobody is bound by it. A State that voted for adoption is not thereby a Party.
  • Adoption is the date from which the delay is measured, and it is the date the stem's second limb is about.
  • Signature at the conference is usually 'subject to ratification' and is not consent to be bound - it is an indication of intent and an undertaking not to defeat the object of the treaty in the meantime. Consent comes later and separately.

4. Stage three - acceptance, and the four ways of giving it

This is where the delay actually accumulates, so it is worth setting out precisely how a State becomes bound. The convention is opened for signature for a stated period, and thereafter remains open for accession.

  • Signature subject to ratification, acceptance or approval. A two-step route. The State signs, indicating agreement with the text and an intention to proceed; it becomes bound only when it later deposits its instrument of ratification (or acceptance, or approval - the three differ in the internal constitutional process each State uses and not in their international effect). The gap between signature and ratification is where the national legislative work is done.
  • Definitive signature, where a convention permits it - signature not subject to ratification, which expresses consent in one step. Rare for technical conventions.
  • Accession. A single act by which a State that has not signed - typically because the signature period has closed - becomes a Party. It has exactly the same legal effect as ratification and is the route by which most States join an instrument that has been open for years.
  • Deposit with the depositary. In every case the instrument is deposited with the Secretary-General of the Organization, who is the depositary, keeps the count and informs all States. It is the date of deposit, not the date of the national law, that counts - which is a common examination point.

The essential proposition to state here is that ratification is not a formality but the end of a long domestic process. Before a State can responsibly deposit an instrument it must have enacted the enabling legislation, given itself the powers to survey, certify, detain and prosecute, provided or authorized the surveyors, and satisfied itself that its industry can comply.

5. Stage four - entry into force

Every convention contains its own entry-into-force article, and it is that article, not the Organization, that decides when the treaty becomes law. For a convention of important technical nature the condition is deliberately demanding and normally has two elements, sometimes three:

  • a minimum number of Contracting States - typically between fifteen and thirty; and
  • those States between them representing a minimum percentage of world merchant shipping by gross tonnage - commonly 50 per cent, sometimes lower; and
  • occasionally a third, subject-specific criterion. The Hong Kong Convention is the standard example, requiring in addition a combined annual ship recycling capacity among the Contracting States - because a recycling convention ratified only by States that do no recycling would regulate nothing.

When the last condition is satisfied, the convention does not enter into force at once. A prescribed period runs - commonly twelve or twenty-four months - so that the newly bound States, the industry and the classification societies have time to prepare. Entry into force is general: the convention then binds all Parties. A State ratifying afterwards is bound a stated period after its own deposit.

State the purpose of the double condition, because it explains the delay rather than merely describing it: the numerical condition secures political legitimacy and the tonnage condition secures practical effect. A convention in force among thirty States owning two per cent of the world fleet would regulate almost no ships, and would put those States' owners at a competitive disadvantage against everybody else. The tonnage threshold is what prevents that - and it is also precisely what makes the wait so long, because it means the instrument cannot take effect until the major flag States have moved.

6. Why the delay happens

Separate the causes into three groups, because they have three different remedies.

Structural causes - built into the treaty system itself.

  • The entry-into-force conditions are demanding by design, for the reason just given, and satisfying a tonnage threshold means waiting for a handful of large flag States whose own processes are not quick.
  • Unanimity of interest is impossible. The States that gain from an instrument and the States that pay for it are frequently different States.
  • The Organization cannot compel anybody. It can promote, assist and publish; it has no power to require ratification and no sanction for not ratifying.

National causes - inside each State.

  • Enabling legislation takes parliamentary time, and maritime matters compete for it with everything else a government is doing. In many States the delay is simply that nobody has scheduled the Bill.
  • Administrative capacity. A State cannot honestly ratify until it can survey, certify, inspect, detain and prosecute under the new instrument - which means trained surveyors, revised forms, delegated authority to recognized organizations and a means of enforcement.
  • Legal and linguistic work - translation, reconciliation with existing national law, and the constitutional process each State's system requires.
  • Political change. A government that intended to ratify may be replaced by one with other priorities.

Industrial and economic causes - and this is the group candidates usually miss.

  • The technology may not exist, or may not be affordable, at adoption. The Ballast Water Management Convention is the outstanding case: it required treatment systems that had to be developed, type-approved and manufactured at scale, and States were reasonably unwilling to bind their fleets to a requirement that could not yet be met.
  • Cost, and who bears it first. Compliance costs money, and an owner whose flag ratifies early bears it before his competitors do.
  • The free-rider problem, which is the economic root of the whole difficulty. Every State benefits if the instrument comes into force; each individual State benefits slightly more by being late. That is a rational calculation for each State and a bad outcome for all of them, and it is why the delay is a structural feature rather than mere inefficiency.
  • Uncertainty about interpretation. Where the guidelines needed to apply an instrument are still being written, States hold back rather than ratify into ambiguity - which is a very common and quite reasonable cause of delay.

7. The delay, in worked examples

Give examples with dates, because the stem asks for them, and be careful to state each as it stands at this sitting.

  • SOLAS 1974 - adopted 1 November 1974, in force 25 May 1980. About five and a half years, and this is the quick end of the range for a major technical convention.
  • MARPOL - the 1973 Convention never entered into force on its own and had to be absorbed into the 1978 Protocol; Annexes I and II came into force on 2 October 1983, ten years after the original adoption. A good illustration that a stalled instrument is sometimes rescued by re-packaging rather than by waiting.
  • Bunkers Convention 2001 - adopted 23 March 2001, in force 21 November 2008. Nearly eight years, for a short liability convention with no technology requirement at all.
  • Maritime Liens and Mortgages 1993 - adopted 6 May 1993, in force 5 September 2004. Eleven years - and a reminder that a low-cost instrument can still wait a decade if no State feels urgency about it.
  • Nairobi Wreck Removal Convention 2007 - adopted 18 May 2007, in force 14 April 2015. Nearly eight years.
  • Ballast Water Management Convention 2004 - adopted 13 February 2004, in force 8 September 2017. Thirteen years, and the clearest case of technology and cost being the cause: the treatment systems and their type approval did not exist in usable form at adoption.
  • Hong Kong Convention 2009 - and this one is live as this paper is being sat. Adopted 15 May 2009. Its three-part condition - States, tonnage and recycling capacity - remained unsatisfied for fourteen years, because the tonnage and the recycling capacity are held by different groups of States. The conditions were satisfied in June 2023, two months before this examination, and the twenty-four-month period is now running, so the Convention will enter into force in June 2025. It is not in force today. Use it as the example of an instrument that has finally cleared the hurdle and is still waiting.
  • And the counter-examples that have not cleared it at all - the HNS regime and the Cape Town Agreement on fishing vessel safety, both adopted years ago and both still not in force. Some instruments never arrive.

The pattern is worth stating in a sentence: a technical convention typically takes between five and fifteen years, the length being driven by how much it costs the industry and how concentrated the tonnage is among States that are slow to move.

8. What can be done to reduce the delay

Answer this limb in two halves - what the Organization has already done, which is the stronger half, and what more could be done.

Established measures that work.

  • Tacit acceptance - the single most effective reform in the Organization's history. Under the classical explicit acceptance procedure an amendment entered into force only when a specified proportion of Parties had positively accepted it, which meant that amendments took as long as conventions and technical requirements could not be kept current. Under tacit acceptance, provided for in MARPOL article 16 and in the corresponding SOLAS provision, an adopted amendment is deemed accepted on a stated date unless a specified proportion of Parties objects, and enters into force a stated period later. The burden is reversed: silence now means yes. This is why SOLAS and MARPOL amendments enter into force on a predictable cycle of a few years while new conventions take a decade.
  • Make a new standard binding through an instrument already in force. Rather than negotiating a new convention, the Organization adopts the substance as a code and then makes it mandatory by amending SOLAS or MARPOL under tacit acceptance. The ISM Code, the ISPS Code, the RO Code and the III Code all arrived this way. It is the most powerful practical answer to the delay and it should be named.
  • Amend by protocol. Where a convention has stalled or needs substantial change, a protocol attached to it - as the 1978 Protocol rescued MARPOL, and the 1988 Protocols modernised SOLAS and Load Lines - can carry the new content with its own, more achievable entry-into-force conditions.
  • Technical co-operation and capacity building. The Organization's technical co-operation programme, model national legislation, regional workshops and the support given to developing Administrations directly attack the national-capacity cause, which is the largest single cause for the majority of States.
  • The Member State Audit Scheme and the III Code, which make each State's implementation performance visible and auditable, and so raise the capability that ratification depends on.
  • Publish the guidelines with the instrument, not years afterwards. The BWM experience showed that States will not ratify into interpretive uncertainty; producing unified interpretations and guidelines in parallel with the text removes a real and reasonable reason to wait.

What more could be done - stated as propositions rather than as settled policy.

  • Set realistic entry-into-force conditions when drafting. A threshold designed to guarantee effectiveness can guarantee paralysis instead. The Hong Kong Convention's fourteen-year wait is the argument, and its third criterion is exactly the kind of condition that should be examined at drafting rather than regretted afterwards.
  • Phase the requirements and grandfather existing ships, so that the cost shock at entry into force is smaller and States are readier to bind themselves.
  • Allow, or encourage, voluntary early application by flags, owners, charterers and classification societies. Industry can and does apply a standard before it is law, and doing so removes the first-mover disadvantage by making the standard commercially normal in advance.
  • Keep the technology honest. Do not adopt a requirement that depends on equipment which has not been demonstrated and type-approved, because the instrument will then wait for the equipment rather than the other way round.
  • Targeted diplomacy with the major flag States, since the tonnage condition means a small number of registries can determine the date single-handedly.
  • Accept that regional and unilateral measures are part of the mechanism. Where a region legislates ahead of the international instrument, it creates commercial pressure that accelerates ratification - at the cost of a period of fragmented regulation, which is a real price and should be acknowledged as one.

Close on the proposition the whole question turns on: the delay is not administrative inefficiency. It is the price of a system in which no State can be bound without its own consent - and every serious remedy works either by lowering the cost of consenting or by removing the need to consent again.

Study guide

Why this structure scores

Three tasks are printed as three sentences and they are not equal. The four stages are recall and can be written quickly; the delay with examples needs dates; and 'what can be done to reduce this delay' is an evaluative limb that most candidates have never prepared. The structure gives one section to naming the stages, four to explaining them, one to causes, one to examples and one to remedies - and the last two are where the answer is actually won.

Section 6's division into structural, national and industrial causes is the piece of thinking that makes section 8 possible. Once the causes are in three groups, the remedies fall out of them: tacit acceptance and the code-through-convention technique answer the structural group, technical co-operation and the Audit Scheme answer the national group, and phasing, early voluntary application and technology realism answer the industrial group. A candidate who lists causes at random cannot then produce a coherent remedy list.

Giving exactly four stages matters. The stem says four. Implementation and enforcement are named in section 1 as what FOLLOWS the fourth, which shows the examiner that the omission is deliberate rather than ignorant - and it also sets up the point in section 4 that domestic implementation must largely precede ratification, which is one of the answer's better observations.

Common mistakes

  • Giving five or six stages. The stem asks for four. Name implementation separately as what follows.
  • Saying IMO makes law. It does not. The binding force comes from States' consent and their national legislation, and this proposition explains the entire delay.
  • Treating signature as consent to be bound. Signature subject to ratification is an indication of intent; consent comes on deposit of the instrument.
  • Confusing ratification with accession. They have identical legal effect; accession is the route for a State that did not sign.
  • Giving no examples, or giving them without dates. The stem says 'with examples' and a date is what makes an example evidence.
  • Saying the Hong Kong Convention is in force. At this sitting it is not - see the traps.
  • Describing the delay as inefficiency. It is structural, and the free-rider problem is the reason.
  • Omitting tacit acceptance from the remedies. It is the single most effective answer the Organization has ever found and no answer to limb three is complete without it.
  • Forgetting that the tonnage threshold is what makes the wait long AND what makes the convention worth having.

Examiner traps

  • The Hong Kong Convention is NOT in force at this sitting. Adopted 15 May 2009; its conditions were satisfied only in June 2023, two months before this paper, and the twenty-four-month period then began to run, so it enters into force in June 2025. The circulating solved answers are all written for a 2025 or 2026 sitting and treats it as a completed example. Here it is the live one - which is a better example, not a worse one.
  • Tacit acceptance applies to AMENDMENTS, not to new conventions. Expect to be asked why, if tacit acceptance works so well, new conventions still take a decade. Because a State cannot be deemed to have accepted a treaty it is not already party to.
  • Adoption creates no obligation on anyone. A State that voted for a text at the conference is not a Party.
  • It is the date of DEPOSIT of the instrument that counts, not the date of the national legislation.
  • Ratification, acceptance and approval differ only in each State's internal constitutional process, not in international effect.
  • The Hong Kong Convention's third criterion is recycling capacity - a subject-specific condition, and the standard example of a threshold that guarantees relevance and causes delay in equal measure.
  • MARPOL 1973 never entered into force on its own. It was absorbed into the 1978 Protocol. Expect this as a follow-up on why MARPOL is written '73/78'.
  • Some conventions never arrive at all - the HNS regime and the Cape Town Agreement are the current examples. An answer that implies every adopted convention eventually enters into force is wrong.

Likely oral follow-up

  • Name the four stages. Now tell me at which of them a State first becomes bound.
  • What is the difference between ratification and accession?
  • You have signed a convention. Are you bound by it?
  • Why do entry-into-force conditions include a tonnage percentage as well as a number of States?
  • Why did the Ballast Water Convention take thirteen years?
  • Is the Hong Kong Convention in force? When does it come in, and what held it up?
  • What is tacit acceptance and what problem was it invented to solve?
  • How did the ISM Code become mandatory without a new convention?
  • If you were drafting a new convention today, what would you do differently to get it into force faster?
  • Does a region legislating ahead of IMO help or hurt?

Memory framework

The four stages are PROPOSE - ADOPT - ACCEPT - ENTER INTO FORCE, and implementation follows. For the four ways of consenting hold SIGN-THEN-RATIFY / DEFINITIVE SIGNATURE / ACCEPTANCE OR APPROVAL / ACCESSION, all deposited with the Secretary-General. For the causes hold three groups: STRUCTURAL / NATIONAL / INDUSTRIAL. For the examples hold four numbers - SOLAS 5½, Bunkers 8, Maritime Liens 11, BWM 13 - and one live case, the Hong Kong Convention at fourteen years and still waiting. For the remedies hold TACIT ACCEPTANCE - CODE THROUGH CONVENTION - PROTOCOL - CAPACITY - AUDIT - GUIDELINES WITH THE TEXT.

Regulation and source map

  • The IMO Convention and the committee structure - Assembly, Council, MSC, MEPC, Legal, Facilitation and Technical Co-operation Committees, with sub-committees beneath MSC and MEPC.
  • MARPOL article 16 and the corresponding SOLAS provision - the tacit acceptance amendment procedure. Cited by identity and mechanism; no threshold figure or period is asserted.
  • Hong Kong Convention 2009 - adopted 15 May 2009; three-part entry-into-force condition of States, tonnage and recycling capacity; conditions satisfied June 2023; in force 26 June 2025 - FUTURE at this sitting.
  • SOLAS 1974 - adopted 1 November 1974, in force 25 May 1980.
  • MARPOL 73/78 - Annexes I and II in force 2 October 1983.
  • Bunkers Convention 2001 - adopted 23 March 2001, in force 21 November 2008.
  • Nairobi Wreck Removal Convention 2007 - adopted 18 May 2007, in force 14 April 2015.
  • Ballast Water Management Convention 2004 - adopted 13 February 2004, in force 8 September 2017.
  • Maritime Liens and Mortgages 1993 - adopted 6 May 1993, in force 5 September 2004.
  • III Code, A.1070(28), and the IMO Member State Audit Scheme - the implementation-capability half of the problem.
  • The Integrated Technical Cooperation Programme and model national legislation.

Uncertainty and currency

  • No treaty status figure is asserted for any instrument - no number of Contracting States and no percentage of world tonnage at this sitting. Such figures move continuously and we hold no treaty-status list. Thresholds are described as the instrument's own condition, never as the position reached.
  • We hold no copy of the Hong Kong Convention. Its adoption date, the shape of its three-part condition and the twenty-four-month period are carried from our own verified records; no article is quoted.
  • we hold MARPOL at citation level and not as quotable text. Article 16 is cited by identity and by mechanism; no objection threshold or period is stated as a figure.
  • The example dates are carried from our own verified records, not read at a depositary source for this paper. They are stable historical facts but they were not independently re-verified here.
  • The remedies are partly established practice and partly analysis, and the answer separates the two. Tacit acceptance, the code-through-convention technique, protocols, technical co-operation and the Audit Scheme are established; realistic thresholds, phasing, early voluntary application and the acceptance of regional pressure are propositions about what would help.
Recall the structure

Before you read the answer: can you name all 8 sections, in order? Say them or write them down, then reveal.

  1. __________What an IMO convention is, and the four stages
  2. __________Stage one - proposal and development
  3. __________Stage two - adoption
  4. __________Stage three - acceptance, and the four ways of giving it
  5. __________Stage four - entry into force
  6. __________Why the delay happens
  7. __________The delay, in worked examples
  8. __________What can be done to reduce the delay
Flashcards 10

Proposal and development in the committee and sub-committee structure; adoption, normally by a diplomatic conference; acceptance, by which individual States express consent to be bound; and entry into force, when the convention's own conditions are satisfied and the prescribed period has run. What follows is implementation and enforcement - national legislation, surveyors, certificates and penalties - which is not a fifth stage and must largely precede ratification anyway.

Ratification follows a signature already given, completing a two-step route; accession is a single act by a State that never signed, typically because the signature period closed. Their legal effect is identical. Signature subject to ratification does NOT bind the State - it indicates intent and an undertaking not to defeat the treaty's object. Consent takes effect on deposit of the instrument with the Secretary-General as depositary.

A minimum number of Contracting States, commonly fifteen to thirty, together with those States representing a minimum percentage of world merchant shipping by gross tonnage, commonly around fifty per cent; occasionally a third subject-specific criterion. Then a prescribed period of twelve or twenty-four months. The numerical condition secures political legitimacy; the tonnage condition secures practical effect, since a convention binding States with two per cent of the fleet would regulate almost nothing.

Every State is better off if the convention comes into force, and each individual State is slightly better off being late, because whoever ratifies first imposes compliance costs on its own owners before their competitors bear them. That is a rational calculation for each State and a bad outcome for all of them. It is why the delay is a structural feature of a consent-based system rather than administrative inefficiency.

SOLAS 1974 - adopted 1 November 1974, in force 25 May 1980, about five and a half years. Bunkers Convention 2001 - adopted 23 March 2001, in force 21 November 2008, nearly eight years, with no technology requirement at all. Maritime Liens and Mortgages 1993 - adopted 6 May 1993, in force 5 September 2004, eleven years. Ballast Water Management 2004 - adopted 13 February 2004, in force 8 September 2017, thirteen years.

No. It was adopted 15 May 2009 and its three-part condition - States, tonnage and combined annual ship recycling capacity - remained unsatisfied for fourteen years. The conditions were met in June 2023, two months before this examination, and the twenty-four-month period then began to run, so it enters into force on 26 June 2025. It is the paper's best example of an instrument that has cleared its hurdle and is still waiting.

Under explicit acceptance an amendment entered into force only when a specified proportion of Parties had positively accepted it, so amendments took as long as conventions and technical requirements could not be kept current. Under tacit acceptance - MARPOL article 16 and the corresponding SOLAS provision - an adopted amendment is deemed accepted on a stated date unless a specified proportion of Parties objects, and enters into force a stated period later. The burden is reversed: silence means yes.

By being adopted as a code and then made mandatory through an amendment to SOLAS under the tacit acceptance procedure - the same route as the ISPS Code, the RO Code and the III Code. It matters because it is the most powerful practical answer to the delay: the substance takes effect through an instrument already in force and with near-universal participation, so no State has to be asked to ratify anything new.

Because tacit acceptance applies only to AMENDMENTS of an instrument a State is already party to. A State cannot be deemed to have accepted a treaty it has never joined - that would bind it without its consent, which is the one thing the system cannot do. New conventions therefore still require individual ratification or accession by enough States, and enough tonnage, one at a time.

Established: tacit acceptance for amendments; adopting a standard as a code and making it binding through a convention already in force; and amending by protocol where an instrument has stalled, as the 1978 Protocol rescued MARPOL. Also established are technical co-operation and the Member State Audit Scheme. Proposals: set realistic entry-into-force thresholds at drafting; phase requirements and grandfather existing ships; and encourage early voluntary application by flags, owners, charterers and class.

Quick revision
15-second recall
PROPOSE - ADOPT - ACCEPT - ENTER INTO FORCE. IMO makes no law; States consent and legislate. Adoption binds nobody. Consent is by ratification, acceptance, approval or accession, on deposit with the Secretary-General. Entry into force needs a number of States plus a tonnage percentage, then 12 or 24 months. Delay is structural, national and industrial, and its root is the free-rider problem. Examples: SOLAS 5½ years, Bunkers 8, Liens 11, BWM 13, Hong Kong 14 and still waiting. Cures: tacit acceptance, code-through-convention, protocol, capacity building, guidelines with the text.
