Critical regulation
MARPOL article 16 - tacit acceptance. If you remember one thing in limb three, remember what it changed: under explicit acceptance an amendment waited for positive acceptances and took as long as a convention, so technical requirements could not be kept current. Under tacit acceptance the amendment is deemed accepted unless enough Parties object. The burden is reversed and silence now means yes - and that is why SOLAS and MARPOL amendments arrive on a predictable few-year cycle while new conventions take a decade.
Major trap
Saying the Hong Kong Convention is in force, or that it 'took sixteen years'. At this sitting it is not in force: adopted 15 May 2009, its conditions were satisfied only in June 2023, two months before this paper, and it enters into force on 26 June 2025. The circulating solved answers are all written for a 2025 or 2026 sitting and treats it as completed. Here it is the live example - and it is a better illustration of the stem for exactly that reason.

Also on the platform: QP2308 Q2 - the Maritime Liens and Mortgages Convention 1993, which took eleven years to enter into force and is one of this answer's worked examples · QP2308 Q5 - the RO Code, adopted as a standard and then made binding by amending conventions already in force: a remedy for delay in action · QP2308 Q9 - the III Code and the Member State Audit Scheme, which address the implementation half of the same problem

Once in this set Set once in the sittings MIW has transcribed.

Understand this first

A certificate says one thing and only one thing: somebody competent looked at this ship and found her to comply. The difficulty is that the looking happened on a particular day and the certificate then has to stay believable for five years, during which the ship trades, corrodes, is loaded and unloaded and is sometimes damaged. Everything in this question is the machinery for keeping that statement honest. Most of it is a rhythm. Every year somebody comes and has a general look to see that nothing has gone backwards - quick, mostly visual, and it does not produce a certificate, it just keeps the existing one alive. Once in the middle of the five years the look goes deeper, because a walk round the deck will never find what is happening inside a ballast tank. And at the end of the five years the ship is examined properly from end to end, docked, measured for wasted steel, machinery opened up - and only that survey asks the real question, which is whether she is fit for the next five years. That is why only that one produces a new certificate. But two of the five surveys the examiner names have nothing to do with any of this rhythm, and noticing that is most of the question. When a ship hits something, catches fire, or breaks down, nobody waits for an anniversary. Somebody comes to find out what is broken and how bad it is - and then, separately, somebody comes to confirm that the fixing was done properly. Those two are called by events, not by the calendar. And there is a subtlety in the second of them worth holding: what is being approved is not just that the steel is now sound but that the repair was done to an agreed procedure and watched while it was done. A superb repair nobody witnessed does not clear anything, because the point of the whole system is not that the ship is good but that somebody accountable has said so.

Model written answer

1. The distinction that organises the whole answer

The stem names five surveys in one breath, which invites the candidate to describe them as five stages of one cycle. They are not. Say so first, because it is the discrimination the question is really testing.

  • Annual, intermediate and renewal are PERIODICAL surveys. They are calendar-driven, they recur on a fixed cycle anchored to the ship's anniversary date, they are what keep her certificates valid, and they are the surveys the harmonized system was built to co-ordinate.
  • Damage and repair surveys are OCCASIONAL surveys. They are event-driven. They are not part of the cycle, they appear in no schedule, and their timing is decided by what has happened to the ship rather than by the calendar.

A second distinction runs across all five and should be stated once rather than repeated: every one of them may be performed either as a statutory survey, on behalf of the flag Administration under a convention, or as a class survey, under the society's own rules - and in practice the two are carried out together by the same surveyor on the same visit. The findings, however, go to different places: a statutory finding affects a convention certificate, a class finding produces a condition of class.

2. Annual survey

  • When. Every year, within a window of three months before or after the anniversary date of the certificate. It is the lightest and most frequent of the periodical surveys.
  • Purpose. To confirm that the ship, her equipment and her arrangements remain in a satisfactory condition and have not been altered without approval - in short, that nothing has changed for the worse since the last survey.
  • Scope. A general examination, largely visual and afloat, of the hull, closing appliances, freeboard marks, means of access, life-saving appliances, fire-fighting arrangements, machinery in operation, safety systems, pollution prevention arrangements and the ship's documentation. Selected equipment is tested in operation. It is deliberately not an opening-up survey.
  • What it produces. An endorsement on the existing certificate. The annual survey does not issue a certificate; it keeps one alive. An annual survey not completed within its window renders the certificate invalid, and that is the practical point that matters to a Chief Engineer.

3. Intermediate survey

  • When. Once in each five-year certificate period, taking the place of either the second or the third annual survey - so at roughly the mid-point of the cycle.
  • Purpose. To provide a deeper check part-way through the period, so that five years do not elapse between one thorough examination and the next. It exists because a purely visual annual survey cannot detect deterioration that develops inside tanks and structure.
  • Scope. Everything in an annual survey, plus additional examination - internal examination of selected ballast and cargo spaces, thickness measurement where the surveyor considers it necessary, more extensive testing of machinery and safety systems, and, on ships under the enhanced survey programme, a defined programme of tank examinations.
  • What it produces. An endorsement on the certificate in place of the annual endorsement. It does not produce a new certificate either.

4. Renewal survey

  • When. At intervals not exceeding five years, completed before the certificate expires. It is also called the periodical or special survey, and in class practice it may be carried out progressively over the final years of the cycle rather than in one attendance.
  • Purpose. To establish that the ship is fit in every respect for the next five years of service. This is the only survey that asks that question. It is a complete re-examination, not a confirmation that nothing has changed.
  • Scope. The most extensive of the periodical surveys: examination of the structure inside and out, including drydocking or an approved in-water survey; internal examination of tanks and spaces; thickness measurement to establish wasted material, with substantial corrosion identified and the affected areas either renewed or brought under a defined monitoring regime; testing of tanks; opening up and examination of machinery, shafting and safety devices; overhaul or examination of boilers and pressure vessels; and full testing of the fire, life-saving and pollution prevention arrangements.
  • What it produces. A new certificate, valid for a further five years. It is the only one of the five surveys that does.

One practical point completes this section, because it is frequently examined: the bottom of the ship must be examined twice in every five-year period, one of those examinations being the renewal survey's docking, with a maximum interval between them - so drydocking is on a rhythm of its own within the cycle.

5. Damage survey

  • When. Whenever the ship has suffered damage or a defect affecting her safety, her structural integrity, her class or the validity of a statutory certificate - grounding, contact, collision, fire, heavy weather damage, cargo or ballast tank failure, main engine or steering gear breakdown, shafting or rudder damage. It is not scheduled and there is no window.
  • Purpose. To establish what is damaged, how badly, and what must be done about it before the ship proceeds. It is a diagnostic survey: it determines the extent of the damage, its cause where that can be established, whether the ship remains fit to proceed and on what conditions, and what repair is required.
  • Scope. Confined to the damage and to anything the damage may have affected - and that qualification carries a mark, because grounding damage forward can affect tank boundaries, piping and structure well away from the visible dent.
  • Who calls it. The owner or master must report the damage - there is a standing duty to report a casualty or defect affecting the ship's safety or the efficiency or completeness of her equipment - and this is the survey candidates most often describe as though the surveyor turns up by himself. He does not; he is called.
  • What it produces. A survey report, a condition of class or a recommendation with a date by which the repair must be completed, and, where the damage is serious, a decision that the certificate is suspended or that the ship may proceed only to a repair port, in ballast, in fair weather, or under tow.
  • Insurance dimension. It also produces the technical evidence on which a hull and machinery claim will turn, and the surveyor's report is the document underwriters, average adjusters and, if it comes to it, a court will read.

6. Repair survey

  • When. During and after repair work - whether the repair follows a damage survey, is planned at a docking, or is the renewal of wasted structure found at a renewal survey. It commonly follows a damage survey but is a separate survey, and the difference between the two is the difference between diagnosis and treatment.
  • Purpose. To satisfy the society and the Administration that the repair has been carried out to an approved procedure, with approved materials, by qualified people, to the required standard, so that the ship is restored to the condition the certificate asserts.
  • Scope. Approval of the repair procedure before work begins; approval of materials and of welding procedures and welder qualifications; attendance during the work at agreed hold points; non-destructive examination of welds; and testing on completion - tank testing, pressure testing, operational testing of repaired machinery, and sea trials where the repair affects propulsion or steering.
  • What it produces. A report clearing the condition of class or recommendation that the damage survey raised, and the restoration or re-endorsement of the affected certificate.
  • The point that distinguishes a good answer. A repair carried out without the survey - even a technically excellent one - does not clear the condition, because what is being certified is not only that the steel is sound but that it was done under approval and witnessed. Emergency repairs at sea are accepted as temporary and must be reported and surveyed at the first opportunity.

7. The harmonized system that ties the first three together

The three periodical surveys are not three independent regimes, and saying why earns marks. Before harmonization each convention ran its own survey cycle on its own dates, so a ship could face a Load Line survey in one port, a Safety Equipment survey two months later and an IOPP survey two months after that, with certificates of different lengths expiring at different times.

The Harmonized System of Survey and Certification, introduced by the 1988 Protocols to SOLAS and to the Load Line Convention and in force from 3 February 2000, fixed that:

  • a common five-year period for the principal certificates;
  • a single anniversary date for the ship, to which every annual and intermediate survey is referred;
  • three-month windows either side of the anniversary date, so that surveys can be planned around the ship's trading pattern rather than dictating it;
  • a common vocabulary of initial, annual, intermediate, periodical, renewal and additional surveys across the instruments; and
  • provision for a certificate to be extended by a short period to allow a ship to complete a voyage to a port where the survey can be carried out - with the replacement certificate still dated from the original expiry, so that the extension does not buy extra time.

The operative survey guidance at this sitting is the Assembly's Survey Guidelines under the Harmonized System of Survey and Certification, resolution A.1156(32), which sets out for each certificate what the surveyor is to examine at each type of survey.

Damage and repair surveys sit entirely outside this scheme, which is why they have no windows, no anniversary and no guidelines of this kind - and that observation closes the first half of the question.

8. The statutory certificates, their issuing authority and their convention

One standing note covers the whole register and saves repeating it in every line: the issuing authority is the flag State Administration in every case, and in practice most of these are issued by a Recognized Organization acting on the Administration's behalf under a specific delegation. Where a certificate is not delegable, or is not issued by the flag at all, the register says so.

Now the part that separates a strong answer. The stem asks for the IMO Convention under which they are issued - and several documents carried on board are not issued under an IMO convention at all. Name them and say what they are issued under:

  • Certificate of Registry - issued by the flag State registry under national law, in India the Merchant Shipping Act, 1958. It is a document of nationality and title, not a technical certificate, and no IMO convention issues it.
  • Maritime Labour Certificate, and the Declaration of Maritime Labour Compliance Parts I and II - issued under MLC 2006, which is an International Labour Organization convention, not an IMO one. Part I states the flag State's own national requirements and is the flag's alone; Part II sets out the shipowner's measures and may be certified by an RO.
  • Ship Sanitation Control Certificate, or Ship Sanitation Control Exemption Certificate - issued by a port health authority under the International Health Regulations 2005, a World Health Organization instrument.
  • Certificates of competency, proficiency and endorsements - issued to seafarers by the Administration under STCW. An IMO convention, but the certificates are personal to the seafarer and are not ship certificates.
  • Certificate of Class - issued by the classification society under its own rules. It is not a statutory certificate at all, and it is the item most often wrongly included in this list. It is nonetheless indispensable, because most statutory certificates presuppose that the ship is in class.

Add the honest qualification: which certificates a particular ship carries depends on her type, size, trade and flag - a chemical tanker carries a Certificate of Fitness a bulk carrier does not, and a ship trading solely in domestic waters may carry national certificates in place of international ones.

Close on the proposition that connects the two halves of the question: a certificate is a statement that the ship was found to comply on a particular day. The periodical surveys keep that statement believable over five years; the occasional surveys are what happens when something occurs that makes it untrue.

Study guide

Why this structure scores

The stem names five surveys as a list and the commonest failure is to describe them as five stages of one cycle. Section 1 refuses that in three lines - annual, intermediate and renewal are periodical and calendar-driven; damage and repair are occasional and event-driven - and that single distinction is worth more than any amount of detail about any one of them, because it is the discrimination the examiner set the question to test.

Sections 2 to 6 are deliberately parallel: when, purpose, scope, what it produces. The parallel structure lets a marker compare five surveys instantly, gives the candidate a template under time pressure, and makes one crucial fact visible - only the renewal survey produces a new certificate, while the annual and intermediate merely endorse one. That is a comparison an unstructured answer never quite makes.

In section 8 the table is the answer to the second limb, but the bulleted list after it is what earns the marks. The stem says 'the IMO Convention under which they are issued', and knowing that the Certificate of Registry is national, MLC is ILO, the Ship Sanitation Control Certificate is WHO and the Certificate of Class is not statutory at all is worth more than four more rows of the table.

Common mistakes

  • Presenting all five surveys as one cycle. Two of them are not in the cycle at all.
  • Saying the annual survey issues a certificate. It endorses one. Only the renewal survey issues a new certificate.
  • Putting the Certificate of Class in a list of statutory certificates. It is the society's own document under its own rules.
  • Forgetting the reporting duty in the damage survey. The surveyor is CALLED - the owner or master must report a casualty or defect. Candidates write as though he appears by himself.
  • Merging damage and repair surveys. One diagnoses, the other confirms the treatment; the repair survey is what clears the condition of class.
  • Saying a good repair clears the condition. It does not - the repair must be to an approved procedure and witnessed. That is what is being certified.
  • Omitting the harmonized system. The examiner named annual, intermediate and renewal in that order for a reason.
  • Listing certificates without their issuing authority or convention when the stem asks for all three columns.
  • Importing the 33rd Assembly resolution number for the survey guidelines. See the traps.

Examiner traps

  • The Survey Guidelines under the HSSC at this sitting are A.1156(32), not A.1186(33). A.1186(33) was adopted 6 December 2023, four months AFTER this paper, and it revoked A.1156(32). Material written for any later sitting carries the later number.
  • The SOLAS Consolidated Edition current at this sitting is 2020, not 2024. The 2024 edition applies from 1 July 2024.
  • Only the renewal survey issues a certificate. Expect 'the surveyor has done your annual - what did you get?' An endorsement.
  • The intermediate survey REPLACES the second or third annual; it is not an extra survey on top of it.
  • An annual survey missed outside its window invalidates the certificate. Expect to be asked what happens if you cannot reach a surveyor - which is what the short extension provision is for, and the replacement certificate still runs from the original expiry, so the extension buys no time.
  • The bottom must be examined twice in five years, with a maximum interval between the two.
  • The Minimum Safe Manning Document, the Continuous Synopsis Record and Exemption Certificates are NOT delegable to a Recognized Organization.
  • MLC 2006 is an ILO convention, not IMO - and only Part II of the DMLC may be certified by an RO, Part I being the flag State's statement of its own law.
  • The Ship Sanitation Control Certificate is a WHO instrument issued by a port health authority under the International Health Regulations 2005.
  • CLC, Bunkers and wreck-removal certificates are financial security certificates issued by a State on evidence of insurance - they are not survey certificates and an RO does not issue them.

Likely oral follow-up

  • Which of the five surveys you named is not on a calendar, and why not?
  • You have just completed your annual survey. What document did you get?
  • When does an intermediate survey take place, and what does it add to an annual?
  • The ship touched bottom leaving the berth and nothing appears wrong. What do you do?
  • What is the difference between the damage survey and the repair survey?
  • You made an excellent temporary repair at sea with your own people. Is your condition of class cleared?
  • How many times must the bottom be examined in a five-year period?
  • Your Safety Equipment Certificate expires next week and there is no surveyor available. What are your options?
  • Name three certificates that a Recognized Organization cannot issue for your flag.
  • Is the Maritime Labour Certificate issued under an IMO convention?
  • Is your Certificate of Class a statutory certificate?

Memory framework

First hold the split: PERIODICAL - annual, intermediate, renewal; OCCASIONAL - damage, repair. For each survey hold the same four questions: WHEN - PURPOSE - SCOPE - WHAT IT PRODUCES. Hold one line for what each produces: ENDORSE - ENDORSE - NEW CERTIFICATE - CONDITION OF CLASS - CLEARS THE CONDITION. For the harmonized system hold FIVE YEARS - ANNIVERSARY DATE - THREE-MONTH WINDOWS - COMMON VOCABULARY - SHORT EXTENSION. For the certificate register run the instruments in order: SOLAS - LOAD LINES - TONNAGE - MARPOL - AFS - BWM - LIABILITY, then the four that are NOT IMO: REGISTRY, MLC, SANITATION, CLASS.

Regulation and source map

  • SOLAS chapter I part B - surveys and certification: initial, annual, intermediate, periodical, renewal and additional surveys, and the duty to report a casualty or defect.
  • Harmonized System of Survey and Certification - introduced by the 1988 Protocols to SOLAS and to the Load Line Convention, in force 3 February 2000: five-year period, anniversary date, three-month windows, common survey vocabulary.
  • Resolution A.1156(32) - Survey Guidelines under the Harmonized System of Survey and Certification, 2021. The operative edition at this sitting.
  • SOLAS 1974 and 1988 Protocol - the Cargo Ship Safety Construction, Equipment and Radio Certificates, the combined Cargo Ship Safety Certificate and the Passenger Ship Safety Certificate.
  • Load Line Convention 1966 and 1988 Protocol - the International Load Line Certificate.
  • Tonnage Convention 1969 - the International Tonnage Certificate (1969).
  • MARPOL Annexes I, II, IV and VI - IOPP, NLS, ISPP, IAPP, EIAPP and IEE Certificates and the fuel oil consumption Statement of Compliance.
  • AFS 2001 and the BWM Convention 2004 - the anti-fouling and ballast water management certificates.
  • ISM Code (SOLAS IX) and ISPS Code (SOLAS XI-2) - the DOC, SMC and ISSC.
  • CLC 1992, Bunkers 2001, Nairobi WRC 2007 - financial security certificates issued by a State on evidence of insurance.
  • NOT IMO: Certificate of Registry (national law - in India the Merchant Shipping Act, 1958); MLC 2006 (ILO); Ship Sanitation Control Certificate (WHO, International Health Regulations 2005); Certificate of Class (the society's own rules).

Uncertainty and evidence limits

  • No certificate validity period is asserted beyond the five-year harmonized period, which is stated because the harmonization cannot be explained without it. The stem does not ask for validity.
  • We hold no copy of A.1156(32). It is cited by identity, adopting session and revocation date only; no paragraph of the Survey Guidelines is quoted.
  • we hold SOLAS at citation level and not as quotable text. Chapter I part B is cited by identity and subject; no regulation number below part level is asserted for the survey scheme.
  • No class rule number is cited for damage survey, repair survey, the recording of a condition of class or the approval of a repair procedure. All four are described by function.
  • The register is the general set for a conventional cargo ship, with ship-type-specific certificates marked. What a particular ship carries depends on type, size, trade and flag, and the answer says so.
  • Damage and repair surveys are answered from survey practice rather than from any earlier solved paper we hold, so they are the part of this answer resting on the least corroboration and carrying the most examination value.
Recall the structure

Before you read the answer: can you name all 8 sections, in order? Say them or write them down, then reveal.

  1. __________The distinction that organises the whole answer
  2. __________Annual survey
  3. __________Intermediate survey
  4. __________Renewal survey
  5. __________Damage survey
  6. __________Repair survey
  7. __________The harmonized system that ties the first three together
  8. __________The statutory certificates, their issuing authority and their convention
Flashcards 10

1 the periodical-versus-occasional distinction; 2 annual; 3 intermediate; 4 renewal; 5 damage; 6 repair; 7 the harmonized system that ties the first three together; 8 the certificate register with issuing authority and convention, plus the documents that are not issued under an IMO convention at all. Each survey is given as when, purpose, scope and what it produces.

Annual, intermediate and renewal are PERIODICAL: calendar-driven, anchored to the anniversary date, and what keep the certificates valid - they are what the harmonized system co-ordinates. Damage and repair are OCCASIONAL: event-driven, with no window, no anniversary and no place in any schedule. The stem names all five as though they were stages of one cycle, and two of them are not in the cycle at all.

Only the renewal survey. It is the only one that asks whether the ship is fit for the NEXT five years, and it produces a new certificate valid for a further five. Annual and intermediate surveys ENDORSE the existing certificate - the intermediate endorsement taking the place of the second or third annual. A damage survey produces a condition of class; a repair survey clears it.

To establish what is damaged, how badly, and what must be done before the ship proceeds - a diagnostic survey covering the damage and anything it may have affected, which reaches beyond the visible dent. The owner or master must report the casualty or defect: the surveyor is called, he does not appear by himself. It produces a report, a condition of class with a date, possibly a conditional voyage to a repair port, and the technical evidence for any insurance claim.

No. What the repair survey certifies is not only that the steel is sound but that the repair was carried out to an APPROVED PROCEDURE, with approved materials and qualified welders, and was WITNESSED. Procedure approval is required before work begins, with attendance at hold points, non-destructive examination and testing on completion. An unwitnessed repair, however good, clears nothing. Emergency repairs at sea are accepted as temporary and must be reported and surveyed at the first opportunity.

Before it, each convention ran its own survey cycle on its own dates with certificates of different lengths, so a ship met surveyors in different ports for different certificates. The 1988 Protocols to SOLAS and the Load Line Convention, in force 3 February 2000, gave a common five-year period, a single anniversary date, three-month windows either side of it, a common survey vocabulary, and a short extension to complete a voyage - with the replacement certificate still running from the original expiry.

Resolution A.1156(32) of 2021. NOT A.1186(33), which was adopted 6 December 2023 - four months after this sitting - and revoked them. The parallel edition trap is the SOLAS Consolidated Edition: the current one here is 2020, the 2024 edition applying from 1 July 2024. Material written for a later sitting carries both later references.

The Certificate of Registry, issued under national law - in India the Merchant Shipping Act, 1958. The Maritime Labour Certificate and DMLC, under MLC 2006, which is an ILO convention. The Ship Sanitation Control Certificate, issued by a port health authority under the International Health Regulations 2005, a WHO instrument. And the Certificate of Class, issued by the society under its own rules and not a statutory certificate at all.

Exemption Certificates, because exempting a ship is a sovereign act belonging to the Administration; the Continuous Synopsis Record, which is the flag's own record of the ship's history; and the Minimum Safe Manning Document, which is the Administration's determination of what manning the ship needs. Also not delegable in practice are DMLC Part I, which states the flag's own national requirements, and the CLC, Bunkers and wreck-removal financial security certificates, which are issued by a State on evidence of insurance rather than after a survey.

The harmonized system provides for a short extension to allow the ship to complete her voyage to a port where the survey can be carried out - but the replacement certificate is still dated from the ORIGINAL expiry, so the extension buys no additional validity. Separately, an annual survey not completed within its three-month window renders the certificate invalid, which is a different and harsher rule from expiry.

Quick revision
15-second recall
Three periodical, two occasional. Annual - yearly within three months of the anniversary, general and visual, ENDORSES. Intermediate - replaces the second or third annual, goes inside tanks, ENDORSES. Renewal - five-yearly, drydocking, thickness measurement, machinery opened, issues a NEW certificate. Damage - event-driven, diagnoses, raises a condition of class; the surveyor is called. Repair - confirms the fix was to an approved procedure and witnessed, and CLEARS the condition. Harmonized system: 1988 Protocols, in force 3 February 2000 - five years, anniversary date, three-month windows. Guidelines at this sitting: A.1156(32).

Critical regulation
The Harmonized System of Survey and Certification, introduced by the 1988 Protocols to SOLAS and the Load Line Convention and in force from 3 February 2000. If you remember one thing about it, remember what it fixed: before it, each convention ran its own survey cycle on its own dates, so a ship met surveyors in three different ports for three different certificates. One anniversary date, one five-year period and three-month windows are what let a ship be surveyed around her trading pattern instead of despite it.
Major trap
Citing A.1186(33) for the HSSC Survey Guidelines. It was adopted 6 December 2023, four months after this sitting, and it revoked A.1156(32), which is the operative edition here. The related edition trap is the SOLAS Consolidated Edition: the current one at this sitting is 2020, not 2024. Circulating material written for a 2024 or later sitting carries both later references.

Also on the platform: QP2308 Q5 - the Recognized Organization that carries out most of these surveys and issues most of these certificates, and the limits on its authority · QP2308 Q7 - how the conventions under which every one of these certificates is issued came into force · QP2308 Q4 - the condition monitoring record that can support a planned maintenance scheme in place of an opened-up survey

Once in this set Set once in the sittings MIW has transcribed.

Understand this first

A country can sign a treaty in the morning and change nothing at all. Signing is a promise; keeping it needs laws on the statute book, inspectors who exist and are trained, an office that answers letters, and courts that will actually fine somebody. For years the uncomfortable truth was that some States had ratified everything and implemented almost nothing, and there was no polite way of saying so. The III Code is the answer to that - not a new set of technical rules about ships, but a description of what a State has to be able to do before its ratifications mean anything. Notice the shape of it. A State meets shipping wearing three different hats and the Code takes them one at a time. As a flag, it is responsible for its own ships wherever in the world they are - certifying them, manning them, and going after them when they misbehave abroad. As a coastal State, its duties are mostly the opposite of policing: it has to provide things - charts, weather, radio, search and rescue, buoys, traffic services - because claiming authority over a stretch of sea comes with the job of making it safe to cross. And as a port State, it takes an independent second look at foreign ships that visit, which is the check on the first hat being worn carelessly by somebody else. The clever part, and the thing to carry away, is that each part ends by telling the State to measure itself. A flag State is told to watch how often its ships are detained in other people's ports - which is exactly the evidence that a foreign inspector, not itself, has produced. So the three roles are deliberately overlapped, the failures of one showing up in the records of another, and the whole thing is then audited from outside against this same Code.

  • Load lines; tonnage measurement; preventing collisions at sea

  • SOLAS 1974 with its 1978 and 1988 Protocols; MARPOL and its 1997 Protocol; STCW 1978 and the STCW Code; LL 1966 and LL PROT 1988; TONNAGE 1969; COLREG 1972
  • Do NOT lead with a count - neither instrument states one and both nine and ten circulate

  • Evaluate performance on a periodic basis, in respect of processes, procedures and resources
  • Port State control detention rates
  • Flag State inspection results
  • Casualty statistics
  • Communication and information processes
  • Annual loss statistics, EXCLUDING constructive total losses

  • Further review areas: detentions relative to fleet size, certificate-holder incompetence, responses to PSC deficiency reports, serious casualty investigations, pollution violations, suspensions and withdrawals

  • A safety and environmental protection programme, with an audit and inspection programme INDEPENDENT of any body issuing the certificates
  • Ships sufficiently and efficiently manned, on the Principles of Safe Manning
  • Delegation to a recognized organization under a formal written agreement, with specific instructions for ships found unfit to proceed
  • An oversight programme with supplementary surveys and competent supervising staff
  • Prohibit sailing until compliant; inspect periodically; penalties of adequate severity
  • Institute proceedings after investigation IRRESPECTIVE OF WHERE the violation occurred
  • Qualified, trained surveyors and investigators carrying identification documents
  • Impartial and objective casualty investigation, aimed at prevention, reported to the Organization
  • And the part closes on evaluation and review

  • Exercising a right conferred by an instrument incurs the additional obligations that go with it
  • The obligations are overwhelmingly SERVICES TO SHIPPING, not controls over ships
  • Radiocommunication services; meteorological services and warnings; search and rescue; hydrographic services
  • Ships' routeing systems; ship reporting systems; vessel traffic services; aids to navigation
  • A control and monitoring programme within its waters
  • Timely response to pollution incidents, and cooperation in casualty investigation
  • Periodic evaluation of its own performance

  • Adequate reception facilities for ship-generated waste
  • Port State control
  • A register of local fuel oil suppliers
  • No more favourable treatment for ships of non-parties
  • A PSC programme consistent with the Assembly's Procedures - at this sitting A.1155(32) - and with the regional MOU
  • PSCOs free of commercial pressure and NOT employed by recognized organizations or classification societies
  • Periodic evaluation of its own performance
  • The three roles overlap deliberately so a failure by one is caught by another - detentions abroad return as the flag's own first indicator
  • Above all three sits the Member State Audit Scheme, auditing the State against this Code
Model written answer

1. The Code, and how a recommendatory text came to bind

The IMO Instruments Implementation Code - the III Code - was adopted by the Assembly as resolution A.1070(28) on 4 December 2013, its operative paragraph revoking A.1054(27), the 2011 Code that had supported the voluntary audit scheme.

Begin with the point that most answers miss, because it explains the instrument rather than summarising it. The Code is written in 'should' from beginning to end. It was never redrafted into obligation language. What binds is not the Code's own wording but the fact that the conventions were amended to require its use - by A.1083(28), A.1084(28) and A.1085(28) for Load Lines, Tonnage and COLREG; by MSC.366(93), MSC.373(93), MSC.374(93) and MSC.375(93) for SOLAS, STCW, the STCW Code and the 1988 Load Line Protocol; and by MEPC.246(66) and MEPC.247(66) for MARPOL and its 1997 Protocol. It became mandatory from 1 January 2016, and the IMO Member State Audit Scheme became mandatory with it, using the Code as its audit standard.

Recognise the technique, because it recurs across this paper: a standard is adopted as a code and then made binding by amending the conventions that refer to it - exactly as the ISM, ISPS and RO Codes were.

The Code has four parts: common areas, then flag States, coastal States and port States - and each of the three State parts closes on evaluation and review of its own performance, which is the Code's characteristic move and the reason limb (b) exists.

2. The objectives of the Code

The objective is stated in the annex's first paragraph and it has two halves. The second half is routinely dropped and it is half the sentence:

  • to enhance global maritime safety and protection of the marine environment; and
  • to assist States in the implementation of instruments of the Organization.

The assisting half matters because it fixes the Code's character. It is not a new set of technical requirements. It is a statement of what a State has to be able to do in order to make instruments it has already ratified actually work - which is the gap between ratification and effect that Q7 of this paper is about.

Three further propositions from the common part complete the objective:

  • A State is bound only in respect of instruments to which it is a Contracting Government or Party. The Code creates no obligation under a treaty a State has not joined.
  • Which role dominates is decided by geography and circumstance. A large flag with little coastline is engaged mainly as a flag State; an archipelagic State with few ships is engaged mainly as a coastal State. The Code addresses all three roles because most States occupy all three.
  • The State is to promulgate laws and take all other steps necessary to give the instruments full and complete effect - and, in a provision worth quoting in substance, is not to transfer damage or hazards from one area to another, or transform one type of pollution into another.

3. The strategy recommended to the flag State

The Code's strategy paragraph has three limbs and they are a cycle, not a document:

  1. Develop an overall strategy to ensure that the State's international obligations are met, covering its flag, port and coastal roles together rather than separately.
  2. Establish a methodology to monitor and assess that the strategy is in fact delivering implementation and enforcement - so the strategy has to be measurable.
  3. Continuously review it in order to achieve, maintain and improve overall performance and capability.

Develop - monitor - review. That is the answer to 'the strategy recommended to flag state', and it should be given in those terms rather than as a list of good intentions.

The Code then says what the strategy must be built on. National legislation and administrative procedures must address the whole field the instruments cover, and the State must be able to communicate the strategy and the legislation to everybody concerned. Most usefully for an examination answer, the Code identifies three initial actions a State must take before anything else is possible:

  • the ability to promulgate laws giving the instruments effect;
  • a legal basis for enforcement and verification - the power to inspect, detain, penalise and prosecute; and
  • sufficient personnel with maritime expertise to do the work.

That third item is why the Code exists at all: a State that has ratified a convention and has no surveyors has implemented nothing.

4. Which IMO instruments the Code covers

The printed question - 'Which all IMO instruments covered in the code' - is ambiguous, and there are two correct answers. Give both and say why they differ; that is what a careful candidate does.

First answer - the six subject areas. The Code's own scope paragraph names six areas and names no convention at all:

  • safety of life at sea;
  • prevention of pollution from ships;
  • standards of training, certification and watchkeeping;
  • load lines;
  • tonnage measurement of ships; and
  • preventing collisions at sea.

Note carefully what is not there: maritime security is not one of the six, although the preamble refers to security. That is a favourite examination point.

Second answer - the treaty instruments under which the Code is actually mandatory. These are the conventions that were amended to require it, and the Assembly maintains a non-exhaustive list of obligations under instruments relevant to the Code. At this sitting that list is resolution A.1157(32). The instruments are SOLAS 1974 with its 1978 and 1988 Protocols; MARPOL and its 1997 Protocol; STCW 1978 and the STCW Code; the Load Line Convention 1966 and its 1988 Protocol; the Tonnage Convention 1969; and COLREG 1972.

Do not lead with a count. Neither the Code nor the list states a number, and both nine and ten circulate depending on how the Protocols are counted. List the instruments.

5. Key performance indicators for flag State performance

This is limb (b), and it is short, factual and heavily marked. The Code requires the flag State to evaluate its own performance on a periodic basis, in respect of its processes, procedures and resources, and it offers indicators for doing so.

  • Port State control detention rates.
  • Flag State inspection results.
  • Casualty statistics.
  • Communication and information processes.
  • Annual loss statistics - excluding constructive total losses.

Two propositions about these indicators separate a good answer from a list.

  • Their stated purpose is diagnostic. They exist so the State can determine whether its staffing, resources and procedures are adequate to meet its obligations - not to produce a league table.
  • They are for the State's own self-evaluation, not a return to the Organization. The Code is not a reporting regime; the external check is the Member State Audit Scheme, which is a different mechanism.

The Code then lists further areas for regular review, and naming several of them shows depth: detentions in relation to the size of the fleet; incompetence or misconduct of individuals holding certificates the State has issued; the State's responses to port State control deficiency reports; serious casualty investigations and the lessons taken from them; pollution violations; and the suspension or withdrawal of certificates and of delegations.

Notice the shape of the list: it is designed to catch a flag State that issues certificates freely and does nothing when the ships carrying them are detained abroad.

6. Flag State responsibilities

Limb (c) has three parts and the flag State's is the largest. Give it under six heads.

  • Implementation. Establish and maintain a safety and environmental protection programme, with minimum elements including policy and instructions, resources, an audit and inspection programme independent of any body issuing the certificates, and defined responsibilities. That independence requirement is the same principle as the RO Code's and is worth naming.
  • Manning. Ensure ships are sufficiently and efficiently manned, applying the Organization's Principles of Safe Manning, and issue the manning document.
  • Delegation. Where survey and certification are delegated to a recognized organization, do so under a formal written agreement, give specific instructions on what is to happen when a ship is found unfit to proceed to sea, and maintain an oversight programme with supplementary surveys and competent supervising staff. This limb connects directly to Q5 of this paper.
  • Enforcement. Prohibit a ship from sailing until she complies; inspect periodically; provide penalties of adequate severity to discourage violation; and institute proceedings after investigation irrespective of where the violation occurred - which is the provision that makes flag State jurisdiction real rather than territorial.
  • Surveyors. Ensure surveyors and investigators are qualified, trained and carry identification documents, whether they are the State's own staff or an RO's.
  • Casualty investigation. Investigate impartially and objectively, with a view to prevention rather than blame, and report to the Organization.

And the part closes, as every State part does, on evaluation and review - which is limb (b) above.

7. Coastal State responsibilities

This is the limb candidates most often leave empty, and the reason is that they look for controls over ships and find none. The coastal State's obligations under the Code are overwhelmingly SERVICES TO SHIPPING, not controls over it. Say that first and the limb writes itself.

The Code opens the part with a general proposition that applies to coastal and port States alike: exercising a right conferred by an instrument incurs the additional obligations that go with it. A State that claims jurisdiction over its waters accepts the duty to service them.

The service areas are:

  • radiocommunication services;
  • meteorological services and warnings;
  • search and rescue services;
  • hydrographic services - surveying and charting;
  • ships' routeing systems;
  • ship reporting systems;
  • vessel traffic services; and
  • aids to navigation.

Beyond the services, the coastal State should operate a control and monitoring programme within its waters, be able to respond in a timely way to pollution incidents, and co-operate in casualty investigation where a casualty occurs in its waters or involves its interests. And it too must periodically evaluate its own performance.

8. Port State responsibilities, and how the three roles interlock

The port State part is short and has three obligations at its centre:

  • provide adequate reception facilities for ship-generated waste - the obligation whose absence undermines MARPOL more than any other single failure;
  • operate port State control; and
  • maintain a register of local fuel oil suppliers, which is the enforcement handle for fuel quality.

Three further propositions carry the marks:

  • No more favourable treatment. A ship flying the flag of a State that is not party to an instrument is not to receive more favourable treatment than a ship of a Party. Without this the whole convention system could be evaded by re-flagging.
  • The port State control programme should be consistent with the Assembly's Procedures for Port State Control - at this sitting, resolution A.1155(32) - and in practice with the regional memorandum of understanding to which the State belongs.
  • Port State control officers must be free of commercial pressure, and must not be employed by, or undertake work on behalf of, recognized organizations or classification societies. The reason is exact: a PSCO checking a certificate issued by his own employer is checking his employer's work, and the whole value of port State control is that it is an independent second look at what the flag State and its RO have certified.

And the port State part, like the others, ends on periodic evaluation of its own performance.

Close by joining the three roles, because that is what the limb is really asking and it converts a list into an understanding. The flag State certifies its own ships wherever they are; the coastal State provides the services and monitors the waters it claims; the port State takes an independent second look at ships that come to it. The three overlap deliberately, so that a failure by one is caught by another - a flag that certifies too easily is exposed by detentions abroad, and those detention rates come back to it as the first of its own performance indicators. The Code's real subject is not any of the three roles but the closing of the loop between them - and above all three sits the Member State Audit Scheme, under which the State is itself audited against this Code.

Study guide

Why this structure scores

The stem prints three lettered limbs, and the later sittings of this identical question print (6), (4) and (6) against them - a split that is not printed here but is a fair guide to the examiner's intent. The structure follows it: sections 2 to 4 answer limb (a), section 5 answers limb (b), and sections 6 to 8 answer limb (c), with section 1 supplying the framing that makes all three intelligible.

Section 1 is not preamble. The single most valuable observation about the III Code is that it is written in 'should' throughout and binds only because the conventions were amended to require it. A candidate who says that has explained an instrument; one who does not has summarised a document. It also sets up the technique - code adopted, then made mandatory through the conventions - which appears twice more on this paper, at Q5 and Q7.

Section 7 is where the marks are quietly available. Almost every candidate writes a long flag State section and a reasonable port State section, and then produces two vague lines on coastal States. The reason is that they are looking for controls over ships. Once you see that the coastal State's obligations are SERVICES - radio, weather, search and rescue, charts, routeing, reporting, VTS, lights - the limb becomes a list you can actually write.

Common mistakes

  • Giving only the first half of the objective. It is to enhance safety and environmental protection AND to assist States in implementation. The second half is what the Code is actually for.
  • Answering 'which instruments' with only one of the two possible answers. The six subject areas and the treaty instruments are different answers to an ambiguous question - give both.
  • Including maritime security in the six subject areas. It is not one of them.
  • Leading with a count of instruments. Neither the Code nor the Assembly's list states a number, and both nine and ten circulate.
  • Writing the coastal State limb as controls over shipping. It is services TO shipping.
  • Treating the performance indicators as a report to IMO. They are the State's own self-evaluation; the external check is the Audit Scheme.
  • Forgetting that annual loss statistics EXCLUDE constructive total losses.
  • Omitting the port State's reception facility obligation, which is the one whose absence does most damage in practice.
  • Citing the 33rd Assembly resolution numbers. See the traps - this is the single largest risk on this question.

Examiner traps

  • The list of obligations at this sitting is A.1157(32), NOT A.1187(33). A.1187(33) was adopted 6 December 2023 - four months after this paper - and revoked it.
  • The Procedures for Port State Control at this sitting are A.1155(32), NOT A.1185(33). Same date, same boundary, same trap. Circulating material written for any later sitting carries both later numbers.
  • The Code itself is A.1070(28) of 4 December 2013 and is unaffected. It was the LISTS that were replaced, not the Code - and confusing the two is the error the reversal invites.
  • The Code reads in 'should'. Expect 'if it only says should, how is it mandatory?' Because the conventions were amended to require its use, from 1 January 2016.
  • Maritime security is not one of the six subject areas.
  • The flag State's audit and inspection programme must be independent of any body issuing the certificates. Same principle as the RO Code's independence requirement.
  • Port State control officers must not be employed by, or work for, recognized organizations or classification societies. A PSCO checking his own employer's certificate is not an independent second look.
  • Proceedings are instituted irrespective of where the violation occurred. That is what makes flag State jurisdiction real.
  • Constructive total losses are excluded from the annual loss statistic. A small point that is asked because it shows whether the candidate has read the indicator or recited it.

Likely oral follow-up

  • The III Code says 'should' everywhere. So is it mandatory, and how?
  • What are the two halves of the Code's objective?
  • Name the six subject areas. Is security one of them?
  • How many instruments is the Code mandatory under?
  • Give me the flag State performance indicators. Who are they for?
  • Your flag has a high detention rate abroad. What does the Code expect it to do about that?
  • What does a coastal State actually have to provide?
  • Why must a port State control officer not work for a classification society?
  • What is no more favourable treatment, and what would happen without it?
  • Who audits the flag State, and against what standard?

Memory framework

The route is HOW IT BINDS - OBJECTIVE - STRATEGY - INSTRUMENTS - INDICATORS - FLAG - COASTAL - PORT, which maps onto framing, limb (a) in three parts, limb (b), and limb (c) in three parts. For the strategy hold DEVELOP - MONITOR - REVIEW. For the six areas hold SAFETY - POLLUTION - TRAINING - LOAD LINES - TONNAGE - COLLISIONS. For the indicators hold DETENTIONS - INSPECTIONS - CASUALTIES - COMMUNICATION - LOSSES. For the coastal State hold RADIO - WEATHER - SAR - CHARTS - ROUTEING - REPORTING - VTS - LIGHTS. For the port State hold RECEPTION - CONTROL - FUEL REGISTER. And hold the closing idea: the Code's subject is the closing of the loop between the three roles.

Regulation and source map

  • Resolution A.1070(28) - the IMO Instruments Implementation Code, adopted 4 December 2013; revoked A.1054(27). Mandatory from 1 January 2016.
  • A.1083(28), A.1084(28), A.1085(28) - Load Lines, Tonnage and COLREG amendments making the Code mandatory.
  • MSC.366(93), MSC.373(93), MSC.374(93), MSC.375(93) - SOLAS, STCW, the STCW Code and the 1988 Load Line Protocol.
  • MEPC.246(66), MEPC.247(66) - MARPOL and its 1997 Protocol.
  • A.1070(28) annex paragraph 1 - the two-part objective. Paragraph 3 - develop, monitor, review. Paragraph 6 - the six subject areas. Paragraphs 7 to 9 - legislation, initial actions, communication.
  • Paragraphs 15 to 41 - the flag State part. Paragraphs 42 to 44 - evaluation and the performance indicators. Paragraphs 45 to 51 - the coastal State part. Paragraphs 52 to 63 - the port State part.
  • Resolution A.1157(32) - the non-exhaustive list of obligations under instruments relevant to the Code. OPERATIVE AT THIS SITTING.
  • Resolution A.1155(32) - Procedures for Port State Control, 2021. OPERATIVE AT THIS SITTING.
  • IMO Member State Audit Scheme - mandatory from 1 January 2016, using the III Code as its audit standard.

Uncertainty and currency

  • No count of the instruments the Code is mandatory under is asserted. Neither the Code nor the Assembly's list states a number; nine and ten both circulate depending on how the Protocols are counted. The answer lists the instruments instead.
  • The text of A.1070(28) was not re-read for this paper. Its substance is carried from our own verified records for three later sittings of this identical question, which read the resolution in full. The paragraph-level content here is reused verification, not fresh verification.
  • We hold no copy of A.1157(32) or A.1155(32). Both are cited by identity, adopting session and revocation date only. No content of the 2021 Procedures for Port State Control is asserted beyond their identity as the operative edition.
  • The Member State Audit Scheme's own Framework and Procedures was not read. The claim that it uses the III Code as its audit standard rests on the mandatory-application chain. No claim is made about audit periodicity or procedural rules.
  • The paper that prints this identical stem has not yet completed our own review. QP2302-Q2 was read to confirm the printed text and for nothing else; all substance comes from three integrated sittings of the same stem.
Recall the structure

Before you read the answer: can you name all 8 sections, in order? Say them or write them down, then reveal.

  1. __________The Code, and how a recommendatory text came to bind
  2. __________The objectives of the Code
  3. __________The strategy recommended to the flag State
  4. __________Which IMO instruments the Code covers
  5. __________Key performance indicators for flag State performance
  6. __________Flag State responsibilities
  7. __________Coastal State responsibilities
  8. __________Port State responsibilities, and how the three roles interlock
Flashcards 10

1 how a text written in 'should' came to bind; 2 the objective; 3 the strategy; 4 which instruments are covered - both answers; 5 the performance indicators; 6 flag State responsibilities; 7 coastal State responsibilities; 8 port State responsibilities and how the three interlock. Sections 2-4 are limb (a), section 5 is limb (b), sections 6-8 are limb (c).

Because the conventions were amended to require its use. The Code, resolution A.1070(28) of 4 December 2013, was never redrafted into obligation language; instead A.1083(28), A.1084(28) and A.1085(28) amended Load Lines, Tonnage and COLREG, MSC.366(93) and MSC.373-375(93) amended SOLAS, STCW, the STCW Code and the 1988 Load Line Protocol, and MEPC.246(66) and MEPC.247(66) amended MARPOL and its 1997 Protocol. Mandatory from 1 January 2016.

To enhance global maritime safety and protection of the marine environment, AND to assist States in the implementation of instruments of the Organization. The second half is routinely dropped and it is what fixes the Code's character: it is not a new set of technical requirements about ships but a statement of what a State must be able to do to make instruments it has already ratified actually work.

Safety of life at sea; prevention of pollution from ships; standards of training, certification and watchkeeping; load lines; tonnage measurement of ships; and preventing collisions at sea. MARITIME SECURITY IS NOT ONE OF THEM, although the preamble refers to security. Note that this is one of two correct answers to 'which instruments' - the other is the list of treaty instruments under which the Code is mandatory.

Port State control detention rates; flag State inspection results; casualty statistics; communication and information processes; and annual loss statistics excluding constructive total losses. Their purpose is diagnostic - to let the State determine whether its staffing, resources and procedures are adequate - and they are for the State's own periodic self-evaluation, not a return to IMO. The external check is the Member State Audit Scheme.

Provide services, not controls. Radiocommunication services; meteorological services and warnings; search and rescue; hydrographic services; ships' routeing systems; ship reporting systems; vessel traffic services; and aids to navigation. Beyond those, a control and monitoring programme in its waters, timely response to pollution incidents, cooperation in casualty investigation, and periodic self-evaluation. Candidates leave this limb empty because they look for controls over ships and find none.

Adequate reception facilities for ship-generated waste; port State control; and a register of local fuel oil suppliers. Plus no more favourable treatment for ships of non-parties, and a PSC programme consistent with the Assembly's Procedures and the regional MOU. Port State control officers must be free of commercial pressure and must not be employed by, or work for, recognized organizations or classification societies - a PSCO checking his own employer's certificate is not an independent second look.

A.1157(32) for the non-exhaustive list of obligations, and A.1155(32) for the Procedures for Port State Control, 2021. NOT A.1187(33) and A.1185(33), both adopted 6 December 2023 - four months after this paper - each revoking its predecessor. Note that the Code itself, A.1070(28), is NOT reversed: it was the lists that were replaced, not the Code.

The flag State certifies its own ships wherever they are; the coastal State provides services and monitors the waters it claims; the port State takes an independent second look at visiting ships. They overlap deliberately, so a failure by one is caught by another - a flag that certifies too easily is exposed by detentions abroad, and those detention rates come back as the first of its own performance indicators. Above all three sits the Member State Audit Scheme.

Do not give a number. Neither A.1070(28) nor the Assembly's list of obligations states one. Nine circulates because it counts SOLAS 1974, SOLAS PROT 1988, MARPOL, MARPOL PROT 1997, STCW 1978, LL 1966, LL PROT 1988, TONNAGE 1969 and COLREG 1972; ten circulates because the list also carries a SOLAS PROT 1978 column. List the instruments instead of counting them.

Quick revision
15-second recall
A.1070(28), 4 December 2013, mandatory 1 January 2016 - and it binds only because the conventions were amended to require it, since the Code itself says 'should'. Objective has two halves: enhance safety and environmental protection, AND assist States to implement. Strategy is develop - monitor - review. Six areas: safety, pollution, training, load lines, tonnage, collisions - security is NOT one. Indicators: detentions, inspections, casualties, communication, losses (excluding CTLs). Flag certifies, coastal SERVICES, port takes the second look. At this sitting: A.1157(32) and A.1155(32), not the 33rd Assembly numbers.

Critical regulation
Resolution A.1070(28) - the III Code itself. If you remember one thing, remember why a document that says 'should' on every page is nonetheless mandatory: the conventions were amended to require its use, from 1 January 2016, by a chain of Assembly, MSC and MEPC resolutions. The same technique made the ISM, ISPS and RO Codes binding, and recognising it is worth a mark in the oral.
Major trap
Citing A.1187(33) for the list of obligations or A.1185(33) for the Procedures for Port State Control. Both were adopted 6 December 2023 - four months after this sitting - and each revoked its 32nd Assembly predecessor. The operative instruments here are A.1157(32) and A.1155(32). All three integrated sittings of this identical question sit after that boundary and correctly carry the later numbers, so every piece of circulating material is wrong for this paper. Note that the Code itself, A.1070(28), is NOT reversed - it was the lists that were replaced, not the Code.

Also on the platform: QP2308 Q5 - the Recognized Organization the flag State delegates to, and the oversight programme the Code requires over it · QP2308 Q8 - the surveys and certificates through which the flag State's implementation actually happens · QP2308 Q7 - how the instruments the Code covers came into force, and why implementation lags ratification

Repeated — same wording Returns in the same words from February 2023 Q2. Also set at January 2024 Q9, March 2024 Q7, December 2024 Q9, October 2025 Q7.

No question matches that search. Try a topic, a regulation or a question number — for example general average, MARPOL Annex VI or Q5.
Across all solved papers

Nothing in this paper — these are from other sittings.

August 2023 rapid revision

Every question in one pass. Generated from each question's Quick Revision block — open the full answer from the question number.

QTopicAnswer routeCritical rule / numberMajor trap
Q1 Internet of Things in the Maritime Industry
16 marks		 What the Internet of Things is → The principles - the five layers → Advantages of loT in the maritime industry → Disadvantages, and the ones that are specific to a ship → How loT is changing the maritime industry → loT in energy efficiency optimization → loT in predictive Maintenance → What the Chief Engineer has to do about it MARPOL Annex VI as revised by MEPC.328(76), in force 1 November 2022, carrying EEXI and the operational CII from 1 January 2023. If you remember one thing, remember that the CII is an operational index computed over a whole year - which is precisely why continuous measurement, rather than an annual estimate, is what it takes to manage it.
<		 Citing EU ETS or FuelEU Maritime as the regulatory driver. EU ETS applies to shipping from 1 January 2024 and FuelEU was adopted 13 September 2023 - four months and one month AFTER this sitting. The April 2024 sitting of this identical question rests on both, so any circulating answer to it is wrong for this paper.
Q2 Maritime Lien, In Rem and In Personam
16 marks		 What a maritime lien is → How the lien travels with the property → In rem - the action against the ship → In personam - the action against the person → Which convention deals with maritime lien → The five secured claims, and how they rank among themselves → The order of settlement after legal action → The Indian position, stated separately Admiralty (Jurisdiction and Settlement of Maritime Claims) Act, 2017, section 9(1) - the five liens in their statutory order, wages first. If you remember one thing, remember that this is the provision an Indian court actually applies, because India is not a party to the 1993 Convention even though the Act reproduces its scheme.
<		 Putting salvage first in the priority order. It is fourth in the list as printed and third in the ranking - wages, then loss of life or personal injury, then salvage. The inverted order has been published in study material. What IS true about salvage is separate: a salvage lien jumps ahead of liens that attached before the salvage, and salvages rank among themselves in inverse order of time.
Q3 Rudder Efficiency Improvement Devices
16 marks		 Where the recoverable energy is → (a) Rudder Thrust Fins → (b) Asymmetric Rudders → (c) Rudder Bulbs → (d) Grim Vane Wheels → What the four have in common, and how they combine → Selection and retrofit in practice → Judging whether a claimed gain is real MARPOL Annex VI chapter 4 as revised by MEPC.328(76) - EEXI and the operational CII, applicable from 1 January 2023. If you remember one thing about the regulation here, remember that it is the commercial reason these devices are being fitted at all: a permanent efficiency gain improves the attained EEXI and helps the annual rating, which is worth more than the fuel bill alone.
<		 Citing the Initial IMO Strategy of 2018 as the current IMO strategy. It was superseded on 7 July 2023 by the 2023 IMO GHG Strategy, resolution MEPC.377(80) - one month before this sitting. Note the direction: the January 2023 sitting of this identical question cites the 2018 Strategy CORRECTLY for its own date, so the trap here is a stale citation from an EARLIER paper, not an imported one from a later paper.
Q4 UMS Data, a Condition Monitoring Chart, and Trend Analysis
16 marks		 The position you are in, and what the question is asking → What a UMS ship gives you, and what it takes away → The specific data that deserves special attention → Telling a trend from noise → Formulating the condition monitoring chart → The computer tools for graphical representation → Trend analysis proper → What you do with what the trend tells you SOLAS chapter II-1 part E - periodically unattended machinery spaces. If you remember one regulatory point, remember that it is part E which permits the space to be unmanned, by requiring the detection, alarm and safeguard arrangements that substitute for the watchkeeper - and that ISM Code element 10 is a separate obligation, about maintaining the ship and identifying the equipment whose sudden failure would be hazardous.
<		 Treating an alarm as the monitoring system working. It is the opposite: by the time a reading crosses its setpoint the deterioration is complete and the only decision left is how to stop safely. The related traps are moving the baseline to accommodate a drifting plant, and raising a setpoint to silence a nuisance alarm - both hide deterioration rather than manage it, and both are management-of-change failures.
Q5 Limitations of Recognized Organizations, and the Statutory Services Delegated
16 marks		 The boundary the question turns on → Limitation one - the function is delegated, the responsibility is not → Limitation two - the authority is specific, never general → Limitation three - the RO cannot make, waive or dispense with the law → Limitation four - independence, and the conflict the scheme is built around → Limitation five - the RO has no enforcement power → Limitation six - practical and jurisdictional limits → The statutory services undertaken on behalf of the Administration RO Code Part 2 section 8 - the conventional bases for delegation and the requirement that the authorization SPECIFY the functions conferred. If you remember one thing, remember the word specify: it is what makes every other limitation enforceable, because a scope that was written down function by function can be narrowed, suspended or withdrawn function by function.
<		 Citing A.1187(33) for the obligations list or A.1188(33) for the ISM implementation guidelines. Both were adopted 6 December 2023, four months after this sitting, and each revoked its 32nd or 30th Assembly predecessor. The operative instruments here are A.1157(32) and A.1118(30). Almost all circulating material is written for a later sitting and carries the wrong numbers.
Q6 Power Transformation to Effective Power, and Sea Trial Manoeuvres
16 marks		 The power chain, as one idea → Indicated power - what the engine develops in the cylinder → Brake power, and mechanical efficiency → Shaft power and delivered power - the transmission → Thrust power and effective power - the propeller and the hull → The efficiencies that join the stages → Sea trials - what a navigation trial is for, and the conditions it must be run in → The essential manoeuvres, and the objective of each IMO Standards for Ship Manoeuvrability, resolution MSC.137(76). If you remember one thing about it, remember that its criteria are expressed in ship lengths and not in metres - which is what allows a coaster and a very large crude carrier to be judged against the same standard, and why a large ship's alarming absolute stopping distance can still comply comfortably.
<		 Stopping the power chain at delivered power, or confusing ship speed with speed of advance. Thrust power is thrust times the speed of ADVANCE, which is lower than ship speed because the propeller works in the hull's wake. The related trap is treating wake and thrust deduction as the same thing: wake is the propeller seeing slower water, thrust deduction is the propeller making the hull harder to push, and you need both to reach hull efficiency.
Q7 The Four Stages of an IMO Convention, and the Entry-into-Force Delay
16 marks		 What an IMO convention is, and the four stages → Stage one - proposal and development → Stage two - adoption → Stage three - acceptance, and the four ways of giving it → Stage four - entry into force → Why the delay happens → The delay, in worked examples → What can be done to reduce the delay MARPOL article 16 - tacit acceptance. If you remember one thing in limb three, remember what it changed: under explicit acceptance an amendment waited for positive acceptances and took as long as a convention, so technical requirements could not be kept current. Under tacit acceptance the amendment is deemed accepted unless enough Parties object. The burden is reversed and silence now means yes - and that is why SOLAS and MARPOL amendments arrive on a predictable few-year cycle while new conventions take a decade.
<		 Saying the Hong Kong Convention is in force, or that it 'took sixteen years'. At this sitting it is not in force: adopted 15 May 2009, its conditions were satisfied only in June 2023, two months before this paper, and it enters into force on 26 June 2025. The circulating solved answers are all written for a 2025 or 2026 sitting and treats it as completed. Here it is the live example - and it is a better illustration of the stem for exactly that reason.
Q8 Survey Types Differentiated, and the Statutory Certificate Register
16 marks		 The distinction that organises the whole answer → Annual survey → Intermediate survey → Renewal survey → Damage survey → Repair survey → The harmonized system that ties the first three together → The statutory certificates, their issuing authority and their convention The Harmonized System of Survey and Certification, introduced by the 1988 Protocols to SOLAS and the Load Line Convention and in force from 3 February 2000. If you remember one thing about it, remember what it fixed: before it, each convention ran its own survey cycle on its own dates, so a ship met surveyors in three different ports for three different certificates. One anniversary date, one five-year period and three-month windows are what let a ship be surveyed around her trading pattern instead of despite it.
<		 Citing A.1186(33) for the HSSC Survey Guidelines. It was adopted 6 December 2023, four months after this sitting, and it revoked A.1156(32), which is the operative edition here. The related edition trap is the SOLAS Consolidated Edition: the current one at this sitting is 2020, not 2024. Circulating material written for a 2024 or later sitting carries both later references.
Q9 IMO Instruments Implementation Code - Objectives, Flag State Strategy, KPIs and the Three State Roles
16 marks		 The Code, and how a recommendatory text came to bind → The objectives of the Code → The strategy recommended to the flag State → Which IMO instruments the Code covers → Key performance indicators for flag State performance → Flag State responsibilities → Coastal State responsibilities → Port State responsibilities, and how the three roles interlock Resolution A.1070(28) - the III Code itself. If you remember one thing, remember why a document that says 'should' on every page is nonetheless mandatory: the conventions were amended to require its use, from 1 January 2016, by a chain of Assembly, MSC and MEPC resolutions. The same technique made the ISM, ISPS and RO Codes binding, and recognising it is worth a mark in the oral.
<		 Citing A.1187(33) for the list of obligations or A.1185(33) for the Procedures for Port State Control. Both were adopted 6 December 2023 - four months after this sitting - and each revoked its 32nd Assembly predecessor. The operative instruments here are A.1157(32) and A.1155(32). All three integrated sittings of this identical question sit after that boundary and correctly carry the later numbers, so every piece of circulating material is wrong for this paper. Note that the Code itself, A.1070(28), is NOT reversed - it was the lists that were replaced, not the Code.
